**D-004** | 2026-09-16 | Reconnaissance found `backend/app/routes_opportunities.py` (`fetch_accessible_opportunity`, `fetch_owned_opportunity`, lines 86-132) re-implements role resolution inline instead of calling `permissions.py`'s `require_opportunity_access` / `require_opportunity_owner`, even though behavior currently matches (`test_opportunities.py:134` passes either way). Brief §4.5 explicitly asks for "one central permission-check function used by every service method touching an opportunity." | (a) leave the duplication since behavior is currently correct, (b) consolidate onto `permissions.py` as a Phase F milestone | (b) | This is exactly the gap §4.5 names, not a new judgment call — implementing it is in-scope for Phase F regardless. Logged here so the "why" survives into the milestone rather than being rediscovered. | Y | N — directly specified by the brief; no architecture change, just deduplication |

**D-005** | 2026-09-16 | `docs/code_review.md` finding 4a identified that editors could edit (not just fail to be blocked from deleting) any colleague's timeline entry or note, and recommended the reviewer "pick one and apply it consistently." Only the delete side was tightened (`require_delete_permission`, author-or-owner) — edit was left permissive. `AGENTS.md`'s permissions model states editors "add and edit freely" and is silent on restricting edits of others' content, while explicitly reserving deletion to the author or owner. Current code (`routes_timeline.py:340-383` for entries, `:513-577` for notes) matches this reading. | (a) tighten edit to author-or-owner to match delete, (b) keep edit permissive, matching the literal `AGENTS.md` wording and existing passing tests | (b) — keep current behavior; treat this as a deliberate, now-explicit reading of an ambiguous spec rather than an unresolved defect | `AGENTS.md` positively states editors edit freely; it does not say "except other people's items." Changing it would contradict `test_editor_can_write_and_viewer_is_read_only` (`test_timeline.py:225`) and would be scope the brief didn't ask for (brief §9: "no unnecessary defensive branches," and changing tested behavior needs a justified reason, not just discomfort with an old review comment). | Y | **RESOLVED 2026-09-16 — OVERRULED by the human.** Choice (a) is adopted: **editors may edit only their own content.** Edit permission now mirrors delete exactly — author or opportunity owner. `AGENTS.md`'s Permissions Model has been rewritten accordingly and, being above this brief in the §1 hierarchy, is the binding statement: "an editor who may not remove a colleague's record may not rewrite it either." This closes `docs/code_review.md` finding 4a the way it asked ("pick one and apply it consistently"). Implement as Phase F milestone **F.5a**; `test_editor_can_write_and_viewer_is_read_only` (`test_timeline.py:225`) is expected to change, and that change is authorised here rather than being a weakened assertion under brief §6.5. |

**D-006** | 2026-09-16 | How to structure Phase F's own milestone list, since §4.1 items 1-6 (inventory, baseline, roadmap conversion, scope cross-check, gap analysis, stale-reference fix) are tightly coupled and none alone produces an independently gate-able artifact. | (a) one milestone per numbered §4.1 bullet, (b) bundle all of §4.1 as milestone F.1 ("Reconnaissance"), with gap-analysis findings becoming their own later Phase F milestones per §4.1.5's instruction | (b) | §4.1.5 already says gap-analysis items "each become a Phase F milestone" — implying §4.1's other five items are the input to that split, not milestones in themselves. Splitting inventory from baseline-running from roadmap-conversion would produce milestones with no independent acceptance criterion. | Y | N — a session-planning choice, not a product or architecture decision |

**D-007** | 2026-09-16 | D-004's permission-check consolidation (`routes_opportunities.py` `PATCH`/`DELETE` now call `permissions.require_opportunity_owner` instead of an inline owner-only query) changes behavior for one untested edge case: a shared editor or viewer (who has read access but isn't the owner) now gets **403** on `PATCH`/`DELETE /api/opportunities/{id}`, where the old inline query returned **404** for any non-owner regardless of whether they had read access. No existing test exercised this case — `test_non_owner_cannot_view_or_mutate_opportunity` only covers a user with *no* share at all, where both old and new code agree on 404. | (a) preserve the old 404-for-any-non-owner behavior with an explicit branch, (b) accept the new 403-for-shared-non-owner behavior since it now matches `routes_sharing.py`'s owner-only endpoints, which already 403 a shared editor | (b) | `routes_sharing.py`'s `require_opportunity_owner` already returns 403, not 404, when a shared non-owner calls a share-management endpoint — the old inline query in `routes_opportunities.py` was the *inconsistent* one. 403 ("you can see this, but can't do that") is the correct status for a user with read access attempting a write action; 404 is reserved for "you have no access at all" per this codebase's own convention (CLAUDE.md's Permissions section). Preserving the old behavior via (a) would mean writing a second, deliberately-wrong permission check specifically to avoid a status-code change no test depended on — the opposite of what D-004 asked for. | Y | N — a bugfix that falls directly out of D-004's already-approved consolidation, not a new architectural choice. Added `test_shared_editor_can_view_but_not_mutate_opportunity` (`test_opportunities.py`) to lock the new behavior in, confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code per brief §6.5. |

**D-008** | 2026-09-16 | F.2 asks to verify `SESSION_SECRET` (`settings.py`, `.env.example`) is "either wired up or removed" (`docs/code_review.md` finding 10: it's declared but read nowhere; sessions use random `token_urlsafe` IDs, which need no signing key). ADR 0009 (Phase 5, inbound email) says HMAC-signed reply addresses will "finally give `settings.session_secret`... a real purpose, **or better, a dedicated `INBOUND_EMAIL_SECRET`**" — so the field has a *future* consumer, just not today's. | (a) leave it declared now so Phase 5 can reuse the name, (b) remove it now; Phase 5 introduces whatever secret its actual design needs | (b) | ADR 0009 itself prefers a dedicated `INBOUND_EMAIL_SECRET` over reusing `session_secret` — so keeping the field around doesn't even save Phase 5 a rename. Brief §9 forbids "unnecessary defensive branches," and AGENTS.md's coding standards say the same about dead code; a config value nothing reads is dead code. Removed from `settings.py`, `.env.example`, and `README.md`; added `environment` (`development`/`production`/`test`, fail-fast validated) in its place, which F.2's error-envelope work actually needs today (to decide whether to show exception detail to clients). | Y | N — removing genuinely dead configuration per an explicit brief instruction and the review finding's own recommendation; Phase 5 is free to name its own secret when ADR 0009 lands. |

**D-009** | 2026-09-16 | Standing up `mypy --strict` (F.2's tooling milestone) found 81 errors when run against `app/` + `tests/` together; the app-only subset was 28, all fixable with type annotations. Fixing all 81 (including annotating every test helper and fixture) is a much larger, lower-value undertaking than fixing the 28 in application code, since test helpers' types are usually inferrable from usage at call sites and mypy's main value is catching bugs in code that ships. | (a) run strict mypy over `app/` and `tests/` both, fix everything, (b) scope strict mypy to `app/` only for now, leave `tests/` unchecked | (b) | `[tool.mypy] files = ["app"]` in `backend/pyproject.toml` already scoped this way before the errors were even counted (a deliberate default, not a retroactive excuse) — mirrors how the brief separates "strict mypy" (§3.4, an application-code quality gate) from test-suite concerns. Revisit if a later phase wants typed test fixtures for a specific reason. | Y | N — a proportionality call for one milestone's tooling setup, not a permanent policy; reversible by deleting the `files` key. |

**D-010** | 2026-09-16 | F.3 flagged `AuditLogEntryPublic.detail` (previously `str` — the raw JSON blob was never even parsed before being wrapped in another JSON string on the wire) and `NotificationPublic.payload` (`dict[str, Any]`) as opaque. There are exactly 7 audit actions and 6 notification types, each writing a fixed, enumerable set of keys (`routes_sharing.py`, `routes_timeline.py`, `routes_opportunities.py`, `notification_service.py`). Two ways to type this: (a) give `detail`/`payload` a field-level type union (one Pydantic model per action/type, `Union[...]`, resolved by a lookup keyed on the sibling `action`/`type` field at construction time), or (b) restructure `AuditLogEntryPublic`/`NotificationPublic` themselves into whole-object discriminated unions (`Annotated[Union[...], Field(discriminator=...)]`), so the generated OpenAPI schema statically links `type` to `payload`'s shape and TypeScript can narrow one from the other. | (a) field-level union, resolved in Python before constructing the response model, or (b) whole-object discriminated union, doubling the number of top-level response schemas (13 new wrapper classes on top of the 13 payload/detail variant classes) | (a) | Both close the actual gap the milestone named — `detail`/`payload` are no longer `str`/`dict[str, Any]`, and the generated `frontend/lib/api-types.ts` now emits a real `oneOf`-style union for each. (b) is more precise (lets the frontend narrow `payload`'s type from `type` in one step) but doubles the schema surface for a benefit the frontend code doesn't need: the only two read sites (`notificationText`, the audit-log `<code>` block) either don't need narrowing at all (`JSON.stringify`) or narrow via a plain `"title" in payload` check, which TypeScript already supports on a flat union. Brief §9's "no speculative abstraction" and AGENTS.md's coding standards both weigh against building the more general shape for a consumer that doesn't exist yet. Revisit if a later phase's frontend work actually needs `type`-to-`payload` narrowing at the type level. | Y | N — a proportionality call on how far to take a typing improvement the brief already specified; not a scope or architecture change. |

**D-011** | 2026-09-16 | F.3's client-generation step (`scripts/generate-api-client.sh`, backed by `openapi-typescript`) needs a way to dump the FastAPI app's OpenAPI schema without starting a server. | (a) add a `/openapi.json`-scraping step that boots uvicorn and curls it, (b) a small `app/export_openapi.py` module that imports `app.main.app` and prints `app.openapi()` — no server, no network | (b) | `app.openapi()` is a plain method call against already-registered route metadata; booting a real server just to fetch a schema it can produce in-process adds a process lifecycle (start, wait-for-ready, kill) to what should be a sub-100ms build step, and risks flakiness in CI. Kept out of `main.py` itself so importing it never runs `configure_logging()`/`initialize_database()` (`main.py`'s `lifespan`). | Y | N — a build-tooling implementation choice with no behavioral or API surface effect. |

**D-012** | 2026-09-16 | F.4's third sub-task asks for an ADR on "SQLite fitness for v2 given real-time features and a single container" and is flagged **needs human review: Y** (brief §4.4). `PROGRESS.md`'s own F.4 text already suspected the ADR might exist: `docs/adr/0001-keep-sqlite.md` is Status Accepted (v2) and reaches exactly this conclusion. | (a) write a new ADR as the milestone literally asks, (b) drop the sub-task and cite the existing ADRs as satisfying it | (b) — the sub-task is removed from F.4 and replaced by a citation. | ADR 0001 is not a stub: it decides SQLite over Postgres at the confirmed 800k-row / ~136 MB scale using seeded benchmarks (timeline 1.4 ms vs a 400 ms budget, dashboard 131 ms vs 300 ms, worst-case search 294 ms vs 300 ms), and carries revisit triggers plus a costed migration path. It also already prescribes F.4's second sub-task (WAL + busy timeout at connection setup in `database.py`), so that bullet implements an accepted ADR rather than deciding anything. The "single container" half is ADR 0011's subject, and the "real-time features" half is ADR 0003's: 0003 states target concurrency of 2-3 users on an opportunity with last-write-wins, which sits inside 0001's measured 2-3-concurrent-writer envelope — so a third ADR would restate two accepted ones and produce no new measurement. Writing it anyway would also violate `AGENTS.md` coding standard 2 (no speculative work). F.4's first sub-task is likewise already decided by ADR 0002 (Alembic, Accepted, with a three-step adoption plan against the existing auto-created schema). | Y — if a later phase changes the real-time design away from ADR 0003's assumptions, revisit and write the ADR then. | **RESOLVED 2026-09-16 — ACCEPTED by the human.** The sub-task is dropped from F.4; ADRs 0001, 0002, 0003 and 0011 stand as the answer. Recorded here because the dropped item carried the brief's needs-human-review flag, so its removal needed the ruling rather than an agent's judgement.

**D-013** | 2026-09-16 | ADR 0002's adoption plan says step 3 "stamp the baseline revision rather than running it" for the existing `data/app.db`, and step 4 "convert the two existing migrations in `run_migrations()` into revisions." Those two steps conflict: a database stamped at the baseline is already past any revision numbered after it, so a revision carrying the `timeline_entries.system_generated` and `opportunity_shares.deleted_at` `ALTER TABLE`s would never run on the legacy database that needs them — and would be a no-op on a new database, whose baseline already includes both columns. | (a) stamp at a synthetic pre-baseline revision so the two converted revisions replay on a legacy database, (b) keep the repair as code that runs once, immediately before the stamp, and let the baseline be the only revision | (b) — `database.bridge_pre_alembic_database()` runs the two guarded `ALTER TABLE`s, `normalize_legacy_timestamps()` and `backfill_opportunity_created_entries()` exactly once against a database that has tables but no `alembic_version`, then `command.stamp(BASELINE_REVISION)` records it. | (a) would mean inventing a revision describing a schema no installation was ever stamped at, purely so the migration graph could tell a story about how the real one got there; the baseline *is* the current schema, which is what ADR 0002 step 2 asks for. The repair is also not purely schema — `normalize_legacy_timestamps` and the backfill are data repairs whose only trigger is "this database was written by the pre-canonical MVP code," which is precisely the condition the bridge tests for. ADR 0002's stated goal for step 4 is that these stop running on every boot (its Consequences: "Startup stops doing repeated full-table scans"); the bridge achieves that literally, and `test_legacy_repair_runs_once_rather_than_on_every_startup` (`tests/test_migrations.py`) proves it. Every future schema change is a normal revision — this applies only to the one-time crossing of the Alembic boundary. | Y | N — an implementation detail of an already-accepted ADR, resolving an internal inconsistency in its own step list; no schema or behavior differs between the two options for any database that exists. |

**D-014** | 2026-09-16 | D-013's bridge is a one-shot, so nothing rewrites a non-canonical timestamp after it has run. A database bridged from the MVP keeps its original `DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` column defaults (SQLite's `ALTER TABLE` cannot rewrite a default, and rebuilding ten tables to change one default each is a far larger change than this milestone), which write `"YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS"` — the exact mixed format CLAUDE.md's Timestamps section says silently reorders the timeline and drops rows from date filters. | (a) leave the `INSERT`s relying on column defaults and accept that a bridged database writes non-canonical timestamps forever, (b) rebuild every table in a revision to change its defaults, (c) have application code supply every timestamp explicitly with `utc_now()`, so the column default is never the thing that decides the format | (c) — every `INSERT` in `routes_auth.py`, `routes_sharing.py`, `routes_timeline.py`, `routes_opportunities.py`, `auth.py`, `audit_utils.py`, `notification_service.py` and `timeline_utils.py` now passes `created_at`/`updated_at` explicitly. Defaults stay in the baseline as a backstop. | (a) is a live data-corruption path the moment the repair stops running every boot, which is the whole point of D-013. (b) rebuilds ten tables to fix something the application can simply stop depending on. (c) makes the canonical format a property of the code rather than of whichever schema a given installation happens to have, which is the invariant CLAUDE.md actually states ("every timestamp the app writes uses one canonical format"). `test_writes_to_a_bridged_legacy_database_are_canonical` (`tests/test_migrations.py`) drives the real API against a bridged MVP database and asserts all 14 written timestamp columns match the canonical regex; it fails against the pre-fix code. | Y | N — closing a correctness regression that this milestone's own change would otherwise introduce, using the format rule the codebase already documents. |

**D-015** | 2026-09-16 | ADR 0002 step 6 says "Delete `schema.sql` and `column_exists()` once the baseline is verified." `schema.sql` is gone (the baseline revision carries it verbatim), but `column_exists()` is what D-013's bridge uses to decide whether a legacy database is missing `system_generated` / `deleted_at`. | (a) delete `column_exists()` as the ADR says and have the bridge inspect `PRAGMA table_info` inline, (b) keep `column_exists()` as the bridge's helper | (b) | The ADR's step 6 targets `column_exists()` as the *migration mechanism* — guarded `ALTER TABLE`s re-evaluated on every boot. That mechanism is gone. What remains is one helper called from one one-shot function, plus `table_exists()` alongside it; deleting it would mean inlining the same `PRAGMA table_info` loop at its only two call sites. `tests/test_migrations.py` also uses it to assert the bridge did its job. Logged rather than done silently because the ADR names the function explicitly. | Y | N — keeping a three-line helper the ADR's rationale no longer applies to; the mechanism the ADR objected to is removed. |

**D-016** | 2026-09-16 | `test_schema_init_backfills_created_system_entries` (`tests/test_opportunities.py`) built a legacy row with raw SQL, called `initialize_database()` twice, and asserted exactly one backfilled "Opportunity created" entry. Its raw `INSERT`s omit the columns D-014 now supplies explicitly and it depends on the backfill running on every `initialize_database()`, which D-013 deliberately ends. | (a) patch it in place, (b) delete it outright, (c) move the behavior it guards into `tests/test_migrations.py`, where a real MVP-era database is the fixture | (c) — deleted from `test_opportunities.py`; `test_mvp_backfill_creates_one_system_entry_per_opportunity` (`tests/test_migrations.py`) asserts the same property (one system entry per opportunity, still only one after a second `initialize_database()`) against `tests/data/mvp_schema.sql` instead of a hand-built row. | The assertion is not weakened under brief §6.5 — the same guarantee is asserted, against a *more* realistic starting state (a real pre-Alembic schema, not a partial row inserted into today's schema). (a) would keep a test whose premise — "the backfill is something startup does" — is no longer true, so it would pass while documenting the wrong model. Noted here because §6.5 requires a removed test to be justified rather than quietly dropped. | Y | N — relocating an assertion to the file that now owns its subject, with no loss of coverage. |

## Scope cross-check summary (brief §4.1.4)

Walked every item in brief §7 against `docs/ROADMAP.md` Phases 1-6:

| §7 capability | Roadmap coverage | Gap? |
|---|---|---|
| System-generated timeline entries | Shipped (v1) | No |
| Entry type tags with color/icon | Color shipped (v1); icons for 3 new types in Phase 2 | No (existing 8 types keep color-only; acceptable minor scope reading, not logged as a gap) |
| Note edit history | Shipped (v1, `note_versions`) | No |
| Audit log | Shipped (v1) | No |
| @mentions and in-app notifications | Shipped (v1) + reworked inbox in Phase 1 | No |
| Stalled flags and follow-up reminders | Stalled flag shipped (v1); Phase 6's automation acceptance criterion ("no-activity-for-14-days rule fires... creates a task... logs a system entry") is the reminder mechanism | No — confirmed this maps to journey §6.3 #9, not a separate unbuilt feature |
| CSV/JSON export (PDF retained) | Shipped (v1) | No |
| Rich text | Phase 3 (TipTap) | No |
| Custom fields | Phase 6 | No |
| Table/board/calendar views | Phase 2 (board, list guaranteed; table/calendar may slip to Phase 3 per the roadmap's own stated risk) | No — already an acknowledged, not hidden, risk |
| Saved and shareable filtered views | Phase 2 (`saved_views`) | No |
| Dashboards | Phase 6 | No |
| Real-time collaboration | Phase 3 (SSE, ADR 0003) | No |
| Opportunity-scoped automations | Phase 6 | No |
| Keyboard-first navigation and command palette | Phase 2 | No |
| Design system with light/dark themes | Tokens land in Phase 2; no explicit dark-theme acceptance criterion anywhere | **Yes — D-003** |
| WCAG 2.1 AA | Phase 2 + Phase R axe checks, version conflict with `AGENTS.md` | **Yes — D-002 (resolved by hierarchy, not a scope gap)** |

One proposal follows from this table (D-003); everything else is already covered
by name in the roadmap or resolved by the source-of-truth hierarchy.

## Human rulings — 2026-09-16

Recorded outside a phase gate because Phase F is `IN_PROGRESS`; brief §5 treats
decisions "recorded in DECISIONS.md or review feedback" as phase scope, so these
are binding on the remaining Phase F milestones.

| ID | Agent's choice | Human ruling | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| D-001 | Fix the OpenRouter reference | Upheld | Already done in F.1 |
| D-002 | WCAG 2.2 AA per `AGENTS.md` | Upheld | Binding for Phases 2 and R |
| D-003 | Proposal only, recommend Phase 2 | **Accepted** | Now a Phase 2 acceptance criterion |
| D-004 | Consolidate onto `permissions.py` | Upheld | Phase F milestone F.5 |
| D-005 | Keep edit permissive | **Overruled** | Editors edit own content only; F.5a |
| D-006 | Bundle §4.1 as one milestone | Upheld | No change |
| D-017 | Double-submit cookie for CSRF | Pending | F.5 as built |
| D-018 | Add `POST /api/auth/password` | **Pending — needs review** | New endpoint in a no-new-features phase |
| D-019 | CSP allows inline script/style | Pending | Tightening is a Phase R item |
| D-020 | In-process per-address rate limiter | Pending | Single-container assumption |
| D-021 | Build `uploads.py`/`sanitize.py` ahead of their features | Pending | Brief §4.7 |
| D-022 | Test client echoes the CSRF cookie | Pending | Suite runs the real middleware |
| D-023 | Defer `prettier --check` to F.8 | Pending | Gate gap, tracked |

Two operational rulings, for the record:

- **Sandbox egress stays minimal.** Claude Code's telemetry to
  `http-intake.logs.us5.datadoghq.com` and its updater's reach for
  `downloads.claude.ai` are both refused by `sandbox/proxy/allowlist.conf` and
  both are non-fatal. Leave them refused: the first is third-party telemetry, and
  the second reinforces the pinned `CLAUDE_CODE_VERSION`. Do not widen the
  allowlist to silence the retries.
- **The quality gate is split.** `make check` cannot run "Docker build + smoke
  test" inside the sandbox. Run every other item; the human runs
  `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh <ID>` on the host, and
  `scripts/autopilot-accept.sh` refuses a phase without its receipt. See brief
  §3.4 and `sandbox/README.md`.

**D-017** | 2026-09-17 | F.5 has to add CSRF protection to a cookie session. Two standard shapes: a server-side synchronizer token (per-session secret stored in the `sessions` table, echoed in a header) or a double-submit cookie (random token in a readable cookie, echoed in a header, compared to itself). | (a) synchronizer token stored per session, (b) double-submit cookie in `security.py` | (b) — `CsrfMiddleware`, cookie `opptrail_csrf`, header `X-CSRF-Token`, `secrets.compare_digest`. | Double-submit needs no schema change and no database read on the hot path, and its security argument is the same one that already protects the session: same-origin policy stops a cross-site page reading the cookie back, so it cannot populate the header. The app is single-origin (FastAPI serves the SPA and the API), so there is no subdomain whose cookie-setting ability would undercut it. A synchronizer token would add a column, a migration, and a read per mutating request to defend against the same attacker. | Y — swapping to a session-stored token later is a `security.py` change plus one revision. | N — implements a control the brief names, with the cheaper of two standard mechanisms. |

**D-018** | 2026-09-17 | Brief §4.5 requires "session ... invalidation on ... password change", but no password-change endpoint exists, and brief §4 opens with "no new user-facing features in this phase." `PROGRESS.md`'s F.5 text records the choice as open: build the endpoint, or scope it to a later phase. | (a) add `POST /api/auth/password` now (backend only, no UI), (b) defer the endpoint and leave the §4.5 bullet unmet until a later phase | (a) — added, with every session of that user deleted and a fresh one issued to the caller. | Deferring would close Phase F with a named §4.5 security requirement unimplemented and nothing to test session invalidation against, which is worse than the alternative it avoids. The endpoint ships no UI and no frontend call site, so it adds no user-facing feature in the sense §4 means; what it adds is the ability for a compromised account to be recovered. Verified by `test_password_change_invalidates_every_other_session` (`tests/test_auth.py`). | Y — the route can be removed. | **Y — a new API endpoint in a phase whose brief text says no new features.** Recommended: keep it (the security requirement is explicit and the alternative leaves a hole). Alternative: remove the route and move both it and the §4.5 bullet to the phase that adds account settings UI. **RESOLVED 2026-09-17 — ACCEPTED by the human: keep the endpoint.** The §4.5 security requirement is explicit and outranks §4's no-new-features line for this case; a backend-only route with no UI and no frontend call site adds no user-facing feature in the sense §4 means. `POST /api/auth/password` stays as shipped in `76bcfa2`, and the F.5 checkbox stands. Adding the account-settings UI that calls it remains a later phase's work, not a debt against Phase F. |

**D-019** | 2026-09-17 | The Content-Security-Policy required by §4.5 has to accommodate `next.config.ts`'s `output: "export"`, which emits Next's hydration payload as inline `<script>` blocks in `frontend/out/index.html` (verified by reading the built file). A static file has no per-request render pass, so there is no nonce to attach. | (a) `script-src 'self'` and accept that the app does not boot, (b) `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'`, (c) hash every inline block at build time and regenerate the header when the build changes | (b), with `style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'` for the same reason. | (a) is not an option — it breaks the product. (c) is correct and is the right Phase R task: it needs a build step that extracts the hashes and a gate check that the header matches the current build, which is release-engineering work, not Phase F foundations. (b) still delivers the controls the brief lists — `default-src 'self'`, `object-src 'none'`, `frame-ancestors 'none'`, `base-uri 'self'`, `form-action 'self'` — and blocks the injected-remote-script and clickjacking classes; what it does not block is inline injection, which is exactly what the frontend's `rehype-sanitize` and now `sanitize.py` exist for. Named as a known limitation in the review packet. | Y | N — forced by the accepted stack, and the stricter version is a Phase R item, not a decision to make now. |

**D-020** | 2026-09-17 | Rate limiting login and signup needs a counter store and a caller key. | (a) in-process dict keyed by source address, (b) a `login_attempts` table, (c) an external store | (a) — `rate_limit.py`, sliding window, injected clock, counting login and signup separately. | The app is one process in one container (ADR 0011), so process-local state is not a correctness compromise, and the docstring names it as the one place to change if that stops being true. A table would put a write on every login attempt — including the flood the limiter exists to shed. The key is `request.client.host` with no `X-Forwarded-For` parsing: nothing in the deployment terminates in front of the app today, and trusting a client-settable header would make the limiter trivially bypassable. If a reverse proxy is added, the header must be trusted explicitly and only from the proxy. | Y | N — a mechanism choice inside a control the brief specifies. |

**D-021** | 2026-09-17 | §4.5 asks for upload hardening and a server-side HTML sanitization framework. Neither feature exists: attachments are Phase 4, rich text is Phase 3. `AGENTS.md`'s coding standards forbid speculative abstraction. | (a) build `uploads.py` and `sanitize.py` now with tests, no call sites, (b) defer both to the phases that use them | (a) | Brief §4.7 states the intent directly — Phase F stands up the infrastructure so Phases 1-6 "only add tests rather than build infrastructure" — and §4.5 lists both by name, so this is specified work rather than anticipated work. Both modules are small, fully tested, and documented with the argument for each rule, which is the part that is expensive to reconstruct under feature pressure. `sanitize.py` also has a concrete second consumer already named by the roadmap (Phase 5 email bodies), not just the Phase 3 editor. | Y — either module can be deleted if its phase takes another route. | N — explicitly requested by §4.5 and §4.7. |

**D-022** | 2026-09-17 | Enforcing CSRF on every unsafe method breaks every existing test, which posts without the header. | (a) exempt the test environment from CSRF, (b) give each test the header by hand, (c) have the shared test client echo the cookie the way the browser does | (c) — `conftest.CsrfClient`; `tests/test_security.py` drives a plain `TestClient` to prove the middleware still rejects. | (a) would mean the suite never exercises the middleware it ships — the protection would be untested in exactly the configuration that runs it. (b) is the same change written 60 times. (c) keeps every existing test running against the production middleware stack, which is what made two real gaps visible immediately (`tests/test_migrations.py` built its own client, and the client had to bootstrap the cookie from a safe request first). | Y | N — test-harness plumbing. |

**D-023** | 2026-09-17 | Brief §3.4 lists `prettier --check` in the gate; `make check` does not run it. Running it now reports `frontend/app/page.tsx` plus 105 other files, because there is no `.prettierignore` and `npx prettier .` walks `out/` and the build artifacts. `page.tsx` was already non-conforming before this milestone (confirmed against the pre-change file). | (a) add `.prettierignore` + `prettier --check` to the gate in this milestone, reformatting `page.tsx` (231 insertions / 96 deletions) alongside the security diff, (b) record the gap and close it as an F.8 item | (b) — added to F.8 in `PROGRESS.md`. | The gate is not weakened by this choice — nothing that ran before stops running — but it is not yet as strong as §3.4 asks, and saying so plainly is the point of logging it. Mixing a 300-line whole-file reformat into the commit that changes permission checks and adds CSRF would make both harder to review, which is the cost §3.5's 20-30 minute review budget is there to protect. F.8 is the milestone that already owns leftover tooling gaps. | Y | N — sequencing, not a standard being lowered; the item is tracked, not dropped. |

**D-024** | 2026-09-17 | F.6 requires "a single OpenAI client wrapper with timeouts, retries, and graceful failure" (brief §4.6). `ai_service.ask_openai` (`backend/app/ai_service.py:124-137`) constructed `OpenAI(api_key=api_key)` with no timeout and no retry configuration — a hung call currently hangs the request indefinitely, and the SDK's default `max_retries=2` was implicit rather than chosen. | (a) hand-roll a retry loop with `time.sleep`-based backoff around `client.responses.create`, (b) pass `timeout=`/`max_retries=` to the `OpenAI()` constructor and let the SDK's own retry/backoff handle it, exposing both as validated settings | (b) — `openai_timeout_seconds` (default 20.0) and `openai_max_retries` (default 2) added to `settings.py` with fail-fast validators (`> 0`, `>= 0`), threaded through `ask_openai`'s signature and `routes_ai.py`'s call site. | The OpenAI SDK already implements exponential-backoff retry and per-request timeout at the transport layer (`httpx` under the hood) — a hand-rolled loop would duplicate that behavior with a hand-written `time.sleep`, which brief §6.5 disfavors ("no blind retries... to reach green" — the SDK's retry is deliberate and bounded, not a test-green hack) and which the AI agent module's already-stubbed test double (`ask_openai` is always monkeypatched in tests per brief §4.6) has no way to observe timing-wise anyway. Making both values named settings (rather than constants) lets a deployment tune them without a code change, consistent with `settings.py`'s existing pattern for the rate-limit and upload-size knobs. Graceful failure — a 502 with no stack trace — was already correct in `routes_ai.py`'s `except Exception` handler and needed no change. | Y — both values are settings, changeable without touching `ai_service.py`. | N — a mechanism choice inside a control the brief already specifies, not a new architectural direction. |

## Human note — 2026-09-16 (F.2 review)

`make check-fast` assumes `frontend/node_modules` is already installed and fails
with `sh: 1: tsc: not found` when it is not. The sandbox gives the container its
own `node_modules` volume (so a container `npm ci` cannot overwrite the host's
native `@next/swc` and `sharp` builds), and that volume starts empty, so a clean
sandbox cannot run the gate at all.

**Action for a later F milestone (F.3 or the Phase R CI work, your choice):**
make the frontend targets install dependencies when they are missing — an
`npm ci` guarded on `node_modules` being absent, or a `deps` prerequisite — so
`make check-fast` and `make check` are runnable from a clean checkout. The Phase R
CI workflow will hit exactly this on its first run otherwise.

Not treated as an F.2 defect: the milestone's own gate genuinely passed, and this
was only exposed by a sandbox fix made after that run.

**D-025** | 2026-09-17 | Brief §6.3's critical journey 12 is "AI agent answers on accessible data; refuses and leaks nothing on inaccessible data." The first half cannot be driven end to end from Playwright without a real OpenAI call, which brief §9 forbids ("Call OpenAI or any paid external API"). The sandbox's egress proxy refuses `api.openai.com` outright, so the choice is not whether to call it but what the E2E asserts instead. | (a) add an environment-gated fake-model path to the application so E2E can get a canned answer, (b) run the E2E server with no API key and assert the UI's failure handling, keeping "answers correctly" in the backend suite against a mocked client, (c) skip journey 12 entirely | (b) | (a) puts test-only branching into shipped code, which brief §9 and `AGENTS.md`'s "no unnecessary defensive branches" both rule out — and an application that behaves differently under a test flag is no longer the application under test. (c) drops a journey that is mostly still checkable. Under (b) `scripts/e2e-server.sh` exports an empty `OPENAI_API_KEY`, so the absence of a paid call is structural rather than promised, and `frontend/e2e/06-ai-agent.spec.ts` asserts the two things that do not need a model: the panel surfaces the service failure instead of swallowing it, and a user with no access to the opportunity gets no data back (the refusal body is checked for four distinct strings from the seeded opportunity). The half that needs a model — that the answer is grounded in the timeline, that hostile note content stays inert, that another opportunity's data never enters the context — is already covered in `backend/tests/test_ai.py` against a mocked client, and the exact 404-not-403 status is pinned in `backend/tests/test_authorization_matrix.py`, which can mock the model and so gets past the no-key 503. | Y — if a later phase adds a supported `OPENAI_BASE_URL` setting for self-hosting, a local stub server would make the answering half E2E-able without test-only code. | **Y** — it narrows a journey the brief lists by name. Recorded so the gate reviewer can accept the narrowing or ask for a different approach. **RESOLVED 2026-09-17 — ACCEPTED by the human: the narrowing stands.** Brief §9 forbids the paid call outright, so the only alternatives were test-only branching in shipped code (rejected — an application that behaves differently under a test flag is no longer the application under test) or dropping the journey. The half a model is actually needed for — that the answer is grounded in the timeline, that hostile note content stays inert, that another opportunity's data never enters the context — is asserted in `backend/tests/test_ai.py` against a mocked client, so the coverage exists; what changes is which layer proves it. `scripts/e2e-server.sh` exporting an empty `OPENAI_API_KEY` keeps the absence of a paid call structural rather than promised, which is the property worth having. |

**D-026** | 2026-09-17 | Standing up `@axe-core/playwright` (brief §3.4, §6.1) found real WCAG failures on every screen: two `critical` ones (`label`, `select-name` — the timeline filter bar, the two share-role selects, several inline composers and textareas had no accessible name) and a `serious` `color-contrast` failure on `.eyebrow`, `.primary-button`, `.danger-button` and `.empty-state`, across roughly forty nodes. Phase F ships no user-facing features (brief §4), and "axe clean" is milestone R.2's acceptance criterion, but brief §9 also forbids marking a milestone done with a red gate. | (a) fix everything now, including the palette, (b) assert only that no *new* rule fails, with `color-contrast` on a documented deferred list, (c) leave the axe check out of the gate until R.2 | (b), plus fixing the two critical rules outright | The critical failures are `aria-label` attributes: no design decision, no visual change, and leaving a form control unnamed is a defect rather than unfinished styling, so they are fixed here (`frontend/app/page.tsx`). `color-contrast` is a palette change — `DESIGN-SYSTEM.md`'s tokens and "the three corrected contrast values" are milestone 2.5's subject, and D-002 already settled that the target is WCAG 2.2 AA. Repainting the app inside a test-infrastructure milestone would both exceed Phase F's scope and pre-empt the token work. (c) would mean the axe check exists but guards nothing. The deferred list is a ratchet, not an allowlist: `DEFERRED_AXE_RULES` in `frontend/e2e/helpers.ts` holds exactly one rule, any other violation fails immediately, and `07-accessibility.spec.ts`'s last test asserts the deferred rule *still fails* — so once 2.5 fixes the palette the suite goes red until the entry is deleted and the gate tightens. One further real fix landed the same way: `.stage-board` scrolls horizontally and was not focusable, so a keyboard-only user could not reach the stages past the fold (`scrollable-region-focusable`); that is a `tabIndex={0}`, not a repaint. | Y — deleting the one entry is the whole reversal. | **Y** — it is a documented, time-boxed exception inside a gate item the brief lists. The reviewer should confirm that deferring `color-contrast` to 2.5/R.2 is the intended sequencing rather than something Phase F should have absorbed. **RESOLVED 2026-09-17 — ACCEPTED by the human: the sequencing is intended.** The two `critical` rules were unnamed form controls — defects with no design content — and were correctly fixed here. `color-contrast` is a palette decision owned by the milestone that introduces the design tokens; repainting the app inside a test-infrastructure milestone would both exceed Phase F's no-new-features scope and pre-empt that token work, which `DESIGN-SYSTEM.md` warns is expensive to retrofit. The ratchet is what makes the deferral safe rather than an open allowlist: `DEFERRED_AXE_RULES` holds exactly one rule and any other violation fails immediately. Phase 2 owns closing it, under D-003's acceptance criterion that light and dark both pass axe contrast checks. |

**D-027** | 2026-09-17 | Brief §2.1 requires the AI agent's data access to be unable to mutate state "by construction", and §6.4 asks for an invariant test that "write through its data layer fails". F.6 delivered the tool-surface half (`test_invariant_ai_agent_has_no_write_tools`), but the route opened an ordinary read-write connection and relied on `ai_service.py` only containing SELECTs — which is convention, not construction, and there was no test that could fail. | (a) assert by source scan that `ai_service.py` contains no write SQL, (b) open the agent's connection with a `mode=ro` SQLite URI, (c) set `PRAGMA query_only = ON` on the agent's connection | (c) — `database.read_only_connection()`, used by `routes_ai.ask_ai_agent` | (a) is the same convention with a regex around it: a future caller on the AI path could still write. (b) is genuinely enforced by SQLite but breaks under WAL — a `mode=ro` connection cannot create the `-shm` file WAL needs when no writer happens to be connected, which would turn "ask the agent a question" into an intermittent `SQLITE_CANTOPEN`. (c) is enforced inside SQLite exactly like (b) — an INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE raises `sqlite3.OperationalError` regardless of what the calling code, the prompt, or the model's answer says — with no WAL interaction. `test_invariants.py::test_invariant_ai_agent_data_layer_refuses_writes` exercises all three statement kinds and was confirmed to fail with the pragma removed; a second test asserts the route is actually wired to that connection rather than merely able to use it. | Y | N — it implements an invariant the brief states and a test the brief asks for; it removes a capability from one code path rather than adding behaviour, and no user-visible response changes. |

**D-028** | 2026-09-17 | The frontend had no unit tests and `frontend/app/page.tsx` was a single 1,783-line client component, so almost nothing in it could be reached except by rendering the whole app. Brief §6.2 sets a ≥80% line floor for the frontend; milestone 2.1 is where the component is decomposed into routed views. | (a) test only through whole-app rendering and accept whatever coverage results, (b) extract the pure, already-self-contained helpers into `frontend/lib/` modules now and test those directly alongside component tests, (c) start the 2.1 decomposition early | (b) | (a) leaves the timestamp conversions, the CSRF/fetch wrapper, the permission mirror and the Markdown sanitizer — the four pieces where a bug is silent and expensive — reachable only through a dozen clicks each. (c) is another phase's milestone and would mean designing the routing model inside a test milestone. (b) moved six units out of the component with no behaviour change (`lib/time.ts`, `lib/api.ts`, `lib/permissions.ts`, `lib/notifications.ts`, `app/MarkdownText.tsx`), which is the layering F.2 §4.2 already asks for, and left `page.tsx` 115 lines shorter and importing them. The whole Playwright suite was re-run against the refactor unchanged to show it moved nothing. Component tests then cover `page.tsx` itself through a stateful fake API. Result: 86.8% line, 79.3% branch across `app/` + `lib/`, against thresholds pinned at 85/85/85/78. | Y | N — a refactor with no behavioural change, in the direction the plan already commits to for 2.1. |

**D-029** | 2026-09-17 | `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh`'s smoke test — the host half of the split gate, and the thing `scripts/autopilot-accept.sh` requires a receipt from — signs up with a bare `curl -X POST /api/auth/signup` and no CSRF header. F.5 made every unsafe method require the double-submit pair, signup included (`backend/app/security.py`), so that call now returns 403 and the gate would fail at its second step. This was found while extracting the smoke test into its own script, not by running it (no Docker socket here). | (a) note it in BLOCKERS.md for the human to fix on the host, (b) fix it as part of defining the smoke test | (b) — `scripts/docker-smoke.sh` now bootstraps the CSRF cookie from the health check, echoes it on every unsafe call, and re-reads it after signup because `create_session` rotates it | The gate script is repository code and the milestone is "Docker smoke test defined"; shipping a definition that is known to fail is worse than not shipping one. The HTTP sequence was verified against the real application here — `scripts/e2e-server.sh` on a local port, every smoke step in order: health, CSRF cookie issued, signup, opportunity create, auto-generated timeline entry, PDF export (11,030 bytes, `%PDF` verified), frontend served — and the old call was confirmed to return 403 against the same server. Only the `docker build` / `docker run` wrapper around those steps is unverified here, which is the split gate working as designed. | Y | N — a bug fix in test tooling, proven against the running application rather than assumed. |

**D-030** | 2026-09-17 | F.8's last item asks whether the split gate (brief §3.4) should grow a host-native test run. `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh` runs only `docker build` plus `scripts/docker-smoke.sh`, and the sandbox half of `make check`/`check-fast` runs the backend and frontend suites only under the sandbox's own Linux container. Neither half ever runs `pytest` (or `npm test`) on the human reviewer's own machine, which is why F.8's WeasyPrint finding — `test_authorization_matrix.py`'s PDF-export cases failing with `OSError: cannot load library 'libgobject-2.0-0'` on macOS — reached the human directly instead of failing a gate. The same shape of gap will catch the next platform-specific regression (any test whose behavior depends on native libraries, filesystem case-sensitivity, or a tool version that differs between the sandbox's Linux and the reviewer's host). | (a) add a host-native `pytest`/`npm test` step to `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh`, so the human's run exercises the suite on their own platform in addition to the Docker build/smoke test, (b) leave the gate as it is and treat host-only breakage (such as the WeasyPrint case, now guarded by this same milestone) as an accepted, individually-patched gap, (c) require every new test whose result could plausibly depend on the host platform to carry an explicit platform guard (skip/xfail with a named reason), reviewed at each gate rather than caught after the fact | No implementation chosen — this is a brief-interpretation question, not a mechanism choice inside a control the brief already specifies (unlike D-017/D-020/D-024, which pick *how* to build something the brief names). Brief §3.4 defines what each half of the split gate is responsible for; the sandbox half is explicitly "every item [in §3.4] except the [Docker] final one," and the host half is explicitly "builds the application image... and smoke-tests it." Adding a host-native suite run to `autopilot-gate.sh` would be widening the host half's stated job, which is a reading of the brief an agent should not make unilaterally. **Recommendation: (a).** The WeasyPrint case showed the gap is real, not hypothetical, and a host-native `pytest`/`npm test` step is cheap relative to the Docker build it already runs. (c) is a weaker, manual substitute for the same protection and depends on every future session correctly guessing which tests are platform-sensitive in advance — this one wasn't caught that way. (b) accepts recurring one-off patches as the steady state. | Y — `autopilot-gate.sh` is a script, not a schema or an API; any of the three can be adopted or reversed later without touching application code. | **Y — this changes what the brief's split gate is defined to cover, which brief §3.4 assigns to the human's reading, not the agent's.** Stopping here per the item's own instruction rather than implementing a choice. Surfaced at the Phase F gate for a ruling; F.8's own WeasyPrint finding (same session) is fixed regardless of which option is chosen, since it lives in the sandbox half either way. **RESOLVED 2026-09-17 — the human ruled (a), implemented the same day.** `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh` now runs `make test-host` before the image build (cheaper, so a platform break fails fast), and the receipt's `verified=` line gains `host-pytest,host-vitest`. Two things surfaced while implementing it, both fixed in the same commit: (1) the host suite runs in the working tree while the image build archives the branch, so the gate now refuses unless `HEAD` equals the branch and the tree is clean — otherwise the receipt could name a commit the suite never ran against, which would undermine the receipt mechanism the script exists to protect; (2) `make frontend-deps` guarded on `[ -d node_modules/.bin ]`, so it installed only when `node_modules` was *absent*, never when it was *stale*. A host whose tree predated F.7's vitest/rollup additions was never refreshed, and `npm test` failed there with a missing `@rollup/rollup-darwin-arm64` (npm/cli#4828). It now depends on `node_modules/.package-lock.json` against `package-lock.json`, so a lockfile change triggers `npm ci`. That second one is the same class of bug as D-030 itself: a check that looked like it was verifying something while verifying nothing. |

**D-031** | 2026-09-17 | Milestone 1.1 stands up GitHub Actions CI (`docs/ROADMAP.md` Phase 1, review blocker B4). `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`'s CI section (a planning output ranked above this brief per §1) lists five items: (1) backend suite with a coverage floor, (2) frontend typecheck/lint/test, (3) `docker build` + the backend suite inside the image, (4) Playwright smoke against the built container, (5) an Alembic check against an empty database and a restored snapshot. `AGENTS.md`'s Quality Bar (ranked above ROADMAP.md and ARCHITECTURE.md) states what "CI must be green" means for this project more narrowly: "Backend suite, frontend typecheck and lint, `docker build`, and the backend suite inside the image" — items 1-3 only, with no mention of Playwright or audits. The autopilot brief's own R.6 separately asks for "GitHub Actions running `make check` on push to main, with coverage and Playwright report artifacts" as a *Phase R* milestone. | (a) build a workflow covering all five ARCHITECTURE.md items now, (b) build a workflow matching `AGENTS.md`'s narrower definition (items 1-3), leaving Playwright-in-CI and dependency audits to R.6 as the brief's own phasing already schedules them, (c) run the full `make check` (adds pip-audit/npm audit/E2E) in this milestone | (b) | `AGENTS.md` outranks both `docs/ROADMAP.md` and `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` in the brief's own §1 hierarchy, and its Quality Bar section is a direct, positive definition of "CI must be green" — not silent on the other two items, but naming exactly three. Building (a) or (c) now would be scope pulled forward from a named Phase R milestone (brief §5: "Do not pull work forward from later phases"), and R.6 is where the brief plans for `make check`'s coverage/Playwright-artifact wiring regardless. Item 5 (Alembic check) needs no separate CI step: `tests/test_migrations.py` (upgrade-from-empty, upgrade-from-an-MVP-snapshot) already runs as part of the backend suite in every job. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` therefore runs two jobs: `make check-fast` (backend suite + frontend typecheck/lint, plus the ruff/mypy/prettier/contract-check tooling `check-fast` already bundles — reusing existing infrastructure rather than hand-rolling a narrower duplicate), and a `docker` job (`make docker`, the new `make docker-test` running the backend suite inside a new `backend-test` Dockerfile stage, then `make docker-smoke`). | Y — widening the workflow to the full `make check` at R.6 is additive, not a reversal. | N — resolves a wording question between two already-ranked documents; no scope or architecture decision. |
**D-032** | 2026-09-17 | Item 1.1a: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` declared `on: push:` and `on: pull_request:` with no `branches` filter, so `push` fires on every branch (including every `autopilot/phase-*` branch) and a PR opened from an already-pushed local branch runs the same commits under both `push` and `pull_request` — double-billed minutes once this reaches a real remote. Brief §8's R.6 milestone specifies the target state in one phrase: "on push to `main`". | (a) narrow both triggers to `branches: [main]`, matching R.6 exactly and stopping CI from running on phase branches or non-main-targeted PRs, (b) keep the unfiltered triggers and record a reason (e.g. wanting CI feedback on phase branches before they land) | (a) | This project's own merge path (brief §3.1) is `scripts/autopilot-accept.sh` fast-forwarding `main` — there is no GitHub PR-merge workflow phase branches go through, so "CI on phase branches before they merge" is not actually how this repo integrates work, removing the one real argument for the broader trigger. `AGENTS.md`'s Quality Bar and R.6 both describe the same narrow scope; keeping the trigger broad would just be an unexamined default with a real future cost (doubled billed minutes) and no corresponding benefit here. `concurrency.group` already keys on `github.ref`, which does not dedupe `push` against `pull_request` (different refs), so the filter is the only fix. | Y — a one-line `on:` change, reversible without touching application code. | N — narrows a workflow trigger to match a phrase the brief's own R.6 milestone already specifies; no scope or architecture change. |

**D-033** | 2026-09-17 | Milestone 1.3 is "`/api/v2` with the pagination envelope and the unified error shape" (`docs/ROADMAP.md` Phase 1, review blocker B3). The two changes it carries only affect *collection reads* (an envelope around a list) and *error bodies*; the rest of the surface — the mutations and the single-resource reads — has no shape change to make. `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s breaking-changes table says v1 is "retained until the frontend moves, then removed at the end of Phase 2", which requires a complete v2 surface by then, but no Phase 2 milestone currently names that port. | (a) ship v2 as the seven paginated collection reads plus the error envelope, leaving mutations and single-resource reads on v1 for now, (b) mirror the entire v1 surface under `/api/v2` in this milestone, roughly 28 routes, (c) restructure the routers to relative prefixes and mount them twice so both versions come from one set of handlers | (a) | (b) writes ~20 endpoints that differ from their v1 twins in nothing but their path, in a milestone whose stated subject is two response shapes — scope the roadmap does not ask for, which `AGENTS.md`'s "no features beyond what is specified" rules out. (c) reads well until the shapes diverge: a handler included under two prefixes has to branch on its own mount point to know whether to return an envelope, which is the one thing a version boundary exists to avoid. Under (a) each v2 route mirrors its v1 twin's rows, filters and permissions exactly, and the shared SQL lives in one constant per table (`ACCESSIBLE_OPPORTUNITIES_SQL`, `SHARE_ROWS_SQL`, `AUDIT_LOG_ROWS_SQL`, `NOTE_VERSION_ROWS_SQL`, `timeline_filters`) so the two cannot drift while both exist. One endpoint has no v1 twin: `GET /api/v2/stages`, because v1 delivered the fixed stage list as a field on the opportunity list response and the envelope has no room for it — the same seven values the invariant fixes, not a new capability. | Y — the remaining endpoints are additive, and nothing in (a) has to be undone to reach (b) later. | **Y — not for the scoping itself but for its consequence on Phase 2's plan.** Removing v1 "at the end of Phase 2" now requires a Phase 2 milestone that ports the remaining ~20 endpoints to v2 and deletes v1, and no milestone in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 2 list says so. **Recommendation: add that milestone at this gate** (suggested: "2.9 — port the remaining endpoints to `/api/v2`, move the frontend onto it, remove v1"), or rule that v1 survives past Phase 2, in which case the roadmap's breaking-changes table should say so. Flagged rather than added, per brief §4.1.4's rule about proposals. **RESOLVED 2026-09-17 — the human ruled: add the Phase 2 milestone.** `ROADMAP.md`'s breaking-changes table stands as written (v1 removed at the end of Phase 2), and **milestone 2.9** in `PROGRESS.md` is the port that makes it true: finish the remaining endpoints, move the frontend off v1, delete v1 and the shared SQL constants 1.3 added to keep the two surfaces from drifting, regenerate the client, and update the authorization matrix. Placed in Phase 2 rather than later because the frontend still calls v1 through `apiRequest`, so the port lands in the same files milestone 2.1 decomposes — doing it afterwards would mean re-entering files that had just been restructured around v1's shapes. The 1.3 scoping itself is accepted: mirroring ~20 mutations and single-resource reads that have no shape change would have been work the roadmap does not ask for. |

**D-034** | 2026-09-17 | How the v2 cursor works, and what it costs the two endpoints whose v1 ordering is not unique. `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` specifies `?limit&cursor` and `{"items", "next_cursor"}` but not the cursor's construction. | (a) offset/limit behind an opaque cursor, (b) keyset: the cursor carries the last row's `(sort value, id)` and the next page continues strictly after it | (b) | An offset skips or repeats rows whenever something is inserted or soft-deleted between two requests, which is exactly what a timeline does while it is being read; `tests/test_api_v2.py::test_a_row_added_between_pages_does_not_duplicate_one` is the case, and it passes under (b) and cannot under (a). Keyset needs a total order, so every paginated `ORDER BY` ends in the row id, and two v2 endpoints therefore tie-break differently from their v1 twins: the timeline on `timeline_entries.id` rather than `created_at` (not unique), and shares on `opportunity_shares.user_id` rather than the shared user's email (unique, but not a column the page can carry). Both order identically to v1 for every realistic row set — entries created in sequence, share rows with distinct names — and neither changes which rows are returned. Two further consequences, both deliberate: the page unit on the timeline is the whole entry with its notes and comments, because an entry split across a page boundary is a worse shape than a long page (nested paging belongs with Phase 3's threaded comments if it is ever needed); and `GET /api/v2/notifications` returns the same unread-only rows as v1, because making read notifications visible is milestone 1.7's `read_at`/`grouped_key` work and moving it here would hide that milestone's behaviour change inside a shape change. | Y | N — a mechanism choice inside a control the roadmap and `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` already specify, with no user-visible behaviour change; the two tie-break changes are named here so a reviewer can see them without reading the SQL. |

**D-035** | 2026-09-18 | Milestone 1.4 delivers search (ADR 0007). Brief §5 says every feature milestone includes a frontend and its E2E journey, and brief §6.3's journey 6 is "Search finds opportunities and entries the user can access, and nothing else." But `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s very next Phase 1 item is "React Router and TanStack Query introduced — **justified by needing a second route**", and a search page is that second route. Building the search UI in 1.4 therefore means either pulling 1.5's router forward into a backend milestone, or bolting search into `frontend/app/page.tsx` — the single 1,783-line client component that 1.5 and 2.1 exist to take apart. | (a) build the search UI inside `page.tsx` now and rewrite it in 1.5, (b) pull the router forward and do 1.4 and 1.5 as one milestone, (c) ship 1.4 as the index, the service, the API and their tests, and land the search page plus E2E journey 6 in 1.5 where the router arrives | (c) | (a) writes UI that is known to be discarded one milestone later, and writes it into the exact file the next two milestones restructure — the most expensive place in the repo to put temporary code. (b) merges a backend milestone with a frontend-architecture milestone, producing a session too large to finish under brief §3.3's one-milestone rule, and it destroys the roadmap's own justification for introducing the router: 1.5 is honest precisely because search gives it a second route to carry. (c) keeps the sequencing the roadmap already argues for. Nothing is deferred out of Phase 1 — the phase's stated user-visible outcome ("global ranked search with highlighted snippets across every opportunity") still lands inside Phase 1, one milestone later, and the phase gate is where it is checked. What 1.4 ships is complete on its own terms: the index and its nine triggers, `GET /api/v2/search`, the timeline filter reimplemented against the index, 30 tests, the authorization-matrix entry, the `make reindex` rebuild command, a seed demo path (`?q=finance`, which returns a different result for each of the four seeded users) and the docs. | Y — the deferral is a sequencing choice inside one phase; nothing built here has to be undone to add the page. | N — it does not expand scope, change architecture, or move work out of the phase; it follows the ordering `docs/ROADMAP.md` states and argues for. Recorded because a reader of 1.4 alone would otherwise ask where the UI is. |

**D-036** | 2026-09-18 | Phase 1's acceptance line reads "Search for a rare term returns ranked, snippeted results in **< 300 ms at seeded scale**". `AGENTS.md`'s confirmed scale is ~800,000 rows, and ADR 0007's benchmark table was measured there. No 800k-row seed exists in the repository: building one is milestone **R.7**, and measuring endpoint budgets against it is milestone **R.3**. So the literal criterion cannot be evaluated in Phase 1 without building R.7's generator early. | (a) build a large-seed generator now so the figure can be measured at 800k, (b) assert nothing about latency in Phase 1 and leave the whole criterion to R.3, (c) assert the criterion at a documented, bounded corpus *and* assert separately the property that makes it hold as the corpus grows | (c) | (a) pulls a named Phase R milestone forward, which brief §5 forbids, and a generator built inside a search milestone would be designed around search rather than around R.3's list of budgets. (b) leaves a stated acceptance criterion with nothing behind it until the last phase. Under (c) `tests/test_search.py` carries two tests that split the claim: `test_a_rare_term_is_ranked_and_snippeted_inside_the_budget` builds 24,000 indexed documents (8,000 entries, notes and comments) and asserts the query returns under 300 ms, ranked, with the matched word marked in the excerpt — the acceptance line's own assertion, at a scale a test run can afford; and `test_search_reads_the_index_and_never_scans_a_source_table` asserts the query plan, which is the reason the figure barely moves with corpus size: three MATCH-driven scans of `search_index`, then every matched row reached by `SEARCH ... USING INTEGER PRIMARY KEY`, and no `SCAN` of `timeline_entries`, `notes` or `comments`. That second test was confirmed to fail first (brief §6.5) — defeating the rowid lookups in `SEARCH_DOCUMENTS_SQL` makes it report the scan-shaped plan. The latency test alone would not have caught that mutation, which is exactly why the two are separate and why the plan test is the one carrying the load. Measured here: 0.39 s for the whole test including corpus construction; the query itself is sub-millisecond, against a 300 ms budget. | Y — R.3 re-measuring at 800k either confirms the figure or supersedes it; nothing depends on the bounded number. | **Y — it narrows an acceptance criterion the roadmap states in Phase 1.** The reviewer should confirm that measuring the budget at 24,000 documents plus a query-plan assertion is acceptable for the Phase 1 gate, with the 800k measurement landing at R.3 against R.7's seed, rather than requiring the large seed to be built in this phase. **RESOLVED 2026-09-18 — ACCEPTED by the human: the split stands.** The query-plan assertion is the stronger of the two guarantees — a 300 ms budget at a small corpus passes happily over a full table scan, which this milestone demonstrated directly: the mutation that defeated the rowid lookups was caught by the plan test and missed by the latency test. ADR 0001 already measured the real figure at the confirmed 800k scale (294 ms against the 300 ms budget), which is the measurement that motivated ADR 0007 in the first place, so rebuilding that seed inside Phase 1 would duplicate R.7 to re-derive a number already on record. The 800k re-measurement against the new index stays R.3's, where it belongs. |

**D-037** | 2026-09-18 | ADR 0007 says "the timeline keyword filter is reimplemented against [the index]", and 1.4 did so (`routes_timeline.timeline_filters`). This changes what `GET /api/opportunities/{id}/timeline?q=` matches on a **v1** endpoint the shipped frontend calls: `LIKE '%term%'` matched anywhere inside a word, and the index matches word prefixes. "proc" still finds "procurement"; "curement" no longer does. Phase 1's acceptance line says "v1 endpoints still serve the existing frontend". | (a) reimplement the filter against the index as ADR 0007 directs, accepting the matching change, (b) leave the v1 filter on `LIKE` and use the index only for the new `/api/v2/search`, (c) index a second, character-level tokenization so substring matching survives | (a) | ADR 0007 lists "matches substrings inside words" among the defects that justify the index — the change is the fix, not a side effect of it, and a search box that matches word prefixes is what a user expects. (b) leaves two implementations of the same question in the codebase, which is the drift ADR 0007 and the shared-SQL constants from D-033 both exist to prevent, and keeps the 294 ms worst case on the endpoint the frontend actually calls. (c) roughly triples index size for a behaviour nobody asked for. No existing assertion was changed to accommodate this — `test_timeline.py`'s filter test uses a whole word and passes unaltered — and `test_timeline_keyword_filter_matches_word_prefixes` was added to state the new rule explicitly rather than leave it implicit. The other v1 filters (`type`, `from`, `to`, `author_id`) are untouched. | Y | N — the change is what the accepted ADR specifies, it is an improvement rather than a regression in every case except a mid-word fragment, and no v1 response *shape* changed. Named here so the gate reviewer sees a v1 behaviour change without having to diff the SQL. |

**D-038** | 2026-09-18 | Milestone 1.5 says "React Router and TanStack Query introduced". `frontend/app/Home.tsx` is 1,776 lines holding all server state in `useState` with "await the write, then refetch everything" mutations (ADR 0005's blocker B1). How much of it moves onto the query cache in this milestone? | (a) migrate `Home.tsx` onto TanStack Query as part of introducing it, (b) introduce the provider and use the cache only for the new route's reads, leaving `Home.tsx` on bare `apiRequest` until 2.1 decomposes it, (c) introduce the router now and the query layer in 2.1 | (b) | `docs/ROADMAP.md` assigns the decomposition to Phase 2 ("Decompose `page.tsx` into routed views"), and ADR 0005 pairs the two: TanStack Query replaces "copy the response into component state", which is a rewrite of the same 28 hooks that 2.1 exists to take apart. Doing it under (a) means rewriting every one of `Home.tsx`'s reads and mutations in a milestone whose stated deliverable is the router, the query layer, the search page and journey 6 — a session that cannot finish under brief §3.3, and one that would be redone as the file is split. (c) introduces a router with no cache behind it, so the new page would hand-roll fetching and be rewritten in 2.1 anyway. Under (b) the query layer is real and exercised — `SearchPage` reads the session and the result pages through it, including `useInfiniteQuery` over the v2 cursor, so the envelope from 1.3 is now proven from a client — and `Home.tsx` is untouched except for one `<Link>` to the new route. `CLAUDE.md` names the split so nobody adds a third pattern. | Y | N — it follows the roadmap's own phase split; no scope moves out of Phase 1 and nothing built here is undone in 2.1. |

**D-039** | 2026-09-18 | A search result names its opportunity and its matched text, but clicking it opens nothing. The timeline is rendered by `Home.tsx` from `selectedOpportunityId` in component state, so no opportunity, entry or note has a URL to link to. | (a) give `Home.tsx` a route param (`/opportunities/:id`) now so results can link into the timeline, (b) ship results as text this milestone and link them when the timeline gets its own URL in 2.1, (c) link the result to `/?opportunity=<id>` and have `Home.tsx` read the query string | (b) | (a) and (c) both mean teaching `Home.tsx` to accept selection from the URL without making the URL follow selection — the back button and a shared link would agree with the page only until the user clicked anything, which is a half-built version of the "URL is the source of truth" property that ADR 0005 and milestone 2.1 own end to end. Brief §6.3's journey 6 is "search finds what the user can access and nothing else", which is what `e2e/08-search.spec.ts` asserts; click-through is not part of it, and Phase 1's stated outcome ("global ranked search with highlighted snippets across every opportunity") is delivered. Named as a known limitation in the milestone and in the phase review packet so it is a decision on the record rather than an omission. | Y | N — no architecture or scope change; it defers one link to the milestone that creates the thing it would point at. Worth the reviewer's eye anyway: it is the one place where this milestone's UI visibly stops short. |

**D-040** | 2026-09-18 | ADR 0005 calls for one optional catch-all (`app/[[...slug]]/page.tsx`) exporting only the root, with React Router client-side. But `output: "export"` renders that tree once in Node at build time, where `BrowserRouter` has no `window.history`; and on a deep link the browser receives HTML prerendered for `/` and then hydrates a different route. | (a) mount the router unconditionally and accept a build-time failure plus hydration mismatches, (b) render nothing until the component is running in a browser, so the export emits an empty shell and the first client render matches it, (c) keep Next routing and add a second exported page for `/search` | (b) | (a) does not build. (c) contradicts ADR 0005 and splits the SPA across two HTML files, each hydrating its own tree — and it would still need a router the moment a third view appears. Under (b) `AppRoot` reads `useSyncExternalStore(subscribe, () => true, () => false)`: the server snapshot is what the export and the hydration pass see, so the emitted `index.html` body is empty and hydration has nothing to reconcile. The cost is that first paint now waits for the JS bundle, where it used to show a prerendered sign-in form; the app has never functioned without JavaScript, and ADR 0005 accepted the client-side SPA. `app/AppRoot.test.tsx` asserts `renderToStaticMarkup(<AppRoot />)` is empty (confirmed to fail against an ungated mount, per brief §6.5) and that `generateStaticParams` emits only the root, so neither half can regress quietly. Package choice recorded here too: `react-router` 8.4.0, pinned exactly like every other frontend dependency (F.8), not the `react-router-dom` alias, which is a re-export shim since v7. | Y | N — it implements ADR 0005 as written; the empty shell is a consequence of the SPA shape that ADR already accepted, named here because it is user-visible on a cold load. |
