## Human note — 2026-09-17 (Phase F review, host-side sandbox fixes)

Two sandbox defects found while running the host gate. Neither is a Phase F
deliverable defect; both are in `sandbox/run.sh` and were fixed on the host.

**1. `frontend/node_modules` was shared between host and container.** The F.2
note above states the sandbox gives the container its own `node_modules` volume.
It did not: commit `2960874`, titled "restore frontend overlay volumes and fix
volume ownership", changed only this file and never touched `run.sh`. The two
environments shared one tree through the repo bind mount, so whichever ran
`npm ci` last won and broke the other (npm/cli#4828 — a Linux install leaves the
host without `@rollup/rollup-darwin-arm64`). Observed live: a host `npm ci`
broke the F.9 sandbox session, whose own `npm ci` then broke the host.

Fixed with a `--mount ... volume-nocopy=true` overlay plus an `ensure_volumes()`
that chowns the volume to the caller. `volume-nocopy` is required: Docker seeds
an empty volume from whatever is at the mount path, which here is the host tree,
so a plain `--volume` would copy the mismatch straight in. This is the node
counterpart to `UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT`, which already protects `backend/.venv`.

**2. `sandbox/run.sh verify` destroyed uncommitted work.** Its commit-to-main
probe ran `git commit --allow-empty` followed by `git reset --hard HEAD~1`. On
any branch other than `main` the commit legitimately succeeds, so the probe
reported a false `guard FAILED` and then hard-reset the bind-mounted repo,
deleting uncommitted changes — it ate this very fix once before it was
committed. Now `reset --soft` (the probe commit is empty, so this restores the
prior state exactly) and the message distinguishes "not exercised on this
branch" from a real failure.


---

## Human note — 2026-09-18 (milestone 1.4 review): the search index rowid ceiling

Recorded at the 1.4 gate because it is a constraint nobody chose explicitly and
the phase that trips over it is several phases away.

`search.py` encodes document identity as `source id * 4 + kind code`, which is
why the contentless index needs no mapping table beside it. The encoding is
documented in that module's docstring, repeated by the migration's nine
triggers, and pinned by `tests/test_search.py`. It is a good design for the
three types that exist today (timeline entries, notes, comments).

**It hard-caps the index at four document types.** Phase 4 adds attachments and
Phase 5 adds inbound email, both plausibly searchable. The fourth slot absorbs
one of them; the fifth has nowhere to go.

This is not treated as a defect and no change is asked for now. A contentless
FTS5 index stores no copy of the source text, so it is rebuildable from the
source tables by construction — `make reindex` already does exactly that, and
`test_rebuild_reproduces_the_index_the_migration_built` proves the rebuild
matches what the migration produced. So widening the encoding (to `* 8`, or to
an explicit `(kind, id)` pair) is a migration that rewrites rowids and reindexes,
not a data-loss risk.

**Action for whichever milestone first needs a fourth or fifth searchable type**
(4.x attachments, or 5.x email): widen the encoding as part of that milestone
rather than working around the ceiling, and log the choice. Check
`test_writes_are_indexed_under_the_documented_rowid`, which asserts the current
scheme and will fail first.

---

**D-041** | 2026-09-20 | Milestone 1.6 (`docs/ROADMAP.md` Phase 1) asks for a "`VACUUM INTO` backup job with a tested restore (ADR 0011)." ADR 0011's own decision list calls this "a scheduled job (ADR 0004)," but ADR 0004 (APScheduler-in-process, a durable `outbox` table) is not yet built — `PROGRESS.md`'s milestone 5.1 is where it lands, and nothing before it introduces a scheduler. | (a) stand up a minimal APScheduler instance now, ahead of 5.1, just to give the backup job something to be scheduled by, (b) build the backup/restore mechanism as a script invoked by `make backup` / `make restore` (or host cron), with no in-process scheduling, and revisit wiring it onto APScheduler once 5.1 lands | (b) | (a) is exactly the kind of pulled-forward scope brief §3.1/§9 forbid ("do not pull work forward from later phases"): APScheduler's job is periodic jobs across five phases' worth of features (digests, reminders, stalled detection, inbound email polling), and standing up a partial instance for one job now means either a second, incompatible scheduling mechanism gets built in 5.1 or this milestone's throwaway instance becomes de facto load-bearing infrastructure nobody designed for that. (b) delivers everything the milestone's own line actually asks for — a `VACUUM INTO` snapshot, retention, and a restore procedure that `tests/test_backup.py::test_restore_recovers_data_written_after_a_backup` exercises for real — without deciding ADR 0004's shape early. ADR 0011's "(ADR 0004)" citation names the eventual home for recurring execution, not a precondition for building the mechanism it schedules. | Y — once 5.1 lands, `make backup`'s call becomes a periodic job function registered with APScheduler; `app/backup.py`'s `create_backup`/`restore_backup` need no change, since ADR 0004 asks for job functions "free of framework coupling" for exactly this reason. | N — an ordering call inside an already-accepted ADR and an already-scheduled milestone, not a scope expansion or an architecture change. |

**D-042** | 2026-09-20 | `/api/health`'s writability probe (milestone 1.6) needs to detect a database that cannot be written to. `database.get_connection()` asserts `PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL` on every call, which is itself a write — so a connection to a genuinely read-only database (read-only mount, full disk) can fail before any query runs, not just at an explicit `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` probe. Confirmed empirically: `tests/test_health.py::test_health_reports_unwritable_when_the_database_file_is_read_only` raised an uncaught `sqlite3.OperationalError` from inside `db_connection()`'s `__enter__` when only the probe itself was wrapped in `try`/`except`. | (a) wrap only the `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`/rollback probe in `try`/`except`, leaving connection acquisition unguarded, (b) wrap the whole health check body — connection acquisition and the probe — in one `try`/`except sqlite3.OperationalError`, reporting `database_writable: false` (and `schema_version: "unknown"` if the schema query never ran) on either failure | (b) | (a) turns the one endpoint whose entire job is "answer during a database failure" into a 500 on the specific failure mode (unwritable storage) it exists to report — the opposite of what "reports substance" (ADR 0011) means. `AGENTS.md` standard 4 (log the cause, don't swallow it) is satisfied by `logger.warning(..., exc_info=exc)` either way. | Y | N — a bugfix to this milestone's own new code, caught by its own test before commit, not a new judgment call about existing behavior. |

**D-043** | 2026-09-20 | Milestone 1.7's notifications inbox rework needed a shape for `GET /api/v2/notifications` that CLAUDE.md's existing rule for the whole `/api/v2` surface doesn't fit: "a v2 route mirrors its v1 twin's rows, filters and permissions exactly — the boundary carries response shape, not behavior." `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s breaking-changes table already settles the substance ("v1 shape preserved behind `/api/v1`"), but leaves the mechanics open: how literally "preserved" should be read, and whether a single mark-read mutation needs a v2 twin. | (a) keep `NotificationPublic` as the one response model, adding `read_at`/`grouped_key` as new optional fields so v1 gains them too (additive fields are usually non-breaking); route the v1 list query to also read `read_at` for consistency, (b) add a distinct `NotificationPublicV2` used only by the v2 route, with v1's model and query completely untouched, and (b2) do not add a v2 twin of `PATCH .../read` — keep the existing v1 route as the one mark-read mutation, since it now writes `read_at` too via the shared `notification_service.mark_notification_read` helper | (b) + (b2) | (a) would make a client parsing v1's response see fields it never asked for and that the roadmap's own words ("v1 shape preserved") argue against introducing — "preserved" reads more naturally as "unchanged" than "unchanged plus". (b) keeps that literal: v1's route, query and model are all byte-for-byte what they were before this milestone, which is also what let the existing v1 tests pass with zero edits. For (b2): a v2 mark-read route would return the exact same row through a different serializer for no behavioural gain over the v1 route now that both read/write the same `read_at` column — two endpoints doing one job is the kind of surface brief §9's "simplicity over defensiveness" argues against, and milestone 1.7's own text names `mark-all-read` as the new mutation, not a v2 single mark-read. | Y — reversible: a later milestone (2.9, "finish the v2 migration and remove v1") can add the v2 mark-read route and retire v1's when v1 itself is retired, without touching v2's list route again. | N — an implementation-mechanics call inside a decision the roadmap's breaking-changes table had already made; not architectural and does not expand scope. |

## Phase 1, rework 1 (`docs/autopilot/reviews/phase-1-review-1.md`)

**D-044** | 2026-09-20 | Review finding 1: the production image runs as root, which is why `tests/test_health.py::test_health_reports_unwritable_when_the_database_file_is_read_only` failed inside `make docker-test` — root bypasses the permission bits the test relies on. ADR 0011 item 5 already calls for a non-root user; the review's ruling is to pull that one item forward from R.4 into this rework rather than debate it again. What was still open was the mechanism: a fixed UID, and how `/data` — a bind mount whose ownership belongs to the host, not the image — stays writable by it. | (a) a fixed numeric UID/GID (1000:1000, the conventional first non-root Linux user) created in `backend-base` so `backend-test` and `final` share one identity, with the run scripts (`start_linux.sh`/`start_mac.sh`) creating `./data` world-writable before the first run, (b) an entrypoint script that starts as root, `chown`s `/data` to the app user, then execs the real command as that user (the `gosu`/`su-exec` pattern), (c) an arbitrary/unpinned UID (`useradd` with no `--uid`), leaving the host-side fix undefined | (a) | (b) needs a second binary in the image (`gosu` or `su-exec`) and a process that briefly runs as root on every container start specifically to avoid a one-time permission fix, which is more moving parts than the problem needs at this app's scale (ADR 0011: "no CORS, no reverse proxy, no service discovery" — the same appetite argues against adding an entrypoint layer here). (c) leaves every operator to discover the UID by inspecting the image before they can `chown` a host directory to match it. (a) is what `test_health`'s own container needs — no bind mount, so the image's own `mkdir -p /data && chown app:app /data` covers CI and `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh`/`docker-smoke.sh` outright — and for a real bind-mounted deployment, `chmod 0777 data` in the three start scripts is one line, doesn't require knowing the container's UID, and matches this app's actual deployment shape: a single operator's own machine, not a shared host where a world-writable data directory is a meaningful new exposure. `backend-test` runs as the same user as `final` so the suite that guards this (including `test_health`'s own assertion) runs the way the image does. | Y — the UID, the chmod approach, and the (rejected) entrypoint pattern are all independently reversible. | N — implements an item ADR 0011 already accepted; the mechanism choice is proportional to a single-operator deployment already described in that ADR, not a new architectural surface. |

**D-045** | 2026-09-20 | Review finding 2: the shipped image's Python links Debian bookworm's system `libsqlite3` (3.40.1); the autopilot sandbox's Python — where ADR 0001 and ADR 0007 were benchmarked, and where this codebase's tests are actually developed — bundles a newer one (3.50.4, confirmed by re-running `python3 -c "import sqlite3; print(sqlite3.sqlite_version)"` in this session); the reviewer's own machine measured a third (3.53.1). The FTS5 query planner changed enough between 3.40 and 3.50 that `tests/test_search.py::test_search_reads_the_index_and_never_scans_a_source_table` — which asserts `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` shape, not a stopwatch, exactly so a planner regression is caught — fails inside `make docker-test` on the older engine, even though the query itself is correct and the ADR 0001/0007 benchmarks that justified it still hold on the version they were measured on. **This is a decision, not a mechanism choice, and is not implemented here — the review's own instruction was to log it and stop.** | (a) move the image to a base with a modern SQLite (a `trixie` base, or compile SQLite in the image), closing the gap in the direction of the engine the ADRs were reasoned and benchmarked against; costs a base-image change touching every Dockerfile stage, and the three WeasyPrint packages (`libharfbuzz-subset0`, `libpango-1.0-0`, `libpangoft2-1.0-0`) would need re-verifying on the new base, (b) pin development and the sandbox to bookworm's 3.40.1 so all three environments agree; cheapest to reason about, but freezes the project on a SQLite three-plus years old and means ADR 0001's and ADR 0007's existing benchmark numbers (taken on 3.50.4) would need re-measuring on the older engine before they could be trusted for what actually ships, (c) keep the gap and make `test_search_reads_the_index_and_never_scans_a_source_table` version-aware (e.g. skip or branch its assertions below some SQLite version) — **rejected**, per the review's own instruction not to weaken either failing assertion; recorded only so the decision explains why it is off the table | **Recommendation: (a).** ADR 0001 and ADR 0007 both reason about and benchmark a modern SQLite (FTS5's bm25 ranking, the query-plan shapes ADR 0007's own test now depends on) and are the two ADRs load-bearing on this codebase's whole search and concurrency design; shipping a materially older engine than the one those decisions were reasoned against is the actual risk this finding names, not just a test failure. (b) inverts that — it would hold the *product* to an engine already three years old at v2's launch, to avoid a Dockerfile change, and would require re-benchmarking two accepted ADRs before their numbers could be trusted again. This agent could not evaluate (a)'s WeasyPrint-on-`trixie` risk empirically: the sandbox has no Docker socket and no root/apt access to install a second `sqlite3` build to test against (confirmed — `apt-get install sqlite3` and `sudo` both fail with permission errors in this environment), so this is a recommendation for the human to weigh, not something ruled out here for lack of trying. | Y — either option is a Dockerfile/tooling change; nothing in application code depends on which is chosen, per ADR 0001's own note that the schema carries no SQLite-version-specific DDL beyond `AUTOINCREMENT` and the canonical-timestamp default. | **Y — architectural, and explicitly not to be adopted unilaterally per the review's own instruction.** Recommend (a); alternative (b), with the re-benchmarking cost named. Until ruled, `tests/test_search.py::test_search_reads_the_index_and_never_scans_a_source_table` is expected to keep failing inside `make docker-test`/`scripts/autopilot-gate.sh 1` on the host — this is the same known, diagnosed failure the review found, still correctly failing, not a new regression (see `docs/autopilot/BLOCKERS.md`). **RESOLVED 2026-09-20 — the human ruled (a), and it is implemented.** The one cost this entry could not evaluate — WeasyPrint on `trixie` — was measured on the host before the ruling rather than assumed, and it is zero: all three packages install under the same names, and the in-image suite passes with no `skipped`, so `test_pdf_export_produces_a_real_pdf` actually executes. The decisive measurement was that `trixie` ships **3.46.1**, between the 3.40.1 that fails and the 3.50.4 the ADRs were benchmarked on — so the open question was whether 3.46.1 is far enough. It is: a `backend-test` image built on `trixie` runs **258 passed** with no test modified, including both failures the review found. That places the FTS5 planner change between 3.40 and 3.46, which is what makes (a) a four-line base swap rather than compiling SQLite into the image. `Dockerfile`'s two base images moved `bookworm-slim` → `trixie-slim`. Note ADR 0001's benchmark annotation stays accurate and should not be deleted: the numbers were measured on 3.50.4, and the image now ships 3.46.1 — a gap of months rather than years, but still a gap, and the annotation is what will matter if search latency is ever re-measured. |

**D-046** | 2026-09-20 | Review finding 3 asks for "an assertion that the environments agree... for example, a test or a `make check` step that fails when the image's SQLite version differs from the one development runs against, naming both" — a detection mechanism, independent of D-045's still-open resolution. | (a) a pytest assertion inside the shared backend suite, comparing `sqlite3.sqlite_version` to a hardcoded expected constant, so it runs everywhere `make check-fast`/`make test-host`/`make docker-test` already do, (b) a standalone host-only script (`scripts/check-sqlite-version.sh`) that runs the project's own Python on the host and inside the freshly built image and diffs the two directly, wired into `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh` (right after the image build, before the suite finding 2's failure lives in) and a new `make sqlite-version-check` / CI step | (b) | (a) cannot work today without also resolving D-045: this sandbox, the reviewer's host, and the shipped image currently report three *different* versions (3.50.4, 3.53.1, 3.40.1), so any single hardcoded constant a normal pytest assertion could check against would newly fail `make check-fast` or `make test-host` in at least one of those places — turning a detector into a second copy of the exact problem it exists to name, and doing so inside the gate this very rework needs to keep green elsewhere. (b) sidesteps that: it compares exactly two things that are directly comparable at the moment anyone runs the host gate — this machine's own Python and the image just built from the same commit — and names both version strings in its failure output rather than requiring a reader to decode a query-plan diff. It is expected to fail today, by design, since D-045 is unresolved; that failure is the mechanism working, not a new defect. | Y — trivially deleted or folded into a pytest test later if D-045's resolution makes a shared constant meaningful. | N — a detection mechanism the review asked for by name; it takes no position on D-045 itself. |

**D-047** | 2026-09-20 | Implementing D-045(a) exposed that `scripts/check-sqlite-version.sh`, written in the same rework to guard this class of gap, compares the host's `sqlite3.sqlite_version` to the image's and fails on any difference. That check is unsatisfiable: the developer's macOS Python (3.53.1), the autopilot sandbox's Linux Python (3.50.4) and the image's Debian `libsqlite3` (3.46.1 on `trixie`) are packaged independently by three different distributors, so they will essentially never be equal. As written the guard fails even after the divergence it exists to catch has been fixed. | (a) keep exact equality and pin all three environments to one build, (b) assert a minimum version floor on the image, (c) drop the guard | (b) | The property that actually has to hold is not that the three agree, but that the image is not *older* than the engine the guarantees were established against — the FTS5 planner shape `test_search_reads_the_index_and_never_scans_a_source_table` asserts, and the WAL/`busy_timeout` behaviour ADR 0001 depends on. A newer host is harmless; an older image is the failure. (a) would mean controlling how Homebrew, the sandbox image and Debian each package SQLite, which is not achievable and would make the gate hostage to an unrelated upstream bump. (c) gives up the only check that names this divergence by version number instead of waiting for it to surface as an unrelated test failure, which is how the original finding was discovered. The floor is `3.46.1`, set by measurement rather than changelog: that version runs all 258 backend tests in-image, and 3.40.1 fails the query-plan assertion. | Y — the floor is one constant in one script. | N — this corrects a check written in the same unmerged rework to be satisfiable at all; it does not change what is being guarded, and the guard is stricter in the sense that matters (it now passes only when the image is new enough, rather than never passing). |

**D-048** | 2026-09-20 | Verifying D-045(a) with a full `docker build .` surfaced that the image build had been broken for the `docker build .` path all along, and nobody had noticed because nothing exercises it. `.dockerignore` excludes `vitest.config.ts` and `vitest.setup.ts` but not `*.test.tsx`; `frontend/tsconfig.json` excludes only `node_modules`, so `npm run build` type-checks the test files; and `vitest.setup.ts` is what imports `@testing-library/jest-dom/vitest` and registers the matcher type augmentations. Without it the build fails with eight `Property 'toBeInTheDocument' does not exist` errors. The reason this never surfaced: `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh` builds with `git archive "$BRANCH" \| docker build -`, and **`.dockerignore` is not applied to a tar stdin context** — verified by inspecting the resulting image, where `/app/frontend/vitest.setup.ts` is present. `make docker-test` escapes it too, but only because `--target backend-test` never builds the `frontend-builder` stage. So `make docker`, `make docker-smoke` and CI's `docker` job (milestone 1.1, never yet run because nothing is pushed) were all broken from a clean checkout, while the gate passed. | (a) stop excluding `vitest.setup.ts`/`vitest.config.ts`, shipping test config into the production image, (b) exclude the test files as well, so the production build has nothing test-shaped to type-check, (c) give the image build its own tsconfig that excludes tests, (d) stop type-checking during `npm run build` | (b) | The production image has no business containing test code or type-checking it, so the fix that also improves image hygiene is the one to take. (a) works but inverts the goal — it adds test scaffolding to the shipped image to satisfy a type-check of files that should not be there. (c) is (b) with an extra config file to keep in sync. (d) gives up a real check: the type error was genuine in the context it ran in. `include` globs do not error on matching nothing, so removing the test files is sufficient with no tsconfig change. Verified by building the `frontend-builder` stage from the working directory, which failed before and passes after. | Y — a `.dockerignore` change. | N — a build fix with a proven before/after, not a design choice. **But it leaves a related finding open, recorded here rather than fixed:** the gate and `make docker` build from different contexts, so the gate does not test the image `make docker` produces. `.dockerignore` is now written so both paths succeed, but they still produce different images (the archive path includes test files, the local path does not). Making the gate build the same context it ships is a change to the split gate's mechanics — the same class of question as D-030 — and belongs in a phase that owns the gate, with R.4/R.6 the natural home. |

**D-049** | 2026-09-20 | Running `make docker-test` after the rework's non-root change (D-044) failed before reaching a test: `error: Failed to initialize cache at /app/.cache/uv — Permission denied (os error 13)`. The `backend-test` stage's `CMD` is `uv run pytest ...`, and `uv` writes a cache under `$HOME`, which is `/app` for the `app` user (`useradd --home-dir /app`) and owned by root — the stage's `chown -R app:app /app/backend` covers the application tree but not `$HOME` itself. The agent that made the non-root change could not have caught this: the sandbox has no Docker socket, so nothing there runs the image. | (a) `ENV UV_NO_CACHE=1` on the stage, (b) `ENV UV_CACHE_DIR=/tmp/uv-cache`, (c) widen the `chown` to `/app`, (d) run `.venv/bin/python -m pytest` instead of `uv run` | (a) | The stage already builds with `uv sync --locked --no-cache`, so disabling the cache at run time matches how the image is assembled rather than introducing a second convention. A container that runs one command and exits has nothing to gain from a populated cache, which is what (b) and (c) preserve at the cost of a writable directory that exists only to be discarded. (d) works — it is what the ad-hoc verification of D-045 used — but bypasses `uv` rather than configuring it, and would drift from the `uv run` the rest of the project uses. Verified: `docker run --rm opptrail-test` now reports 258 passed at 97.32% coverage. | Y — one `ENV` line. | N — a build fix with a proven before/after. Worth noting for the phase that owns R.4: the same `$HOME`-not-writable shape will reappear for any future stage that runs a tool expecting a home directory, and `final` avoids it today only because it runs `uvicorn` from an already-synced venv. |

**D-050** | 2026-09-20 | Found during the human's hand-run user test against the Phase 1 build, not by any automated check. Check 6 of the test procedure asked the tester to open a note's revision history and there was nowhere to open it: `note_versions` is populated and `GET /api/notes/{note_id}/versions` works, but the frontend contains no reference to versions at all, so the feature is unreachable outside curl. Investigating that surfaced the larger gap — only notes keep history. `routes_opportunities.py:271` and `routes_timeline.py:416` both write bare `UPDATE`s: an opportunity's `value`, `name`, `account_name`, `currency` and `expected_close_date` change with no record of the previous value or its author (only a `stage` change leaves a system timeline entry), and a timeline entry's title, body, `event_timestamp` and type are silently overwritten. So the project audits destruction (invariant 5, soft deletes; invariant 7, every delete path writes an audit row) while leaving quiet rewriting untracked — a deal value moving from 485,000 to 250,000 is currently invisible. | (a) build the missing note-history screen only, (b) extend versioning to opportunities and timeline entries as well, and give all three one screen, (c) leave it and record the gap | (b), by the human's instruction: "it is important that we have version history on any revision or edit on any of the elements... perhaps you can limit this for the owner of the opportunity or admin but important to have UI for it." | (a) would have closed the symptom and left the hole: the note is the one element that *does* already keep history. The asymmetry is the finding — `AGENTS.md` argues throughout that this project's value is an auditable record, and a record that can be rewritten without trace is not one. Recorded as **invariant 8** in `AGENTS.md` rather than only as a milestone, because it is a standing property every future edit path must satisfy, in the same way invariant 7 binds every future delete path; added as **v2 scope item 15** and scheduled as milestone **2.10**. Visibility is owner-only, matching the audit log. Worth stating plainly: **there is no admin role in this system** — `permissions.py` has owner, editor and viewer — so the human's "owner of the opportunity or admin" resolves to the opportunity owner. Introducing an admin role would be a separate and much larger decision about a cross-opportunity superuser, which nothing in v2 currently has. Comments need no versioning: they cannot be edited by anyone, so immutability already gives what history would have given. | Y — a schema and UI addition; nothing existing depends on the absence of history. | **Y — a new invariant in `AGENTS.md`, which outranks the autopilot brief in the source-of-truth hierarchy.** Recorded from the human's explicit instruction during review rather than proposed by an agent, so it is already ruled; flagged here so the Phase 2 session treats invariant 8 as binding rather than as a suggestion it may scope down. |

**D-051** | 2026-09-20 | Two further requirements raised by the human while hand-running the Phase 1 user test, with the instruction to add them "only if they are not already reflected". Checked both against `AGENTS.md`, `docs/ROADMAP.md` and this plan before adding. **(1) Editing in place.** Not reflected anywhere. The only "inline" in the roadmap is Phase 4's "editor for inline attachment", which is about embedding files in the rich-text editor and unrelated. Today, editing an entry or note moves the reader to a form elsewhere on the page and they lose their position in the timeline — the complaint that prompted this, and it gets worse the further down a long timeline the item sits. **(2) Timeline order direction.** *Partially* reflected, and worth being precise about rather than claiming either way: milestone 2.2 already carries a "shared filter/sort/group model serialized to the URL", but that model was written for views over the opportunity list — board, table, calendar — and nothing states that an opportunity's own timeline can be read oldest-first or newest-first. The generic word "sort" appearing in an adjacent milestone is not the same as the requirement being captured. | (a) add both as new milestones, (b) fold each into the milestone that already owns its area, (c) treat 2.2's existing sort model as already covering the ordering | (b) for ordering, (a) for editing in place | Ordering belongs inside 2.2 because the direction should live in the URL exactly like every other view parameter, so a particular reading of a timeline can be linked to and saved; a second, separate ordering mechanism beside the shared model is how two sort systems that disagree get built. Editing in place is genuinely new behaviour touching every editable surface, so it earns its own milestone (**2.11**) rather than being buried in 2.2's line. (c) rejected: the requirement would have been satisfied on paper and missed in practice. Both are recorded in `AGENTS.md`'s v2 scope — item 1 gains the timeline's reading order, item 12 gains editing where content is read — because that file is the source of truth a future session reads, and a milestone alone can be descoped while a scope item cannot. Two implementation notes carried into the milestones so they are not rediscovered late: reversing the timeline has to reverse 1.3's keyset cursor comparison, not only the `ORDER BY`, or paging breaks at the boundary; and 2.11 touches the same write paths as 2.10's edit history, so the two should be sequenced together rather than editing those routes twice. | Y — both are UI and query-parameter work. | N — the human raised both explicitly and directed that they be added; the only judgement here was where each belongs, and the honest note that ordering was adjacent to an existing milestone rather than absent from the plan. |

**D-052** | 2026-09-20 | Check 13 of the human's user test reported "AI service not available". Diagnosis: the API key was fine (`GET /v1/models` → 200), but `OPENAI_MODEL` in the local `.env` was `gpt-oss-20b`, which OpenAI does not serve — `GET /v1/models/gpt-oss-20b` → 404 `model_not_found`. That value is the last trace of the OpenRouter-era assumption D-001 corrected in `docs/v2_review_brief.md`: the documentation was fixed, the environment was not. Note the codebase itself was never wrong — `settings.py`'s default and `.env.example` both carry `gpt-5.6-luna`, which exists and answers a real `responses.create` call. Two separate issues fall out: the stale local config (fixed by editing `.env`, which is gitignored and not a code change) and the fact that a thirty-second operator fix presented as an unexplained outage. | For the message: (a) surface the provider's raw error, (b) classify failures into configuration / outage / rejected-request and say what to check, (c) leave it | (b), scheduled into **R.1**, which already owns error states everywhere | (a) leaks provider internals and possibly key fragments to anyone who can ask a question, including a viewer. (c) leaves the operator no signal to act on: a misconfiguration and a provider outage need opposite responses, and today they are indistinguishable. Placed in R.1 rather than given its own milestone because R.1 is already "loading/empty/error states everywhere" and this is an instance of exactly that; noted there as cheap enough to pull forward if an earlier phase is already editing `ai_service.py`, so the placement is about ownership rather than sequencing. Recorded in `AGENTS.md` v2 scope item 14 as well, so a future session reads it as part of what the AI feature *is* rather than as a nice-to-have in a late phase. | Y — an error-handling change. | N — the human asked for the requirement explicitly; the only judgement was where it belongs. **One thing worth carrying forward:** this defect class is structurally invisible to the suite. `tests/test_ai.py` monkeypatches `ask_openai` because brief §9 forbids paid calls in tests, which is correct and should not change — but it means no test ever sends a real model name, and no amount of coverage would have caught a wrong one. The R.1 work should assert the *classification* of a simulated provider error, not attempt a real call. It is also a reminder that mocked boundaries hide configuration defects by construction: the same shape will exist for any future external service. |

**D-053** | 2026-09-20 | After the Phase 1 user test the human asked whether the manual checks could run automatically, with an option to step in, spot-check and review results instead of running them by hand. The machinery mostly exists: `frontend/e2e/` has 18 Playwright specs with axe, and `npx playwright test --ui` already provides watch-run, pause, step, live DOM inspection and trace time-travel — the "step in any time" part of the question is a flag, not a build. The real question is which checks *should* be automated. | (a) automate the whole thing, (b) leave it entirely manual, (c) automate the checks with a fixed right answer and deliberately keep the judgment ones human | (c), scheduled as milestone **2.12** | The evidence is this user test itself. Three findings came out of it, and **not one was reachable by automation**: note history had no screen (the test asked the tester to open something that did not exist — a machine would have had no step to fail), opportunity and entry edits left no trace (D-050 — nothing was broken, something was *absent*), and a misconfigured `OPENAI_MODEL` presented as an outage (D-052). The suite was green throughout. Automation asserts what it was told to assert; it cannot notice an absence or judge that a message is unhelpful. The D-052 case is the sharpest: `tests/test_ai.py` mocks `ask_openai` because brief §9 forbids paid calls, so no test in any suite ever sends a real model name — that defect class is invisible by construction, and more coverage would not change it. So (a) would have produced a clean run and told the human nothing. (b) wastes a human on checks a machine repeats perfectly and forever. Under (c) the deterministic checks become regression guards that protect Phase 2's refactor, and the human's twenty minutes go where only a human can look. Placed in Phase 2 because it rewrites the UI, so `e2e/`'s selectors need revisiting regardless — extending the suite while already in those files is far cheaper than a separate pass. | Y — test-suite scope; nothing in the application depends on it. | N — the human asked for it directly. **One correction recorded rather than quietly dropped:** when this split was first sketched in conversation, the sanitization check (no dialog, no `javascript:` navigation) was grouped with the manual ones. That was too coarse. Its *rendering* half is judgment, but its security half is deterministic and high-severity, so it belongs in CI regardless — `sanitize.py`'s unit tests passing is not the same as asserting it end to end in a real browser. 2.12 lists it under automate. |

**D-054** | 2026-09-20 | Milestone 2.1 gives the opportunity and its timeline their own URL. Does `/opportunities/:id` become a page that replaces the dashboard, or a child route rendered beside the board that stays mounted? | (a) a full page: the timeline replaces the board, dashboard reachable by a back link, (b) a layout route: `DashboardPage` owns the shell and the board and renders `<Outlet/>` where the timeline panel already sat, (c) keep the board and timeline in one component and read the id from the URL without nesting routes | (b) | The MVP already showed board and timeline side by side, and nothing in Phase 2's scope asks to change that — 2.5 (design tokens), 2.7 (board view) and 2.8 (table and calendar) own the visual rework, and doing it here would mix a structural refactor with a redesign in the one milestone whose whole point is that the file stops being 1,863 lines. (b) also keeps the list query mounted across an opportunity switch, so opening a second opportunity re-renders the panel only, which is the behaviour the cache was introduced for. (c) is what `Home.tsx` did with `selectedOpportunityId`, plus a URL read — the half-built version D-039 rejected, where the URL follows selection only until the user clicks something. The panel is re-keyed on the id so filters and a half-finished entry edit do not leak from one opportunity to the next, which is what `openTimeline`'s manual resets used to do. | Y — the route table is four lines. | N — it preserves the shipped layout and follows the roadmap's own phase split; no scope moves. |

**D-055** | 2026-09-20 | The AI transcript used to live in `Home.tsx` as `Record<opportunityId, messages>`, so it survived switching opportunities. With the panel mounted per route, its own state would not. | (a) hold it in component state and accept that it is lost on navigation, (b) lift it to the dashboard layout route and pass it down, (c) keep it in the TanStack Query cache under `["ai-conversation", id]`, appended to by the ask mutation | (c) | (a) silently drops behaviour the app had, and a conversation you lose by glancing at another deal is worse than no conversation. (b) reintroduces exactly the prop-drilled parent state 2.1 exists to remove, and puts a per-opportunity concern in the component that owns none of them. Under (c) the thing that already outlives the route is the thing that holds it: the entry has `staleTime: Infinity` and a `queryFn` that returns an empty transcript, so nothing ever fetches it, and `useAskAi`'s `onMutate`/`onSuccess` append the two turns. It is also what makes the transcript disappear on sign-out for free, since that is when the cache is dropped (D-056). Covered by `keeps the conversation when the reader looks at another opportunity` in `app/OpportunityPanel.test.tsx`. | Y | N — a state-placement choice inside one milestone; no API or scope effect. |

**D-056** | 2026-09-20 | Where the query cache gets dropped so one user's data cannot be read by the next one in the same tab. The obvious place — `queryClient.clear()` in the logout mutation — misbehaves: the observers watching those keys are still mounted for one more render, so removing their queries makes them refetch, and the answers land back in the cache after it was cleared. Measured: a `GET /api/v2/notifications` fired *after* `POST /api/auth/logout`. | (a) `clear()` on logout and accept the trailing refetch, (b) drop everything but the session on the next successful sign-in, when only the auth panel is mounted, (c) gate every query on the session so the observers disable themselves first | (b) | (a) is the version that leaves the previous session's notifications in the cache with a fresh timestamp — the precise failure the clearing was for. (c) makes every query hook take a flag it does not otherwise need, and still loses the race: `enabled` turns false on the next render, not before `removeQueries` runs. Under (b) the removal happens at the one moment nothing is watching those keys, so it cannot start a fetch, and the dashboard mounts afterwards against an empty cache. Logout writes only `session: null`, which is what every view branches on. | Y | N — an implementation detail of the cache introduced in 1.5; no behaviour a user can name changes, and `signs out and clears what was on screen` still asserts the outcome. |

**D-057** | 2026-09-20 | Adding one E2E test (the search click-through, closing D-039) made `08-search.spec.ts`'s last test fail at sign-in. Diagnosis, not guesswork: the suite performed exactly 20 logins before this milestone and `AUTH_RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS` is 20 per 300 seconds per source address (`backend/app/settings.py`, `rate_limit.py`); every Playwright test signs in from 127.0.0.1 and the whole suite runs in ~30 s, so the 21st attempt was refused with 429. The suite had been sitting exactly on the ceiling. | (a) raise the shipped default, (b) raise it for the E2E server only, (c) share one signed-in storage state across tests so the suite logs in once | (b) — `scripts/e2e-server.sh` exports `AUTH_RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS=200` | (a) weakens a security control for every deployment to make a test pass, which brief §9 forbids outright. (c) is a real option and cheaper at scale, but it would have each test inherit another's session and cookies, and several specs are *about* what a particular user is offered — the isolation is the point, not an accident. (b) changes one process's configuration, and the limit itself is still asserted against the real default in `backend/tests/test_rate_limit.py`, which is where that assertion belongs: it is a server rule, not a browser one. Worth stating plainly, since this is the kind of thing that looks like a weakened test later: **the E2E suite never asserted the rate limit** — it was silently consuming the budget, and the next test anyone added would have hit this whoever wrote it. | Y | N — a test-environment configuration fix with a measured cause; the shipped default is untouched. |

**D-058** | 2026-09-20 | `frontend/vitest.config.ts`'s coverage thresholds have been 85/85/85/78 since F.7, held there deliberately (milestones 1.5 and 1.7 both recorded the reason) because `app/Home.tsx` dominated the total and was scheduled for rewrite here. It is now gone, and the suite measures 95.8% lines / 88.3% branches / 94.4% functions. | (a) leave the thresholds where they are, (b) raise them to just under the new measurement | (b) — 93 lines, 93 statements, 92 functions, 85 branches | The repo's own rule is that raising a threshold is how a coverage gain is kept; leaving them at 85 would let the decomposed components lose most of their coverage before the gate noticed. The margin is a few points rather than a fraction so ordinary churn does not fail an unrelated milestone. Floors are never lowered (brief §6.2), so this is a one-way ratchet and the number to beat is now 93. | Y | N — the project's stated practice, applied at the milestone that made it possible. |

**D-059** | 2026-09-20 | Milestone 2.2 puts each view's filter/sort state in the URL, and the dashboard has two of them live at once: the board's `OpportunityViewState` and, in the child route beside it, the open timeline's `TimelineFilters` (the board stays mounted, D-054). Both were reading and writing the whole query string under their own plain field names. They share the field name `q`, so filtering the board by keyword also filtered the open timeline, and writing either state replaced the query string entirely, dropping whatever the other had put there — a timeline being read newest-first silently reverted to oldest-first the moment the board was filtered. | (a) namespace each state's parameters by the entity it belongs to and merge on write, (b) keep plain names and merge only, (c) lift both states into one object owned by the dashboard route, (d) put the timeline's filters in component state again and leave only the board in the URL | (a) | Both halves of (a) are needed and each fixes a different failure: the prefix stops the two states reading each other's fields, the merge stops one write erasing the other's. (b) fixes the clobbering but leaves `q` shared, which is the more confusing of the two because it looks like a feature. (c) puts one component in charge of a parameter set it does not own and re-creates the prop-drilled parent state 2.1 removed. (d) gives up the requirement — a particular reading of a timeline has to be linkable (D-051). The prefix is the entity name a saved view is already filed under (`opportunities.stage`, `timeline.order`), so there is no second vocabulary to keep in sync, and a saved view's stored `filters` keep the plain field names because a view belongs to exactly one entity. Found by a test written to fail first, per brief §6.5: `keeps the board's keyword filter out of the open timeline's query` in `app/DashboardPage.test.tsx` fails against the unprefixed version with a `GET .../timeline?q=northwind` that should never have been sent. | Y — the prefix is one function in `lib/viewState.ts`; changing it changes what old links mean, and no link has shipped yet. | N — a defect found and fixed inside the milestone that introduced it, with a failing test as evidence. |

**D-060** | 2026-09-20 | `docs/FEATURES.md` §1 says a saved view is "personal or shared" and this codebase has no teams, workspaces or tenancy of any kind — the only grouping that exists is per-opportunity sharing (`opportunity_shares`). So what does `is_shared = 1` mean? | (a) visible to every signed-in user, (b) visible only to users who share at least one opportunity with the creator, (c) shareable to named users, a second sharing table mirroring `opportunity_shares` | (a) | A saved view holds filter parameters, not opportunity data: applying someone else's view shows the applier only what they could already read, because every list it feeds is permission-filtered server-side. (c) is the most precise and is a whole feature — a second sharing model, its own routes, its own matrix entries — for a named filter set, which is scope this milestone does not own. (b) sounds safer but is arbitrary: it would make a view appear and disappear as unrelated opportunity shares change, and it still shows the view to people the creator did not choose. What (a) does expose is the view's **name**, to everyone signed in — a name like "Acme renewal push" is a small disclosure, and the reason this entry is flagged for review rather than just recorded. Private is the default (`is_shared` defaults to 0); sharing is an explicit checkbox. | Y — visibility is one clause in one SQL query (`routes_saved_views.list_saved_views`). | **Y** — it defines what "shared" means in a product with no tenancy model, and it means a view name is visible to every user of the installation. If that is wrong, the alternative is (c), and it should be settled before Phase 6 builds dashboards on the same idea. |

**D-061** | 2026-09-20 | Where the board's filtering and sorting run. The opportunity list is `GET /api/opportunities`, which returns every opportunity the caller can read in one unpaginated response. | (a) filter and sort in the client over the list already fetched, (b) add `stage`/`q`/`sort` query parameters to the v1 and v2 opportunity list endpoints | (a) | There is no payload to save: the rows are already in the cache, so (b) would send the same data back in a different order and add a round trip to every filter press — the opposite of the "< 150 ms with data cached" acceptance line. It would also have to be mirrored onto `/api/v2/opportunities` to avoid the drift `CLAUDE.md`'s "API versions" section exists to prevent, so the cost is two implementations of a predicate, not one. The client-side version is `applyOpportunityView` in `lib/domain.ts`, one pure function with its own unit tests. This flips when the list is paginated (milestone 2.3, and the 400-opportunity scale it is written for): a filter over one page is not a filter, so the predicate moves into SQL at the same time the pagination does. Recorded here so 2.3 treats that as planned rather than as a regression it discovered. | Y — one function, and 2.3 is expected to replace it. | N — an implementation placement inside one milestone, with the condition that reverses it named. |

**D-062** | 2026-09-20 | How a saved view stores what it saved. Every filter the board or the timeline can apply is a query-string field, and both view states are flat `Record<string, string>`. | (a) `filters`/`sort` as opaque JSON text columns validated as bounded key/value strings, (b) a typed column per filter (`stage`, `q`, `from`, `to`, ...), (c) a key/value child table | (a) | (b) makes every new filter a migration, and the next three milestones all add filters — 2.6 adds entry types, 2.8 adds the table and calendar views, and Phase 6 adds custom fields whose names are not known at schema-design time. (c) is (a) with joins. The cost of (a) is that the database cannot validate a filter name, which is the right trade here because the *application* cannot either: `stateFromParams` falls back to the default for any field it does not recognise, so an unknown key from an old saved view is ignored rather than fatal. Validation at the boundary is a 2,000-character cap on the serialized JSON (`schemas.MAX_VIEW_PARAMS_JSON_LENGTH`), which bounds one client's storage without interpreting the content. `group_by` is a plain column because grouping is one named dimension rather than an open set. | Y — a column type, with no data shipped yet. | N — follows the roadmap's own statement that the five views share one filter model; the schema just declines to re-declare it. |

**D-063** | 2026-09-20 | D-061 (recorded in 2.2) predicted that "this flips when the list is paginated (milestone 2.3 ...): a filter over one page is not a filter, so the predicate moves into SQL at the same time the pagination does." But `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s actual Phase 2 technical-work line for 2.3 is one sentence: "`VirtualList` — 400 opportunities will not render unvirtualized," which `PROGRESS.md` carried over verbatim per F.1's "preserve roadmap wording" instruction. Nothing in the roadmap's 2.3 line, or anywhere else in Phase 2, asks for the opportunity list to move onto the already-existing paginated `GET /api/v2/opportunities` (`routes_opportunities.py:150`, keyset-paginated since milestone 1.3). Milestone 2.9, by contrast, explicitly says "move the frontend onto v2" as its own line item. So: does 2.3 mean *only* windowed DOM rendering over the list `useOpportunities` already fetches whole, or does it also mean switching the data source to v2 pagination and moving `applyOpportunityView`'s predicate into SQL, as D-061 assumed? | (a) implement 2.3 exactly as ROADMAP.md scopes it — client-side windowed rendering (`VirtualList`) over the existing unpaginated `useOpportunities` fetch, leaving the v2 pagination switch to 2.9, (b) fold D-061's anticipated pagination-and-SQL-filter move into 2.3 now, ahead of 2.9 | (a) | Brief §5 is explicit: "do not pull work forward from later phases or add unaccepted scope," and ROADMAP.md outranks a DECISIONS.md entry in the source-of-truth hierarchy (brief §1) — D-061 is a rationale record for a 2.2 implementation choice, not a scope grant for 2.3. (b) would mean 2.9 arrives at "move the frontend onto v2" with the opportunity list already moved, silently shrinking what 2.9 actually has left to do and coupling two milestones' gates together; it would also require duplicating `applyOpportunityView`'s predicate into SQL against both `/api/opportunities` and `/api/v2/opportunities` (`CLAUDE.md`'s "API versions" drift concern) for a v1 surface 2.9 is about to delete anyway. `VirtualList` windows *rendered DOM nodes*, not *fetched rows* — it is a legitimate, self-contained fix for "400 opportunities will not render unvirtualized" against data however it arrives, so nothing about (a) is deferred or broken by not pagin­ating yet. D-061's own text already named the reversal condition ("this flips when the list is paginated"); this entry is that flip being exercised in 2.9's direction rather than 2.3's, not a reversal of D-061 itself. | Y — 2.3 ships independent of where 2.9 gets its data. | N — a scope-boundary read between two already-planned milestones, not new scope; flagged here so 2.9's plan (not yet started) accounts for both the v2 switch and the SQL-predicate move D-061 anticipated. |

The order follows the dependencies rather than the sentence: 2.4c's action bar reports per-item failures through the toast surface 2.4b builds, and `x` (toggle selection) is already in `docs/UX.md`'s keyboard map, so the map exists before the thing it selects. **D-065** | 2026-09-20 | `docs/UX.md`'s keyboard map has eleven rows and two of them point at things this build does not have: `g` then `t` goes to **Tasks**, which is Phase 4 (`docs/ROADMAP.md`), and `x` toggles **bulk selection**, which is 2.4c per D-064. Publishing the map on a `?` screen makes this a user-facing promise. | (a) publish the full map and leave the unbuilt keys inert, (b) publish the full map with the unbuilt keys marked "coming", (c) publish only what is bound, and let the map grow with the milestones that bind the rest | (c), with `g p` bound to the dashboard and described as "the pipeline board, which is the dashboard today", and one addition: `g s` for search | A reference screen is a contract. (a) teaches a key that does nothing, which is worse than not mentioning it — the reader concludes the feature is broken rather than absent. (b) turns the shortcut reference into a roadmap, which is not what a person presses `?` to read. Under (c) the map is a single array in `lib/keyboard.ts`, `Chord` is a union type derived from it, and the views bind `Partial<Record<Chord, () => void>>` — so a key documented but never bound, or bound but never documented, is a **type error** rather than something a user discovers. 2.4c adds `x` to that array in the same commit that makes it work; Phase 4 adds `g t`. The `g s` addition is the one place this goes beyond `docs/UX.md`: `/search` is a top-level route with no keyboard route to it, and a "go to" prefix that cannot reach one of three screens is a gap, not a simplification. | Y — one array. | N — it keeps a published map truthful, which is what `docs/UX.md` asks for; the single addition is one binding to a route that already exists. |

**D-066** | 2026-09-20 | Found by the E2E journey, not by reasoning: `Modal` returned focus to whatever was focused before, on unmount. The `10-keyboard.spec.ts` snapshot shows exactly that: `textbox "Account" [active]: cPricing call brexq1`. | (a) have the palette pass a ref the modal reads at cleanup to suppress the return, (b) make focus return part of *dismissing* — Esc, the backdrop, the Close button — rather than of unmounting, so a dialog that closes because it moved the page simply calls `onClose`, (c) focus the destination explicitly after each navigating command | (b) | (a) works and was written first; it was withdrawn because the React Compiler lint (`react-hooks/refs`) correctly objects to a ref flowing through render, and because "restore focus unless a flag says otherwise" describes the mechanism rather than the rule. (c) is the most correct for a screen reader and is the thing R.2 (focus management) should do for every route change, not something the palette should special-case; with (b) focus falls to the document, so the next Tab starts at the top of the screen the reader is actually on. | Y | N — a defect found and fixed inside the milestone that introduced it, with the failing journey as evidence. |

**D-067** | 2026-09-20 | Milestone 2.4b needs "restore exactly what this delete took" (`docs/FEATURES.md` §12: undo is "a real `deleted_at = NULL`, not a re-create"). A cascade stamps one `deleted_at` value across the tree it removes, so the stamp identifies the batch — except that `utc_now()` is second-resolution by design (`CLAUDE.md`, Timestamps), so a note deleted on its own within the same second as its entry carries the same stamp and would be restored with it. Found by writing the test first: `test_restoring_an_entry_brings_back_the_notes_that_delete_took` failed because the API calls were microseconds apart. | (a) accept the one-second ambiguity and scope the restore by stamp equality, (b) give `deleted_at` sub-second precision, (c) add a per-delete batch token column and a migration to carry it, (d) have each cascade query for a colliding stamp and advance a second when it finds one | (a) | (b) introduces a second timestamp format into a schema whose single canonical format is documented as load bearing — every comparison here is lexicographic on TEXT — for a sub-second edge case. (c) is a migration and a new column for the same. (d) is exact and costs an extra query plus a loop in all four cascade helpers, which is defensiveness priced above the failure it prevents: the failure is that a row the user deleted a moment earlier comes back with the undo, in full view, and can be deleted again. Nothing is lost, nothing leaks, and no permission changes. Under (a) the restore helpers take the stamp as an argument, which keeps "restore this delete" explicit at the call site, and the tests backdate a prior delete so the fixture means what it says rather than hiding the window. | Y — (d) is a contained change to `soft_delete.py` if a real report ever arrives. | N — a proportionality call inside one milestone, with the limitation documented in `CLAUDE.md` and in the test that meets it. |

**D-068** | 2026-09-20 | `docs/UX.md` says "every mutation applies immediately, reconciles on response, and rolls back with an explanatory toast on failure". Taken literally that includes creates, and a create cannot be rendered before the server answers without inventing an id, an author name and a `created_at` for the row — then reconciling the guess. | (a) optimistic everywhere, with fabricated placeholder rows for creates, (b) optimistic where the cache already holds what changes — the four deletes and the note edit — and server-confirmed for creates and for the opportunity PATCH | (b) | A delete removes a row the cache already has; a note edit replaces a body the reader just typed. Both are predictions the client can make truthfully, and both roll back from a snapshot. A create is not: the id is the server's, and `POST /api/opportunities` also normalizes `value` and can write a system timeline entry, so the optimistic render would be contradicted by the response often enough to read as a flicker rather than as speed. The opportunity PATCH is left out for the same reason — it normalizes `value` and writes a stage-change entry. What the reader actually waits on is a form submit they are already looking at, not a list they are scanning. | Y — each mutation is independent; adding `onMutate` to a create later is a local change. | N — it implements the milestone's stated deliverable and names what it deliberately leaves server-confirmed. |

Under (c) `lib/confirm.ts` keeps exactly two callers and says in its docstring why each one is there, so 2.5's real dialog still replaces a single call site. | Y | N — it applies the rule `docs/UX.md` states to the one control the rule does not mention, and documents the reason at the call site. |

**D-071** | 2026-09-20 | D-068 kept `useDeleteOpportunity` and three siblings optimistic because the cache already holds the row being changed. `useBulkOpportunityAction` (2.4c) sends many ids in one request and the response reports each one's outcome independently — some may succeed and some fail, for reasons the client cannot always predict client-side (an id whose owner changed between selection and submit, D-070's per-item design exists exactly for this). | (a) optimistic per selected id, rolling back only the ones the response marks as errors, (b) server-confirmed: render nothing until `results` comes back, then apply the successes and report the failures in one toast | (b) | (a) is not simply "D-068 applied N times" — it requires holding a snapshot per id and reconciling a response whose failures are not knowable in advance the way a single delete's is (that one either 404s as a whole or succeeds as a whole). Predicting which of several ids will fail is a guess, not a truthful render, which is the same objection D-068 raised against optimistic creates. Under (b) the action bar's controls disable via `canManageOpportunity` when the selection includes anything the reader cannot manage (mirroring the per-card Edit/Delete gate), so the ordinary path never shows a failure at all — the per-item error branch exists for the same reason the endpoint reports per-item rather than failing wholesale: a stale client, not a step in the golden path. Undo is still offered on a bulk delete's successes, via the existing single-item restore route called once per id, so the reversal `docs/UX.md` asks for is not lost to being server-confirmed. | Y — the mutation is one function; adding per-id optimism later does not touch the endpoint. | N — applies D-068's own reasoning to a shape D-068 did not cover, rather than deciding something new. |

**D-072** | 2026-09-20 | Milestone 2.5's token work changed where cards sit on the board, and eight E2E journeys went red on the same step: clicking a card's "Timeline" link no longer navigated. Instrumenting the page showed why, and it is not a test artefact. `article.opportunity-card` has `onFocus={() => onFocus(opportunity)}` and React's `onFocus` is `focusin`, so focusing *any control inside* the card moves the board's keyboard cursor (`focusedId`). `StageBoard` feeds that cursor to `VirtualList` as `activeIndex`, whose effect calls `virtualizer.scrollToIndex` — so the mousedown that focused the link scrolled the column, and the mouseup 1 ms later landed on `.card-actions` instead. The click event fired on the parent and no navigation happened. A reader clicking a card low in a scrolled column loses the click the same way. | (a) treat it as an E2E timing problem and scroll each card into view in the helper before clicking, (b) scroll the column only while the cursor is being moved by the keyboard, (c) stop the card's `onFocus` from setting the cursor at all and set it only from `j`/`k` | (b) | (a) was written first and does not survive its own evidence: the mousedown/mouseup trace shows the application moving content out from under a pointer, which is a defect whether or not a test observes it, and brief §6.5 forbids reaching green by working around the application. (c) loses something real — a card focused by Tab or by a click *is* where the reader is, and the cursor should follow it, so `e` and `x` act on what they are looking at. Under (b) `DashboardPage` keeps one `focusedId` and one `followCursor` flag beside it: `j`/`k` set it true because windowing means the row they move to may not be mounted at all and the list has to go and get it; the card's own `onFocus` sets it false, because a card the reader clicked is already on screen. `StageBoard` passes `activeIndex` only when the flag is set. The evidence is the suite itself: eight journeys failed before the change and pass after it, with nothing else altered. | Y — one boolean and one prop. | N — a defect found inside the milestone that exposed it, with a mousedown/mouseup trace as evidence rather than a guess. |

The tokens are the part that *is* shared now — every component in the app reads them today, which is what makes the later wrappers cheap. `DropdownMenu` is the obvious next one (`NotificationMenu` and `SavedViewsMenu` are hand-rolled poppers), and it belongs to whichever milestone next touches those. | Y — additive, one primitive at a time. | N — it builds what the milestone names and declines to build what it does not. |

**D-074** | 2026-09-20 | D-003 (accepted) requires light and dark to ship together. Two mechanisms can carry a dark palette: a `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` block in the stylesheet, or a resolved attribute on `<html>` that JavaScript writes. `docs/DESIGN-SYSTEM.md` asks for parity and for re-running the contrast audit on the dark palette, and says nothing about who chooses. | (a) media query only — the system decides and a reader cannot override it, (b) attribute only, resolved in JS from a stored preference and `matchMedia`, (c) both — the media query as the default and an attribute that overrides it | (b), with a three-way control (System / Light / Dark) in the header, stored in `localStorage` | (c) is the usual answer and costs a duplicated dark block: the same twenty-odd role tokens written once under `[data-theme="dark"]` and again inside the media query, because a media query cannot be added to a selector list. Two copies of a palette drift, and the contrast test would then have to know which one is authoritative. Under (b) there is exactly one dark block, `lib/theme.ts` decides which palette is in force, and `lib/contrast.test.ts` reads that block straight out of the stylesheet. The cost is that dark depends on JavaScript — acceptable here and nowhere else in this app's shape: `output: "export"` renders an empty shell and `AppRoot` renders nothing until it is in a browser (ADR 0005), so there is no server-rendered content to flash the wrong colour. The three-way control follows from the same place: once a reader can choose, "follow my system" has to stay reachable, which a two-state toggle cannot express. `prefers-color-scheme` is still the input for the default, and the E2E suite audits both palettes through Playwright's `emulateMedia`, so the real media query is what the tests drive. | Y — a media block can be added later without moving the tokens. | N — it implements an accepted criterion and picks the mechanism that keeps one copy of the palette. |

**D-091** | 2026-09-23 | The milestone-2.8 review returned nine passes, with one observation on check v-02: *"when I signed in as Casey, the table view (a view I just created privately for Alex) was the default view for Casey even if it is not a shared view."* Investigated before recording, because a private view reaching another user would be a serious privacy defect. **It is not that.** Verified against the running app: Alex's saved-view list returns `id=4 Table Negotiation (shared=False)` and Casey's does not, so `is_shared` is honoured server-side. (c) misreads the mechanism — the state is in the URL by design, not in storage, and moving it would undo 2.2's linkable-view property. (b) keeps that property and closes the handover. **D-092** | 2026-09-23 | Implementing D-091's fix (**milestone 2.9a**): both sign-out call sites — `app/TopBar.tsx`'s button and `app/CommandPalette.tsx`'s command — need to navigate to a clean URL once the session is gone. The natural first draft was `logout.mutate(undefined, { onSuccess: () => navigate(...) })`, mirroring the shape already used elsewhere for a fire-and-forget mutation. Written that way for the palette, it silently never navigated: confirmed with a debug harness driving the real route tree through a `useLocation()` spy, the URL was unchanged after sign-out despite the session having cleared, and instrumenting the passed `onSuccess` showed it was never called. Cause: `CommandPalette`'s "Sign out" command calls `onClose()` — which unmounts the palette — *before* calling `logout.mutate(...)`, so by the time the mutation's promise resolves the mutation observer that would have delivered the per-call `onSuccess` has no listeners left. `CLAUDE.md` already documents this exact class of bug for `app/Toasts.tsx`'s undo flow ("TanStack Query drops `mutate(vars, { onSuccess })` once the observer has no listeners"), which is the give-away that this was a known shape, not a new one. The same `mutate(undefined, { onSuccess })` in `TopBar.tsx` happened to work when tested in isolation, because nothing there unmounts the button *before* its own `onSuccess` fires — the unmount is *caused by* that handler flipping the session to null, not a race against it — but that ordering is incidental to why the top bar's button exists, not a guarantee anything about the code states. | (a) leave `TopBar`'s working `mutate` + `onSuccess` alone and fix only the palette, using some other mechanism there (e.g. a `useEffect` watching `logout.isSuccess`), (b) switch both call sites to `mutateAsync()` and `.then(...)`, the pattern `app/Toasts.tsx` already uses for the identical reason, (c) move the navigation into `useLogout` itself, coupling `lib/queries.ts` to `react-router` | (b) | (a) leaves two different mechanisms doing the same job for the same reason with no test able to tell which one is load-bearing — exactly the kind of drift `CLAUDE.md`'s "one editing model" reasoning (D-087's own entry) warns against, and the top bar's version stays one incidental timing change away from silently breaking the same way the palette's did. A `useEffect` in (a) also fires on *every* render where `isSuccess` is true, including a re-render after the effect has already run, unless guarded — solving a problem `mutateAsync` does not have. (c) fixes it in one place but makes the framework-agnostic query layer `CLAUDE.md`'s "Server state does not live in components" section describes depend on the router, for a call two components already make through `useNavigate()`; navigation is a routing concern and belongs in `app/`, where the two call sites already are. (b) is one line at each of the two sites, uses the mechanism this codebase already established for the same failure mode, and cannot regress silently: both are covered by a test that signs out and back in and checks what rendered. | Y — a one-line change at each of two call sites, reversible without touching `lib/queries.ts` or the mutation's own definition. | N — applies an already-accepted pattern from this codebase to a newly found instance of the same bug; no new mechanism, no scope change. |

---

**D-093** | 2026-09-23 | Where the board's filter and sort run, now that milestone 2.9 has moved the frontend onto `/api/v2/opportunities` and that endpoint is keyset-paginated. D-061 (2.2) predicted this would flip — "a filter over one page is not a filter, so the predicate moves into SQL at the same time the pagination does" — and D-063 (2.3) deferred the flip to this milestone. In between, milestones 2.7 and 2.8 built four views over one dataset, and `CLAUDE.md`'s "Four views over one list" records what they derive from it: `viewOrder` (the `j`/`k` cursor and shift-click ranges), `checkedOpportunities` (the bulk selection, which is *everything filtered in*, not everything drawn), and the calendar's month, which places `expected_close_date` across a whole month of a whole list. None of those three is a window. | (a) move `applyOpportunityView`'s predicate into SQL and have the board read pages lazily, as D-061 anticipated, (b) keep the predicate in the client and have `useOpportunities` walk `next_cursor` to the last page, so the cache holds the same whole list v1 returned, (c) leave the board on a single first page of 200 | (b) | (c) is wrong at the 400-opportunity scale the roadmap names for 2.3: the 201st deal would silently not exist, in the selection, in the calendar and in search-by-filter. (a) is what D-061 imagined, and the four-view work since has changed what it costs: the bulk selection and the calendar month would each need their own server round trip with their own predicate, `sortField` would need a server-side equivalent for every column `opportunityColumns` offers, and switching renderer — currently free, and held to "< 150 ms with data cached" by this phase's own acceptance line — would become a fetch. That is a redesign of the view layer, and ROADMAP.md's 2.9 line is "finish the `/api/v2` migration and remove v1", which brief §5 says not to exceed. (b) is nine lines (`fetchAll` in `lib/queries.ts`), preserves every behaviour the four views were built on, and is honest about what the client actually needs: the whole readable set. D-061's premise still holds — it says the predicate moves when *the client holds one page*, and under (b) it does not. Cost is two or three requests instead of one at 400 rows, measured against `MAX_LIMIT = 200`. Four reads go through it: the opportunity list, a timeline, a share list and an audit log. The inbox and the saved-view menu keep asking for one explicit page, because both are read from the top. | Y — `fetchAll` is one function, and (a) remains open as a later change. | **Y** — it declines work D-061 and D-063 both pointed at this milestone. If the human wants server-side filtering, it is a view-layer milestone of its own and Phase R's performance slice (brief §8, "large-dataset budgets met") is where it would sit; nothing fails a gate either way, and the 400-row budget is met by the walk. |

**D-094** | 2026-09-23 | `GET /api/health` when v1 is removed. Every other `/api/*` path moved under `/api/v2`; health is the one that did not have to. | (a) move it to `/api/v2/health`, (b) leave it at `/api/health`, outside the version boundary | (b) | Health is not a client surface. It is read by the container's `HEALTHCHECK`, by `scripts/docker-smoke.sh`, by `scripts/autopilot-gate.sh` and by whatever watches the process in production (ADR 0011), and none of those is a thing a version boundary exists to migrate — a probe that moves when the *client* API changes shape is a probe that can break a deployment for a reason unrelated to deployment. Phase R adds `/healthz` and `/readyz` beside it (brief §8), which are also unversioned, so (b) is the shape that section already assumes. The consequence recorded here so it is not read as an oversight: `grep '"/api/' backend/app` returns three things rather than only v2 routes — health, the catch-all in `main.py`, and a docstring. | Y | N — an unversioned operations endpoint is the ordinary convention, and it is where it already was. |

**D-095** | 2026-09-23 | The `notifications.read` column after v1's removal. `read_at` has been the source of truth since revision `20260920_0003_notification_history`; the boolean `read` stayed so v1's unread-only list could keep reading it. With that route gone, `notification_service` still writes `read` and nothing reads it. | (a) drop the column in a new Alembic revision, (b) keep writing it and record that nothing reads it | (b) | (a) is the tidier end state and the wrong trade here: it is a migration, an `expected_schema.sql` update and a destructive schema change (SQLite's `DROP COLUMN` discards the data) in a milestone whose subject is the HTTP surface, for a column that costs one assignment per mark-read. Keeping the write also keeps the column truthful for anyone reading the database directly — `make backup` snapshots and the audit-minded operator ADR 0011 is written for. Recorded rather than left silent so the next person to open `notification_service.mark_notification_read` finds the reason instead of a puzzle; the natural place to drop it is Phase 5, whose digest batching (`grouped_key`) already touches this table. | Y | N — reversible, invisible to clients, and the alternative is a schema change outside this milestone's scope. |

**D-096** | 2026-09-23 | Who may read an edit history. Milestone 2.10 says owner-only, "matching the audit log's existing rule", but one history route already shipped — `GET /api/v2/notes/{note_id}/versions`, open to every role that can read the opportunity (`require_note_access`, and the matrix entry that pinned it at 200 for a viewer). 2.10 gives all three a screen, so the three now answer the same question and had two different answers. | (a) owner-only for the two new routes, leaving notes as it shipped, (b) owner-only for all three, tightening the note route, (c) any-role for all three, widening to match the shipped one | (b) | Under (a) one screen behaves three ways: an editor sees History on a note and not on the entry the note hangs under, with no rule a user could state. (c) is the reading with the most existing code behind it and the wrong one for what a history *is* — it answers "who changed this and what did it say before", which is the audit log's question, and the audit log is owner-only for a reason that does not stop applying because the subject is a note. The milestone also names it outright and says a wider audience is "a separate decision", so widening here would be deciding it by default. The cost of (b) is a tightening of a live v2 route: it is reachable today only by curl — the frontend had zero references to versions before this milestone, which is the gap 2.10 exists to close — so no client breaks, and the matrix entry moves from 200 to 403 for viewer and editor, which the suite proves rather than assumes. Worth naming the one thing it gives up: a note's own author, if they are not the owner, can edit their note and cannot read what they replaced. That is the same asymmetry the audit log already has and the milestone's "owner or admin" wording accepts; if it turns out to be wrong, widening is one line per route and no migration. | Y — three `require_opportunity_owner` calls; widening later changes no data. | Y — it tightens an endpoint that shipped in Phase F and was accepted at that gate. Nothing consumed it, but the human should confirm that owner-only is the audience they meant, particularly for a note whose author is an editor rather than the owner. |
