**D-097** | 2026-09-23 | What the revision does about rows that already exist. `note_versions` has been written on every note write since the baseline, so a note's history is complete. Opportunities and timeline entries have none: every edit before this revision overwrote in place, and those values are gone. | (a) backfill version 1 for every existing row from its current values, (b) no backfill — history starts empty and the first edit after the upgrade is version 1, (c) backfill and mark the backfilled rows with a `system_backfilled` flag so the UI can label them | (a) | (b) fails at exactly the case the milestone was written for: on a database that predates the revision, the first edit would record the *new* value with nothing to read it against, so "485,000 became 250,000" would still show only 250,000 — one version, no before. A baseline is what makes the second version legible. Against (c): the flag buys one sentence of labelling and costs a column on two tables, a schema entry, and a branch in the dialog, for an inaccuracy that is bounded and stated — version 1 is attributed to the row's owner or author and stamped with its `created_at`, which is exactly true for a row never edited and, for one edited earlier, is the only attribution that still exists. The revision's docstring says so where the code is. The seeded and test databases are built by driving the API, so every row in them has an honest version 1 regardless. | N — the rows are written; removing them again would be another revision (and dropping them would leave (b)'s gap). | Y — it writes rows that assert something slightly stronger than the database can prove, for existing installations. The alternative was worse, and the limitation is recorded in the revision and in `CLAUDE.md`; the human should confirm they would rather have an imperfect baseline than none. |

---

**D-098** | 2026-09-23 | Which opportunity fields become editable in place (milestone 2.11). The record has six writable fields — name, account, value, currency, stage, close date — and `AGENTS.md` v2 scope item 12 says content is edited where it is read, without saying which fields are "content". | (a) all six, including currency, (b) the five a reader scans and acts on, leaving currency to the create/edit dialog, (c) stage only, since it is the one the board already changes | (b) | Currency is read as part of the value, not as a field of its own: `formatMoney` prints "USD 185,000" as one number, and there is no column, cell or heading fact that is *the currency*. Making it separately editable would mean either splitting the money cell into two controls — two inputs in one table cell, for a value nobody changes after the deal is created — or editing it somewhere it is not read, which is the thing this milestone removes. It stays in the dialog with everything else the form carries, so nothing is unreachable. (a) costs a worse money cell for a field with no reader; (c) would leave the milestone claiming "opportunity fields" and delivering the one that was already done in 2.7. | Y — adding currency later is one more entry in `editableOpportunityFields` and one more cell branch. | N — a scoping call inside a milestone the human already specified, resolved toward the smaller surface per brief §9 ("no features beyond what is specified"). |

**D-099** | 2026-09-23 | Which *surfaces* get inline editing. An opportunity's fields are read in five places: the board card, the list row, the table cell, the calendar chip, and the opportunity's own heading above its timeline. | (a) every surface that draws a field, (b) the table grid and the opportunity heading, leaving the cards to the dialog `e` and Edit already open, (c) the table only, which is the one `docs/FEATURES.md` §1 names | (b) | A field is edited where it is read *as a field*. The table is a grid of fields — `docs/UX.md`'s view table gives it "inline edit" as its primary interaction — and the heading is where `docs/UX.md` asks for "the facts a rep needs constantly: value, stage, close date", which is precisely a set of fields displayed beside an Edit button that opened a form elsewhere. A board card and a list row read a *deal*, not a field list: an edit affordance on every value there is a control on every line of every card, which is what `AGENTS.md`'s Design bar rules out ("nothing on screen that does not earn its place"), and both already open the dialog from `e` and from Edit. (c) would leave the timeline — where the complaint in D-051 was actually made — untouched. One consequence recorded rather than discovered later: the table's **name** cell is not editable either, because it is the row's link to its timeline and a link inside a button is not a control anyone can operate; the name is edited on the heading that link leads to. | Y — every surface draws through `OpportunityField`, so adding one is a wrapper, not a mechanism. | N — it narrows rather than expands scope, and the narrowing is `AGENTS.md`'s own Design bar. |

**D-100** | 2026-09-23 | How an inline editor's trigger is named for assistive technology. The first implementation gave it `aria-label="Edit Value"`, which is how every other icon-or-value control in this app is labelled. | (a) `aria-label="Edit <field>"`, (b) accessible name from the value itself, with the action as the `title` description, (c) the value plus a visually-hidden " — edit <field>" suffix | (b) | The trigger sits inside the opportunity's `<h2>`, and an `aria-label` on a descendant overrides name-from-content for the ancestor that computes its name from it: with (a) the opportunity's heading was named "Edit Opportunity" rather than the opportunity, which `e2e/helpers.ts`'s `openTimeline` failed on immediately — the accessible name of the heading was gone, not merely odd. (b) names the control what it holds, which is what a reader needs first, and announces what it does as its description; it is also the pattern the same control takes in other tools. (c) keeps the value but pollutes the heading's name with the affordance's wording. A second consequence worth keeping: tests address the trigger by `getByTitle("Edit Account")` rather than by its value, so a selector does not stop matching the moment the value it names is edited — `CLAUDE.md`'s standing Playwright rule. | Y | N — an implementation detail, settled by a test failure rather than by preference. |

## Human rulings — 2026-09-23 (Phase 2 gate, three of the seven open decisions)

Ruled while the reviewer was still working through the rest of the packet.

**D-093 — board filtering stayed client-side. RULED: schedule it for Phase R.**
Not merely deferred: written into milestone **R.3**, which owns the large-dataset
budgets, so it is measured against R.7's 800k-row seed rather than assumed. The
reason it cannot stay as it is: filtering one page of a keyset-paginated list is
not filtering — a row matching on page nine returns nothing while the client
holds page one. Correct today at 400 opportunities, wrong at the confirmed scale.

**D-097 — backfilled history baselines are approximate. RULED: accept as shipped,
no `system_backfilled` flag.** The reviewer's reasoning: *"there's not real
historical data, only trial data."* That is exactly right for the situation, and
worth writing down because the reasoning is situational rather than permanent.
The backfill only ever touches rows that exist when the migration runs. A fresh
production database upgrades across the revision with nothing to backfill, so no
approximate rows are ever created there. The approximate baselines exist only in
the current trial database. **The one case that would revive this:** migrating
the trial database into production, or upgrading any database that already holds
real edits made before this revision. If either becomes the plan, revisit — the
flag is cheap now and impossible to reconstruct later.

**D-096 — edit history reads tightened to owner-only, including notes. RULED:
accept.** Same reasoning: no real historical data, so nothing is lost by
narrowing. Noting the direction for the record — narrowing an already-shipped
read is the hard direction to reverse in general, but here there is no audience
that had come to rely on it. Widening later, if collaborators turn out to want
history as context rather than the owner wanting it as forensics, is a one-line
change to the permission dependency.

---

## Human rulings — 2026-09-23 (Phase 2 gate, remaining four decisions)

All seven decisions from the Phase 2 packet are now ruled. D-093, D-096 and
D-097 are recorded above. The remaining four, all **agreed as recommended**:

**D-070 — three of five bulk actions unbuilt. ACCEPTED**, with the reviewer
asking where the rest go. Scheduled as milestone **3.0a**. Phase 3 rather than
Phase 2 because each needs a capability that does not exist as a single-item
operation — ownership transfer, view membership, multi-record export — and
ownership transfer in particular carries a permission question (may an editor
reassign? does the previous owner keep access?) that belongs with Phase 3's
collaboration work rather than being decided inside a bulk-action wrapper.

**D-078 — icons on all eleven entry types. ACCEPTED**, no further work.

**D-088 — calendar shows close dates only. ACCEPTED**, with the reviewer asking
the effort for the endpoint that would add follow-ups. Scheduled as **3.0b**.
Sized: one new read — follow-up entries in a date range, permission-filtered,
paginated like the rest of v2 — plus its authorization-matrix entry, its tests
and the calendar's second layer. Comparable to the search endpoint in shape but
smaller, since there is no full-text machinery: the data is a column on
`timeline_entries` that nothing currently returns across opportunities. Not a
milestone on its own in a later phase, which is why it is bundled at the front
of Phase 3 rather than deferred to Phase R.

**D-090 — the table is not a true ARIA grid. ACCEPTED**, with the reviewer
asking me to record the consequence. **The note, for the record:** a
screen-reader user navigating the table view hears a list of rows, not a grid.
They cannot use table navigation commands, cannot move cell-by-cell along a
row, and do not hear "column 3 of 5" style position announcements. Each cell
does carry its column name in visually-hidden text, so no information is lost —
it is slower to traverse, not unusable, and `axe` passes because the markup
never claims to be a grid it is not. The cost lands entirely on assistive-
technology users, which is why it is written down rather than left implicit.
The trigger for revisiting: if OppTrail ever needs a genuine data grid — editable
cells, column resizing, keyboard cell navigation — this is the decision that has
to be reopened first, and doing it then means either plumbing `role="presentation"`
and `aria-rowindex` through `VirtualList` or dropping virtualization for the
table. Phase R's accessibility pass (R.2) should confirm the hidden column
labels actually read well in a real screen reader rather than only satisfying
`axe`.

---

**D-101** | 2026-09-23 | *(Recorded at the Phase 2 gate as a second D-098 — the
number was already taken by the 2.11 currency scoping above. Renumbered here at the
start of Phase 3, before anything cited it, so the milestone and the code point at
one entry.)* Raised by the human at the Phase 2 gate, from check g-01: *"the history for each item, note, comment etc is separated. I want one history log, ideally combined with audit log... one place where the whole history of every event to the opportunity resides so people can see who did what when. It should reside at Opportunity level."* The same note asked whether auditable events are defined to exclude some, having seen no audit trail for edits just made. | Investigated before answering. **Yes, deliberately, and nothing is missing.** Three mechanisms record history and none of them meet: system timeline entries (opportunity created, stage changed, sharing changed), audit log rows (the seven actions in `audit_utils` — four deletions, `share_removed`, `share_role_changed`, `is_shared`), and version rows (every edit, in the three `*_versions` tables). `AGENTS.md` invariant 7 scopes the audit log to deletions and sharing; invariant 8 put edits in version tables. Verified against the running app: opportunity 2 has **zero** audit rows while carrying 1 opportunity-version and 14 entry-version rows — exactly as designed, and exactly as confusing as the reviewer found it. | (a) widen the audit log to record edits too, (b) build one merged view over all three sources, (c) leave it | (b), scheduled as milestone **3.0** | (a) would duplicate in the audit log what version tables already hold better, and invariant 7's scope is deliberate — the audit log is owner-only forensics about destruction and access, not a change feed. (c) leaves a product with three partial histories and no answer to "who did what when", which is the question a sales team actually asks. (b) is a read-side join over data that already exists, and needs no schema change. **The real design question it carries:** the audit half is owner-only (D-096, ruled this same session) while system entries are visible to anyone with access, so a merged view either filters per row by role or reopens who-sees-what. That belongs in the milestone, not in this entry. | Y — a new read-only view. | N — the human asked for it directly; the investigation answered the question inside it. |

---

## Phase 3 — milestone 3.0 (one activity log)

**D-102** | 2026-09-23 | Who sees what in the merged activity log — the design question D-101 handed to this milestone. The audit half is owner-only (`AGENTS.md` invariant 7) and the version half is owner-only (D-096), while system timeline entries are visible to anyone with access. A view that merges all three has to answer for each row. | (a) owner-only in full, so the whole feature is one role's, (b) filter per row by the rule of the source it came from, (c) widen: show every reader with access that an edit happened, since a note already carries a visible "edited" marker (invariant 3) and the feed reports the *occurrence* of an edit rather than the snapshot D-096 restricts | (b) | (b) changes no existing permission rule, which is what brief §9 asks of an agent between gates: the route is `require_opportunity_access` and the *rows* are what the role decides, so an editor asking what happened is answered with what they may read rather than refused. (a) would make the answer to the reviewer's question — "so people can see who did what when" — available to one person per opportunity, and it would be the only read in the app where a shared colleague is refused something they can already see on the timeline. (c) is the better product and is genuinely defensible — the fact of an edit is already public, only the prior values are not — but widening who sees an edit trail is a product decision with no way back once someone has read it, so it belongs to the human rather than to this milestone. The cost of (b) meanwhile is bounded and visible: an editor's activity log is the system entries, which is a smaller answer than an owner's, not a wrong one. | Y — (c) is a one-line change to `fetch_activity_page` plus its matrix row and tests. | **Y** — recommend (c) for a later milestone if the reviewer wants a shared deal's collaborators to see each other's edits, which is Phase 3's stated goal ("make two people on one deal see each other"). (b) ships now either way. |

**D-103** | 2026-09-23 | How an event reaches the merged feed exactly once. Three mechanisms overlap: sharing writes a system entry *and* an audit row; a stage change writes a system entry *and* an opportunity version; creating anything writes version 1 *and*, for an opportunity, a system entry. A plain union double-reports all three. | (a) union everything and let the reader see each event twice, (b) drop the duplicate per overlap, keeping the record that is visible to the most readers, (c) add a correlation key to the writers so the feed can join them | (b) — the three `share_*` audit actions are excluded (`SHARE_ACTIONS`), an opportunity version reaches the feed only if it moved a field other than `stage` (`OPPORTUNITY_SILENT_FIELDS`), and version 1 is excluded everywhere by the join to the preceding version | (a) is what the reviewer already complained about in a different form — a history that reads as noise. (c) is a schema change and a write-path change to solve a read-side presentation problem, against a milestone whose whole point is that the data already exists. (b) is three narrow, named exclusions, each keeping the record with the wider audience: the sharing system entry every reader sees rather than the owner-only audit row, and the `stage_change` system entry rather than the owner-only version row. Nothing is lost — `GET .../audit-log` and `GET .../versions` still answer in full, and `tests/test_activity.py` holds each exclusion with the case that would otherwise double. | Y | N — a presentation rule inside a milestone the human specified; no data, permission or schema change. |

**D-104** | 2026-09-23 | What orders two events that carry the same timestamp. Every timestamp this app writes is second-resolution (`time_utils.utc_now()`, and D-067 already accepted the consequence for undo), so one user action's system entry, version row and audit row routinely share one `occurred_at`. The feed's cursor needs a total order regardless. | (a) tie-break on the source row id inside an encoding like `search.py`'s (`row id * base + source code`), (b) tie-break on the source first, ranked in the order one action performs them, (c) add sub-second precision to new timestamps | (b) — the feed id is `rank * ACTIVITY_STRIDE + source row id`, ranked system entry, then the three version tables, then the audit log | (a) was the first implementation and it reads wrongly: within one second the order becomes "whichever table had the smaller row id", so the e2e journey showed an entry deleted *before* the opportunity was created. (b) costs one reordered constant and makes the common case — a thing is recorded, then edited, then removed — read in the order it happened, while across seconds the timestamp decides and the rank never comes up. (c) is the real fix and is far out of scope: the format is fixed-width by design (CLAUDE.md, Timestamps), every stored timestamp is compared lexicographically, and changing it touches every table, the FTS index and every date filter. | Y | N — a tie-break inside one read; `test_events_from_one_action_read_in_the_order_it_performed_them` is what holds it. |

## Phase 3 — milestone 3.0a (the three bulk actions D-070 deferred)

**D-105** | 2026-09-23 | Ownership transfer, the capability "assign owner" needs and the app has never had: `owner_id` is set at creation and no route has ever changed it. D-070's ruling named the two questions inside it — *may an editor reassign?* and *does the previous owner keep access?* — and scheduled them here rather than inside a bulk-action wrapper. | **Who may:** (a) the owner only, (b) the owner or any editor. **What becomes of the previous owner:** (c) nothing, they lose the deal entirely, (d) they stay on as an editor, (e) they stay on as a viewer | (a) + (d) — owner-only, and the previous owner is left an `editor` share by the same transfer (`bulk_assign_owner` in `routes_opportunities.py`, through the shared `share_utils.upsert_share_row`) | **(a)** because every other write to an opportunity row is owner-only through `require_opportunity_owner` — name, value, stage, sharing, deletion — and handing the record to someone else is the largest of them; an editor who may not rename a deal may certainly not give it away. It also keeps `AGENTS.md`'s permissions model as it is rather than adding a fourth rule. **(d)** because a transfer is the one destructive-feeling action with no undo available to the person who performs it: (c) would mean a mis-typed email removes the deal from the previous owner's board with no way back except asking the new owner to share it. Leaving them an editor keeps every note they wrote readable and writable by them, lets the new owner reverse it from the sharing panel in two clicks, and costs nothing anyone cannot see — the share row is listed in the sharing panel and the handover is on the timeline. (e) was considered and rejected as the worst of both: the person who ran the deal until a moment ago cannot add to it. The transfer is recorded the way sharing is, because it is an access change (`AGENTS.md` invariant 7): a system entry every reader sees, an owner-only audit row (`owner_changed`), and a notification to the new owner, who did not ask to be given a deal. No version row: `owner_id` is not one of `OPPORTUNITY_VERSIONS`' snapshot fields, because ownership is not edited where the record is read. | Y — the previous owner's share is an ordinary share row; dropping the grant is one line and one test. | **Y** — a permission and product call the human's D-070 ruling explicitly reserved. The alternative worth naming is (c), the clean break, which is what a reader who expects "transfer" to mean "it is no longer mine" would predict. Recommend (d) as shipped; it is the safer default for an action the previous owner cannot undo themselves, and a new owner who wants the clean break can remove the share. |

**D-106** | 2026-09-23 | What "add to view" adds to. A saved view is a filter/sort spec (`filters`, `sort`, opaque JSON — milestone 2.2), not a curated set of opportunity ids, so D-070 recorded that the action had "no target to write to". | (a) build view membership: a `saved_view_opportunities` table, a membership read, and a rule for how a view's filters and its members combine, (b) write the selection into the view's `filters` as an `ids` filter, read by the client like every other filter, (c) leave it unbuilt again | (b) — `VIEW_IDS_FILTER` in `routes_saved_views.py`, `OpportunityViewState.ids` and `pinnedIds` in `lib/domain.ts` | (b) is the shape this app already has: `filters` is literally a saved `URLSearchParams` and a new filter field was always meant to be "a frontend change, not a migration" (`schemas.py`'s `_bounded_params`). A pinned selection *is* a filter — "these ids" — so it serialises to `?opportunities.ids=3,7`, survives a reload, is shareable by URL, and is permission-filtered per reader like every other view, all without a table. (a) is a second membership model beside the filter model, and then a semantics question nobody has asked for (does a member bypass the view's stage filter, or intersect with it?); brief §9 forbids that much new surface without an accepted decision, and D-070 itself said the missing capability should be decided deliberately rather than invented inside a wrapper. (c) is what D-070 already chose once and the human closed. Two consequences, both accepted and tested: the pinned set is a **filter, not an ordering** (the view's own sort still decides the order), and a view can hold only as many ids as `MAX_VIEW_PARAMS_JSON_LENGTH` allows, which the route reports as a 400 rather than silently truncating. | Y — the ids live in one filter key; a membership table could replace it later and read the same way. | **Y** — this defines what the feature *means*: a view that pins deals is still a filter, so an opportunity that stops matching the view's other filters still shows while pinned, and nothing "belongs to" a view the way a task belongs to a list. Recommend as shipped; the alternative (a) is a Phase 6-sized feature (`custom_field`-scale) and should be scheduled as one if the human wants folders rather than filters. |

**D-107** | 2026-09-23 | Multi-record export. `routes_export.py` generates one document per opportunity — the deal plus its whole timeline, in JSON, CSV or PDF — and the action bar's "export" has a selection rather than a record. | (a) a fourth action on `POST /api/v2/opportunities/bulk`, (b) a `GET` of its own with the ids in the query string, answering with a file, (c) concatenate the per-opportunity export for every selected id | (b) — `GET /api/v2/opportunities/export/{csv,json}?ids=`, CSV and JSON only | (a) cannot work as such: the bulk endpoint answers `{"results": [...]}` per id, and a file has no room for per-item outcomes — mixing the two would mean one endpoint with two response shapes chosen by a field. (b) keeps the action bar's export a plain `<a href>`, exactly like the three export links on an opportunity's own screen, so there is no blob-download path in the client and no second way to trigger a download. The permission filter goes inside the query (`ACCESSIBLE_OPPORTUNITY_IDS_SQL`, the way `search.py` does it), so an id the caller cannot read is absent from the file rather than refused — a 404 naming one id out of a selection would answer whether it exists. (c) was rejected on what it produces: 400 deals' full timelines in one file is not a document anyone reads, and the question "give me the deals I selected" is a spreadsheet of records, which is why the rows are the opportunity's own fields. **PDF is deliberately not offered here** for the same reason: a list of deals is a spreadsheet, and the per-opportunity PDF is untouched. | Y — a third format is one route and one formatter. | **Y** — because it narrows a written spec line: `docs/UX.md` lists "export" on the action bar without saying what comes out, and what ships is CSV and JSON of the *records*, not PDF and not the timelines. Recommend as shipped; if the reviewer wants a selection to export as one PDF per deal in an archive, that is a Phase 4 item beside the attachment work, not a formatter change. |

## Phase 3 — milestone 3.1 (TipTap serializing to Markdown)

**D-108** | 2026-09-23 | How the editor's document becomes Markdown and back. `AGENTS.md` fixes the editor (TipTap) and the storage (Markdown) but not the conversion, and TipTap's own schema is not Markdown's: it has to be told how each node reads and writes. | (a) `@tiptap/markdown`, TipTap's own first-party parser/serializer, (b) `tiptap-markdown`, the community package that predates it, (c) map TipTap's schema onto `prosemirror-markdown`'s `MarkdownParser`/`MarkdownSerializer` by hand | (a), in `frontend/lib/markdown.ts` | (a) is versioned with the editor it serializes (3.31.3 against 3.31.3), is MIT, and brings one dependency of its own (`marked`); `npm audit` reports nothing across the 53 packages the four `@tiptap/*` additions pull in. (b) is a third-party wrapper over two more libraries (markdown-it and turndown) whose last release predates TipTap 3. (c) is a hand-written mapping of every node and mark to maintain forever, for a round trip the first-party package already does — and this milestone's whole premise is that no content changes shape, so the risk sits in the conversion rather than in owning it. **What is load bearing is next to it, not in it:** `contentExtensions` is the one declaration of which nodes the app's content may contain, and it is the renderer's set, not a wider one. `underline` is switched off because Markdown has no syntax for it and `MarkdownText`'s sanitizer has no `u` tag, so the editor would produce something the reader would never see. The allowlist in `app/MarkdownText.tsx` is **unchanged** (`docs/FEATURES.md` §4's requirement): the one construct that needed checking rather than assuming is a checklist's ticked box, and `renders a checklist's boxes with the state the writer gave them` shows it survives the existing schema. `linkProtocols` moved into `lib/markdown.ts` so the editor's link validation and the sanitizer's `href` allowlist are one list — the editor cannot now write a `javascript:` link that the renderer would silently strip. | Y — the conversion is two functions behind `markdownFromDoc` / `docFromMarkdown`. | N — it implements a technical decision `AGENTS.md` already records ("**TipTap** editor, storing **Markdown**"), and adds no capability beyond it. |

**D-109** | 2026-09-23 | Which surfaces the rich composer replaces. `docs/code_review.md` finding 10 names notes and comments — the two `<input>` composers — while a timeline entry's body was already a `<textarea>` whose content was printed as **plain text** (`<p className="timeline-copy">{entry.body}</p>`), so a list typed into it showed its dashes. | (a) notes and comments only, leaving the entry body a textarea rendered as plain text, (b) all three content surfaces: one composer, and one renderer reading them back | (b) | Requirement 17 is "sanitized Markdown rich text", not "in notes": an entry body is the same kind of thing as a note — prose a person writes about a deal — and it is the field a call gets logged into. Leaving it out would mean two composers in one panel with different capabilities, and a body that can be written with structure the timeline refuses to show. It is also the surface that makes milestone **3.5** coherent: PDF export renders "bodies", and rendering notes as Markdown while escaping entry bodies would be half a fix. Cost, stated plainly: an existing entry body containing a Markdown character now renders as Markdown — `*` around a word becomes emphasis, a leading `#` becomes a heading. That is the same exposure notes and comments have had since the MVP, and no stored text changes. | Y — one component swap in `app/TimelineEntryList.tsx`. | **Y** — it widens what the milestone was read as covering, and it changes how already-stored entry bodies display. Recommend as shipped; the alternative (a) is one line, and the seeded demo data shows what (b) looks like. |

**D-110** | 2026-09-23 | Whether a composer is open on arrival. The old note and comment composers were a single-line `<input>` always on screen, one per entry and one per note. An editor is not: each is a ProseMirror view with its own document, plugins and DOM. | (a) mount the editor everywhere the input used to be, (b) collapse each composer to the control that opens it, and mount the editor when someone writes in it, (c) one shared composer for the whole panel, moved to wherever it is being used | (b) — `Composer` in `app/RichTextEditor.tsx` | (b) is what `docs/UX.md` already specifies for the entry composer ("Progressive disclosure. Collapsed it is one line"), and generalising it costs nothing: the draft lives in the `Composer` rather than in the editor, so `Esc` or Cancel puts the composer away with what was written still in it, which is the other half of the same UX line. (a) would mount one editor per entry plus one per note on a timeline of fifty entries to serve the one a reader is typing in. (c) is a single editor that has to be told where it is, which is exactly the "edit it somewhere other than where you read it" pattern milestone 2.11 removed (D-051). Two visible consequences, both accepted: adding a note now takes a click before typing, and posting closes the composer rather than clearing it — resetting a document means creating a new one, and unmounting is how that is done honestly. | Y | **Y** — every reader who adds a note meets this. Recommend as shipped; the alternative is (a), which reads as "the composer is already open" at the cost of dozens of editors on one screen. |

**D-111** | 2026-09-23 | Slash commands. `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 3 outcome names "a real editor with slash commands and @mention autocomplete", and `FEATURES.md` §4 lists slash commands beside mentions, checklists and inline attachments. Milestone 3.1 is the editor; 3.2 is the mention autocomplete. | (a) build the slash menu here, (b) build it in 3.2 beside the mention autocomplete, (c) drop it | (b) | They are one mechanism with two triggers: a `@tiptap/suggestion` popup, keyboard-navigable, announced to assistive technology, that turns a typed prefix into a list of choices. Building it twice — once for `/` here and once for `@` in 3.2 — is the duplication `AGENTS.md`'s standards rule out, and building it here first would mean 3.2 rewrites it rather than reusing it. Nothing is lost in the meantime: every block the slash menu would insert is on the toolbar or has a Markdown input rule (`- `, `1. `, `> `, `### `, `[ ] `), which is the discoverability a slash menu adds. Inline attachments stay Phase 4, where attachments exist at all, and "links to other entries" goes with the same suggestion popup in 3.2. | Y | **Y** — it moves a roadmap-named capability from the milestone a reader would expect it in to the next one in the same phase. Recommend (b); if the reviewer wants it here, it is a milestone of its own rather than an addition to this one. |

**D-112** | 2026-09-23 | `lib/keyboard.ts`'s `isTypingTarget` asked `target.isContentEditable`, which jsdom leaves `undefined` — so the guard that stops a typed letter being a command was untested against a contenteditable composer, and the moment the note composer became one, `app/Keyboard.test.tsx`'s guard test failed: typing "check jack's expiry date?" moved focus out of the composer and into the entry form. | (a) stub `isContentEditable` in the test, (b) read the attribute as well as the property, (c) mark the composer with a data attribute of this app's own | (b) — `target.isContentEditable || target.closest('[contenteditable="true"]')` | (a) hides the gap rather than closing it: the guard test would then pass against a property the real composer's element does not set in the environment the test runs in, which is the definition of a test that proves nothing. (b) is correct in a browser too, and slightly more so: `closest` catches a keystroke whose target is an element *inside* the editable region, which `isContentEditable` also does but a bare property read on a nested node would not if the browser ever changed where it points. (c) invents a second marker for a state the DOM already records. Confirmed the guard fails with (b) reverted before trusting it, per brief §6.5. | Y | N — a bug fix in a guard, with the test that catches it. |

**D-113** | 2026-09-23 | How the unit suite writes in a ProseMirror editor. `userEvent.type` dispatches the keystrokes and the document never changes: ProseMirror applies a typed character by reading back the DOM mutation the browser made, and jsdom makes none. Every existing test that typed a note or a comment stopped working. | (a) drive the editor through a handle the component exposes for tests, (b) paste, which ProseMirror parses itself from the clipboard text and applies as a transaction, (c) move all of these journeys to Playwright | (b) — `writeInEditor` / `clearEditor` in `app/test-editor.ts` | (a) is a test-only seam in shipped code, and it would let a component pass its own tests while being undriveable by a person. (b) is a real reader's action, needs nothing added to the component, and covers the cases those tests were about — what happens to the text once it is in the composer. Deleting is the same story from the other side: a selection delete is a ProseMirror *command*, so `clearEditor` works where typing does not. (c) would move a dozen fast assertions into a browser suite that takes two orders of magnitude longer, for no gain in what is asserted. **What (b) cannot cover is typing itself**, and with it the Markdown input rules — so `e2e/18-rich-text.spec.ts` types a bullet list in a real browser, which is also `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s acceptance criterion for this phase. `vitest.setup.ts` grew the geometry stubs ProseMirror needs for the same reason the virtualizer needed `offsetHeight`: jsdom lays nothing out, and `Text` and `Range` implement no geometry at all. | Y | N — test infrastructure, and the gap it leaves is covered by a browser test rather than left open. |

**D-114** | 2026-09-23 | Re-saving a note nobody edited. The editor writes a document back out in its own syntax, so `* one` returns as `- one` and `__x__` as `**x**`: opening a note and pressing Save with no change would write a new body, a version row and an "edited" marker for an edit nobody made. | (a) let it write, since the text did change, (b) compare the draft against the stored body as this editor would write it, and close without saving when they match, (c) normalize every body on the way in, once, so the two can never differ | (b) — `normalizeMarkdown` in `lib/markdown.ts`, called by `NoteBlock.save` | (a) puts noise into the one screen the human asked for at the Phase 2 gate: an activity log where "Blair edited a note" means "Blair opened a note". (b) is four lines and matches what `InlineEdit` already does for a field (`if (next !== value)`). (c) is a content migration, which is precisely what `docs/FEATURES.md` §4 says this feature does not need — and it would rewrite every stored body on the strength of a serializer's formatting preferences. | Y | N — it prevents a write, and `writes nothing when a note is reopened and saved untouched` holds it. |

## Human rulings — 2026-09-24 (Phase 3 editor checkpoint)

Five decisions ruled, all **agreed as recommended**: **D-105** (ownership
transfer leaves the previous owner an editor share — *"Agree, keep the editor
share"*), **D-108** (Markdown pipeline), **D-109** (the editor replaces entry
bodies too), **D-110** (collapsed composers), **D-111** (slash commands in 3.2
beside mentions). Checks E1, E3 and E5 passed; E2 was not marked.

---

**D-115** | 2026-09-24 | Four corrections from the checkpoint, investigated before scheduling. **(1) Timeline order.** The reviewer asked for an ascending/descending option with descending as default. **It already exists and works** — `TimelineFilterBar` renders a "Timeline reading order" select, 2.2 built it under D-051, and the keyset cursor reverses correctly. What is wrong is the default: `lib/domain.ts:234` sets `"asc"`, so the reviewer saw oldest-first and reasonably concluded the feature was absent. **(2) Two export controls.** Confirmed: `OpportunityPanel.tsx:228` offers CSV and JSON for one opportunity, `BulkActionBar.tsx` offers export over a selection. **(3) The opportunity header sits above the timeline**, so reaching the timeline means scrolling past it. **(4) A version list with one row says only "V1"**, and the reviewer's note was *"I don't know how version control works."* | Fix now, or defer to R.1's consistency pass | Fix now, grouped as milestone **3.1a** | All four are small and all four are the kind of thing that compounds: a default nobody wants, a duplicated control, a screen that buries its own subject, and a list that does not explain itself. Deferring them to R.1 means every remaining Phase 3 milestone is demonstrated on a screen with known irritations, and the reviewer re-reports them each round. The ordering one carries a lesson worth keeping: **a shipped option nobody can find is indistinguishable from a missing one** — 2.2 built the control, D-051 recorded it, and the reviewer still asked for it. So 3.1a's entry asks for discoverability to be checked, not just the default flipped. | Y | N — four corrections with verified causes. |

---

**D-116** | 2026-09-24 | The reviewer asked two related things at the checkpoint and asked for analysis rather than assent: *"Ideal scenario — audit log, history, and activity log all can be either under the same menu or combined with a label. Ideally, we should be able to restore to previous version. Analyze the demand and let me know if it makes sense and if so effort level."* | For restore: (a) build it, (b) decline it as outside v2. For consolidation: (c) merge the three surfaces, (d) leave them separate | (a) and (c), scheduled together as milestone **3.1c** | **Restore makes sense, and the analysis is that it is what makes history worth keeping.** Invariant 8 exists so a rewritten record can be recovered; without restore the recovery is manual retyping from a read-only list, which is recovery in principle only. Verified that nothing does it today: the three existing `restore` routes undo *soft deletes*, not edits — a different operation on a different table. The design that matters: restoring writes the old content back as a **new** version rather than truncating history, so the act of restoring is itself recorded and never destructive. Owner-only, matching D-096. **Consolidation also makes sense, and is nearly free** because 3.0 already did the hard half: Activity merges system entries, audit rows and version rows at deal level with per-row role filtering. What remains is that an activity row for an edit can expand into the before/after it already has, at which point a separate History destination stops earning its place and the audit log stops being a third menu. The two are scheduled together because they touch the same surface — building restore against a consolidated view is one pass, against three views is three. **Effort, since it was asked for:** one milestone, weighted toward the restore routes and their tests rather than the UI merge; comparable to 2.10, which added the version tables these read from. Constraint carried into the milestone: consolidation must not widen who sees audit rows — 3.0's per-row filtering stays. | Y — new routes and a view merge; no schema change, since the versions already exist. | N — the human asked for the analysis and the answer is yes on both. |

---

**D-117** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.1a, implementation-time calls not fully pinned by D-115. **(1) Reading-order discoverability** — D-115 asked that discoverability be checked, not only the default flipped. **(2) The header-and-facts block's new home** — D-115 says "move that block to the right column beneath the AI panel"; the sharing panel sits directly beneath that block today and is not named. | (1) Leave the control as-is vs. also restyle/reposition it. (2) Move only `TimelineHeading` vs. move `TimelineHeading` and `SharingPanel` together | (1) Leave as-is, default flip only. (2) Move both | **(1)** The control already carries a visible caption ("Order") beside its `aria-label`, per D-082's rule that every filter/sort control names itself visibly — that rule is what "discoverable" means elsewhere in this codebase, and it already held before this milestone. The reviewer's actual complaint was the default, not the control's visibility (`docs/autopilot/PROGRESS.md`'s 3.1a entry: "a shipped option nobody finds is the same as a missing one" — true of a default pointed away from what most readers want, not of a labelled dropdown). Restyling it further would be defensiveness against a problem already diagnosed and fixed. **(2)** `SharingPanel` is owner-only opportunity management, same category as the header it sits beside, not part of "the timeline" the milestone is clearing a path to. Leaving it in the left column would mean an owner — the role that asked for this fix — still scrolls past a block before reaching entries, defeating the stated goal ("leaving the timeline as the first thing read") for exactly the reader who filed it. Moved together; both now render inside `.sidebar-column`, beneath `AiSidebar`. | Y (both reversible, one file) | N — small implementation calls within an already-scoped milestone, not new scope. |

---

**D-118** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.1a reported its gate as `make check-fast` green and noted that three E2E specs it had rewritten were *"type/lint-clean, but not executed (no cached Playwright browser; not required for a milestone commit per the brief's gate split — deferred to the Phase 3 end gate)"*. I ran the suite on the host: **58 passed, 3 failed.** | (a) accept the deferral and let the phase gate find it, (b) rework now | (b), as milestone **3.1b** | Two of the three failures are direct regressions from 3.1a's own change — `09-saved-views.spec.ts:115` and `16-edit-in-place.spec.ts:22` both assert timeline ordering that was true under the old ascending default and false after 3.1a flipped it to descending. Deferring those to the phase gate means several more milestones build on a red suite, and by then the cause is one of many candidate changes rather than the obvious one. The third failure, `05-export.spec.ts:43`, is **not a defect**: the E2E web server runs on the host, WeasyPrint's native libraries are absent on macOS, and the PDF case returns the wrong status for that reason alone — the gap `CLAUDE.md` documents. It must not be "fixed" by weakening the assertion; a WeasyPrint-guarded skip matching `tests/test_export.py:224-228` is the only change worth making. **The process finding is the more important half.** A milestone was recorded done against a gate that had not been run, on the reasoning that the split gate permits deferring Docker work to the host. It does not extend to the E2E suite: brief §9 forbids marking a milestone done with a red gate, and an unexecuted gate is not a green one. The correct move when E2E cannot run in the sandbox is to say so and stop, not to defer verification to a gate several milestones downstream. Worth recording that my own first diagnosis was wrong: I assumed the sandbox's `chromium-1194` cache was stale against a newer Playwright, and it is not — the project pins 1.56.1, which wants exactly 1194, so the browser was present. The likely real cause is the frontend `node_modules` volume being empty at that moment, the same nocopy behaviour behind D-048, and 3.1b should establish that rather than assume it. | Y — two assertions and possibly one skip guard. | N — a verified regression with a known cause. |

---

**D-119** | 2026-09-24 | Executing milestone 3.1b (D-118's rework), this session's sandbox had a populated `frontend/node_modules` and the cached Playwright Chromium 1194 build, so `npm run e2e` ran the full 61-spec suite directly — no deferral to the host was needed. This confirms D-118's guess about the cause (an empty node_modules mount, not a missing/stale browser) but also means the "E2E is host-only in this sandbox" assumption several PROGRESS.md entries carry is not structurally true — it depends on mount state at session start, which varies. Separately, fixing `16-edit-in-place.spec.ts:22` surfaced a second, independent cause behind the same failure D-118 attributed only to the ordering default: retargeting the test at the new literal-last entry ("Call 1") still failed, on a 391px scroll the ordering fix did not explain. | (a) loosen the test's 40px scroll budget to tolerate this, (b) find an entry position that does not trigger it and use that, (c) treat it as a pre-existing flake and leave both failures for a later session | (b) — retarget at the second-from-last entry, verified via a throwaway instrumented spec (written, run, deleted) to reproduce zero scroll | The 391px scroll is real browser behavior, not a regression: editing the *literal last* entry in a list gives its growing editor form nothing below it to absorb into, so the browser's own focus-follow scrolls the document to keep the newly revealed `Title` input in view — this would happen for any last-entry edit, in either reading order, whenever the editor's expanded height exceeds the space below the collapsed entry within the viewport. It is not D-051's bug (jumping to an unrelated composer elsewhere on the page); it just shares this test's assertion. (a) would mask a real future regression of the kind D-051 exists to catch, by widening the tolerance past what "did not move" should mean. (c) contradicts the brief's own rule this rework exists to enforce (§9: no milestone done on a red gate) and leaves 3.1b itself half-finished. (b) fixes the test to assert what it always meant — edit something below the fold with context on both sides — without weakening the assertion or the milestone's original intent. | Y — one E2E spec, reversible | N — a verified test-construction issue with cause and fix confirmed by direct measurement, not a product or scope decision. |

---

**D-120** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.1c, what a version restore actually writes. D-116 settled that restore should exist and that it must append rather than truncate; four things under that were not settled. (1) What happens when the version asked for is already the current content. (2) Whether a restore that moves `stage` writes the `stage_change` system entry an ordinary stage change writes. (3) Whether a restored note re-sends the mention notifications its body carries. (4) Whether a restore writes an audit row. | (1) write a duplicate version anyway / reject with 409 / write nothing and answer with the current row. (2) write the system entry / stay silent. (3) notify / do not. (4) audit row / version row only | (1) write nothing and answer 200 with the current row. (2) write the system entry. (3) do not notify. (4) version row only | **(1)** This is D-114's rule — "an edit that changed nothing writes nothing" — asserted on the server rather than in the composer, and it buys idempotence: a retried restore leaves one version, not two, which matters because the only undo available for a restore is another restore. A 409 would be the same information delivered as a failure, and the caller would have nothing useful to do with it. The UI never offers the current version anyway (restore is reached from the version an edit *replaced*), so this guards the API rather than the screen. **(2)** `CLAUDE.md`'s "every stage change is the same write" is load bearing: the timeline is where a stage change is narrated, and a restore that moved the stage without writing one would leave the timeline and the record disagreeing — the exact failure the system-entry mechanism exists to prevent. **(3)** The body is not new. Whoever it names was notified when it was first written, and re-announcing it would make every restore a fresh ping for text those people have already read; the restore is still recorded, as a version and therefore as an activity row. **(4)** `AGENTS.md` invariant 7 scopes the audit log to deletions and sharing, and invariant 8 puts edits in the version tables — the distinction D-101 had to state plainly at the Phase 2 gate. A restore is an edit, so it belongs in the half that records edits. It reaches the activity feed either way, which is where a human actually looks. | Y — three routes, no schema change | N — four implementation calls inside a milestone the human already scoped. |

---

**D-121** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.1c's other half. D-116 said the separate History destination "stops earning its place and the audit log stops being a third menu" once an activity row can expand. Executing that means deciding what is actually removed, and whether "expand" means the server sends the before/after with the feed or the row fetches that item's versions when it is opened. | (a) remove the three History buttons and the sharing panel's audit disclosure, leaving the activity log as the only destination; (b) keep the History buttons and only add the audit rows to the feed; (c) add a `changes` array with before/after values to `ActivityEditEvent` and render from that; (d) have the opened row read the item's existing `GET .../versions` | (a) and (d) | **(a)** is what the human asked for, and what makes the removal safe is that nothing a reader could see is lost. Deletions and restores already reach an owner through the feed as sentences rather than as the JSON blob the audit disclosure printed. Access changes are the one kind of audit row the feed drops (`ACCESS_ACTIONS`, because the system entry beside them says the same thing) — and those system entries carry the person and the role in words ("Blair was added as editor"), so `AGENTS.md` requirement 18's "full audit log per opportunity including deletions, visible to the Owner" still holds, on one screen instead of three. `GET .../audit-log` is untouched; what moved is where a person reads it. A History button on every entry and every note was also the clutter `AGENTS.md`'s Design bar rules out. **(d) over (c)** is the one that is not obvious. Sending the changed values with the feed looks cheaper — the server already selects `previous_*` for the comparison — but the values would arrive as raw column text, and a version's fields are rendered through `lib/domain.ts`'s per-kind `read` functions, which is how `value` becomes "USD 485,000" (it needs `currency` from the same snapshot, which a per-field before/after cannot supply). (c) would have meant either a visible regression in how history reads or a second formatting path built only for this screen. (d) also keeps the three `GET .../versions` endpoints called by something, and fetching on expand is the rule the History dialog already followed — a timeline of forty edits opens with zero history requests. | Y — reversible, but it removes shipped surfaces | **Y** — the human used the per-item History dialog and the audit disclosure at the 3.1 checkpoint, and this retires both. The replacement is on one screen and loses no information; the judgement to confirm is that one destination with expandable rows is what they meant by "under the same menu or combined with a label". |

**D-122** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.2, what the `@` autocomplete actually inserts. The backend finds a mention by matching `@<address>` in the stored body (`notification_service.MENTION_RE`), and TipTap's usual answer to mentions is `@tiptap/extension-mention`, a node with its own attributes. | (a) `@tiptap/extension-mention`, with a Markdown serializer taught to write it back as `@address` text, (b) insert plain `@address` text and add no node at all, (c) insert a `mailto:` link | (b), in `lib/suggestions.ts` | (b) is the whole feature: requirement 13 already works, and what was missing was that nobody can type a colleague's address from memory (`docs/FEATURES.md` §7 — "mentions require typing a full email address with no hint, which makes requirement 13 effectively undiscoverable"). So the autocomplete's output has to be exactly the text a person would have typed, and then *nothing* changes about storage, the API, the notification query, the sanitizer's allowlist or `app/MarkdownText.tsx`. (a) introduces a node the renderer has never seen, for a round trip that has to serialize back to the same characters anyway: the storage is Markdown (D-108), so a mention node's only job would be to become `@address` on the way out and be re-parsed as text on the way in — a schema addition that buys styling and costs the one guarantee 3.1 was built on. It would also mean `contentExtensions` and the sanitizer diverge, which is exactly what D-108 forbids. (c) changes what a mention means: a `mailto:` link invites mail to a colleague instead of notifying them in the app, and `MENTION_RE` would not match the Markdown it produces. | Y — one string in one function | N — it implements the roadmap's "@mention autocomplete" without touching storage or schema. |

**D-123** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.2 needs the list of people a mention can reach, and no route answers it. `GET /api/v2/opportunities/{id}/shares` is the nearest thing and is `require_opportunity_owner`, so an editor or viewer writing a note cannot read it; it also has no row for the owner, since ownership is implicit in `opportunities.owner_id`. | (a) new `GET .../members` (owner + shares), `require_opportunity_access`, (b) widen `GET .../shares` to every role, (c) keep it owner-only and have non-owners type addresses by hand as they do today, (d) return the member list inside `OpportunityPublic` so it arrives with the board | (a) | **The set has to be the one a mention actually reaches.** `notify_mentions` only notifies a user who is in `get_accessible_user_ids`, so an autocomplete over anything else would offer people whose mention silently does nothing. That set is the owner plus the live shares, which is what this route returns and what `SharePublic` structurally cannot. (b) would widen more than is needed: a share row carries `invited_by`, `invited_by_name` and `created_at` — who invited whom and when — which is the sharing panel's owner-only business (`AGENTS.md` invariant 7's neighbourhood), and nothing about the autocomplete needs it. (c) leaves two thirds of the roles with the problem this milestone exists to fix, and an editor is exactly the person most likely to be mentioning the owner. (d) puts a per-opportunity list on every row of the board for a feature used in one composer at a time, and it would make the member list a cache-invalidation problem for every write to an opportunity. **What this does widen, deliberately:** an editor or viewer can now read the names and addresses of the other people on that one deal. Those names are already on the screen — every entry, note and comment prints its author's name, and a viewer can already mention a colleague if they happen to know the address. The addresses are new, scoped to one opportunity the caller can already read, and 404 for anyone who cannot (existence does not leak, as everywhere else). | Y — one route, no schema change | **Y** — it makes colleagues' email addresses visible to every role on an opportunity rather than to its owner. Recommended as shipped, because the alternative is an autocomplete that only owners get; the reviewer's call is whether that visibility is acceptable at 20 known internal users. |

**D-124** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.2's third trigger, "links to other entries" (`docs/FEATURES.md` §4, scheduled here by D-111). An entry has no address today: the app's three routes are `/`, `/search` and `/opportunities/:id`, and nothing points at one entry. So the trigger needs a target, a character, and something to happen on arrival. | Target: (a) `/opportunities/{id}?entry={entryId}`, (b) a fragment `#entry-17`, (c) a new route `/opportunities/{id}/entries/{entryId}`. Character: `#`, `[[`, or `+`. Arrival: scroll and mark the entry, or do nothing beyond opening the timeline | (a), `#`, and scroll-and-mark | **The target.** (c) would be a second Next route, which `output: "export"` turns into a second HTML file and splits the SPA (ADR 0005, and the note in `CLAUDE.md`'s Frontend section) — the reason `AppRoot` holds the route table. (b) reads well but a fragment is not something the panel can act on through the router's own state, and it collides with the anchor behaviour the browser applies first. (a) is a query parameter on the route the entry already lives on, so a link is shareable, reloadable and bookmarkable for free. It is deliberately *not* part of `TimelineFilters`: it is not a filter, so it carries no `timeline.` prefix and `paramsWithState` leaves it alone. **The character.** `#` is how a record is referenced in the tools this app is measured against, and it matches the entry id as typed (`#12`). It shares the character with a Markdown heading, and that costs nothing: a heading is `#` *then a space*, a space ends a suggestion, so `# ` still opens a heading. `[[` would avoid even that flicker at the price of being a convention nobody would guess. **Arrival.** A link that lands the reader at the top of a fifty-entry timeline has not told them anything, so `OpportunityPanel` reads `?entry=` and the card scrolls itself into view and stays marked while the URL names it. That also required one change in `app/MarkdownText.tsx`: a href starting with `/` is rendered as a router `Link`, because the anchor it used to render would reload the whole application to reach a route the client router already holds (and, with `target="_blank"`, in a second tab). The sanitizer is untouched — a relative href carries no protocol, so the allowlist always kept it. | Y | N — one new trigger inside a milestone the roadmap already scopes, no new route and no schema change. |

**D-125** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.2's popup has to be positioned at the caret and driven from ProseMirror, while being rendered by React. `@tiptap/suggestion` 3.31.3 offers `props.mount(element)`, which adopts an element into `document.body` and positions it with Floating UI; the plugin callbacks also have to see the current member list, which arrives from a query after the composer opened. | Positioning: (a) `props.mount`, (b) absolute inside `.rich-editor` from `props.clientRect()`. Currency of the sources: (c) rebuild the extensions when the sources change, (d) a ref the plugins read when a key is pressed | (b) and (d) | **(b)** because `props.mount` moves the element into `document.body`, and the element is React's — taking a React-rendered node out from under its parent is the kind of thing that works until a sibling changes. The composer is already a positioned box the menu belongs inside, so the caret rect minus the composer rect is the whole placement calculation, and there is no new dependency. What (a) would buy is flipping at a viewport edge, which a menu under a caret inside a composer does not need. **(d)** because the extension list is an argument to `useEditor`: changing it rebuilds the editor, which throws away the document the reader is typing into — so a member list arriving two hundred milliseconds after the composer opened would clear the composer. The refs are read only from plugin callbacks, none of which run during a render. This is the one `eslint-disable` in the frontend for `react-hooks/refs`, and it is a false positive the rule cannot see past: it flags *any* function built during a render that captures a ref, including a plugin's own callbacks. The two ways to satisfy it honestly are both worse — (c) above, or a mutable store that is not a ref, which the same compiler refuses under its immutability rule. The disable is one line, names the rule, and carries the reason. | Y | N — implementation of a milestone already scoped; the suppression is narrow and documented in place. |

**D-126** | 2026-09-24 | Milestone 3.3 as `docs/ROADMAP.md` writes it is four features over three schema changes — threaded comments, reactions, resolve/unresolve, watchers — and brief §4.1.3 asks for milestones small enough to finish in one session to a green gate. Threading and resolve are one change to one table and one screen; reactions are a second table with their own read shape, and watchers carry a genuine permission question (`docs/FEATURES.md` §7 says "follow an opportunity you are not shared on", which is in tension with a permission model where absence of access is a 404). | (a) one session attempting all four, (b) split into 3.3 — threading and resolve — and a new 3.3a for reactions and watchers, (c) drop watchers to a later phase | (b) | Splitting keeps the phase's scope whole — nothing is deferred out of Phase 3, and it cannot reach `AWAITING_REVIEW` until 3.3a is done too — while keeping each commit to a gate that was actually run, which is the lesson 3.1b paid for. (a) risks exactly that failure again: four features is more than one session's verified work, and the honest failure mode of trying is a milestone marked done on an unexecuted gate. (c) would drop scope the roadmap names, which is the human's call and not this session's. Threading and resolve are grouped because they are one table and one component; reactions and watchers are grouped because each is a new table with no read-shape change to `comments`. The same precedent exists at 3.0a and 3.0b. | Y | N — sequencing inside a phase whose scope is unchanged. |

**D-127** | 2026-09-24 | How deep may a comment thread go? `comments.parent_id` can express any depth, and nothing in `docs/FEATURES.md` §7 or `docs/ROADMAP.md` says how much is wanted beyond "threaded comments ... Resolve is what keeps a long deal's discussion readable". | (a) arbitrary depth, recursive read and render, (b) one level — a reply answers a thread root and carries no replies of its own, (c) flat with an "in reply to" label and no nesting | (b) | The complaint being fixed is that a question and its answer are two rows that only happen to be adjacent; one level fixes that completely. Arbitrary depth costs a recursive response model, a recursive component, an indentation rule that degrades past the third level, and a "collapse this subtree" affordance nobody asked for — decoration at the 2-3 concurrent users `FEATURES.md` sizes this app for, and the kind of speculative generality `AGENTS.md` rules out. (c) keeps the flat list that is the problem. The rule is enforced in the route rather than in the schema — `create_comment` refuses a parent that is itself a reply with a 400 rather than reparenting it silently, because a silently reparented answer sits under the wrong question — so the column stays able to express a deeper tree if a later phase is asked for one. | N | N — reversible in the direction that matters: the column already holds what a deeper tree would need. |
