**D-128** | 2026-09-24 | Who may resolve a thread, and is resolving recorded anywhere? `AGENTS.md` invariant 2 makes a comment immutable after posting, and invariant 7 scopes the audit log to deletions and sharing. | Permission: (a) anyone with write access, (b) the thread's author or the opportunity owner, (c) the owner only. Recording: (d) an audit row, (e) an activity-log event, (f) on the thread itself and nowhere else | (b) and (f) | **(b)** is the rule this app already applies to changing an existing timeline item, resolved through the same `require_author_or_owner` (D-005, which the human settled in favour of the stricter reading). The person who asked the question can say it is answered and the deal's owner can say so about any thread on it; a third editor closing someone else's question is the drift D-005 exists to prevent. (a) would put a second, looser rule beside the strict one for no reason a reader could infer; (c) would make a thread the author themself cannot close. **(f)** because resolving changes no `body` — invariant 2 is untouched, and the route is a POST rather than the comment PATCH that must never exist — and because widening the audit log past deletions and sharing is an invariant-7 change, which is the human's to make. The thread already shows who resolved it and when, which is where a reader asks the question. Both directions are idempotent (`COALESCE` on the way in, unconditional clear on the way out), so a retry is safe and the first resolver is the one recorded. | N | N — the permission rule is the existing one; recording can be widened later without changing what is stored. |

---

**D-129** | 2026-09-25 | Milestone 3.3a: `docs/FEATURES.md` §7 names "follow an opportunity you are not shared on." Every other opportunity-scoped route resolves a role and 404s an outsider, so existence never leaks (`CLAUDE.md`'s Permissions section, `AGENTS.md` invariant 7's neighbour). Watching cannot go through that gate — the whole point is to be reachable by someone the access model would otherwise turn away. | (a) require a share to watch (a weaker "follow" role inside `opportunity_shares`), which keeps existence hidden but contradicts the feature as named, (b) a new `require_opportunity_exists` check — presence only, no role — used only by the three `.../watch` routes, accepting that any authenticated caller learns whether an id is live, (c) let watching itself stay hidden behind 404 and put the capability somewhere else entirely (an out-of-band "request to watch") | (b) | (a) is not the feature `FEATURES.md` describes; a share-gated "follow" is indistinguishable from a viewer share with no read rights, which is a role this app does not otherwise have and would need its own authorization-matrix rule. (c) invents a request/approval flow nothing in the roadmap or `FEATURES.md` asks for — scope drift `AGENTS.md` invariant 8 rules out. (b) is named and bounded: `require_opportunity_exists` is the one deliberate exception in `permissions.py`, used nowhere else, and what it leaks is narrow — that an id is live, not the opportunity's content, its owner, or anything else a share would reveal. A caller could already infer liveness by trying ids in sequence against any endpoint's 404, so the marginal leak is the existence fact one call sooner. `GET .../watchers` (who is watching) stays owner-only through the ordinary `require_opportunity_owner`, so the one-way exception does not also widen who may read the *result* of watching. | Y — narrowing to a share-gated "follow" later is a permissions.py change plus a migration, not a data-model change (the `watchers` table already carries no role). | Y — it is the one place in the app where existence is knowingly not hidden behind access, which is a change to invariant 7's neighbouring norm even though invariant 7 itself (viewer/editor enforcement) is untouched. Recommended: keep (b); the alternative (a) ships a different feature than the one named in `FEATURES.md`. |

**D-130** | 2026-09-25 | Two smaller calls inside milestone 3.3a, grouped because both concern how a *toggle* — as opposed to *content* — behaves in an app whose default is soft delete and 201/204 responses: (1) are `reactions` and `watchers` rows soft-deleted like every other user table, and (2) what does removing a reaction or unwatching return? | (1) (a) soft-delete both tables like `USER_DATA_TABLES`, (b) hard-delete, alongside `sessions`. (2) (c) 204 No Content, matching `delete_comment` and the share-removal routes, (d) 200 with the resulting state (`{watching: bool}` / the comment with its remaining `reactions`) | (1) (b), (2) (d) | **(1)** A reaction and a watch are "I am currently doing this," not a record whose history is worth keeping — nobody needs an audit trail of emoji added and withdrawn, and `soft_delete.py`'s own comment already draws exactly this line for `sessions` ("ephemeral authentication state, not user content"). Soft-deleting them would mean every reaction count and every watcher list forever filters `deleted_at IS NULL` for a table that gains rows with no corresponding value in keeping the tombstones — the `INSERT OR IGNORE` idempotence these routes already rely on stops working cleanly against a soft-deleted duplicate (a withdrawn-then-reapplied reaction would either need to resurrect a row or create a second one, both worse than a plain delete). `tests/test_soft_delete.py`'s scanner is a hard-coded table list; adding these two required a deliberate decision either way, and (b) is what keeps the scanner's rule ("no `DELETE FROM` against `USER_DATA_TABLES`") meaningful rather than papered over with a table it was never meant to guard. **(2)** A reaction toggle and a watch toggle are both meant to be reflected in the UI the instant they resolve — a reader clicking a reaction pill needs the new count, and the watch button needs to know which state it is now in — and (c)'s 204 would cost a second round trip to learn either. This is not the same shape as `delete_comment` or removing a share, where the *thing* is gone and there is nothing left to describe; a reaction or a watch has a remaining state that is exactly what the caller asked for. | Y — either choice is reversible without a schema change; (1) would need a migration to add `deleted_at` retroactively, (2) is a response-shape change only. | N — both are implementation choices inside a feature the roadmap and `FEATURES.md` already scope; neither touches a permission rule or the data model's shape beyond the two new tables themselves. |

**D-131** | 2026-09-28 | Milestone 3.4: ADR 0003 describes "one long-lived `GET /api/opportunities/{id}/events`" and says nothing about how long is long. An SSE connection that is meant to live until the tab closes has two problems here: an intermediary (or a container restart behind a proxy) eventually cuts an idle one with no reconnect advice, and Starlette's `TestClient` buffers a response whole -- it has no incremental read at all -- so a route that never ends is a route no test in `backend/tests/` can call, including `test_authorization_matrix.py`'s three successful actors. | (a) stream indefinitely, closing only when the client disconnects, and test the broker in isolation while leaving the *route* uncovered by the matrix, (b) close the stream after a configured window (`settings.sse_max_connection_seconds`, default 300s) having advertised an SSE `retry:`, and let the browser's own `EventSource` reconnect, (c) keep it indefinite but add a test-only escape hatch (an undocumented query parameter or a patched module constant) so the suite can end a stream | (b) | (b) is what the transport is designed for: `retry:` and automatic reconnection are `EventSource`'s own mechanism, so a bounded window is invisible to a reader and is standard practice in front of any proxy with an idle timeout. It also means the app never holds a connection it has forgotten about. The cost is a gap -- a write during the reconnect reaches nobody -- and that is paid explicitly: `useLiveUpdates` refetches on every `ready` after the first, so a missed event costs a delay rather than a stale screen. (a) leaves the one route that answers `text/event-stream` outside the authorization matrix, which is the guard brief §6.4 exists for, and 3.1b is this phase's lesson about milestones whose gate was not actually run. (c) is a seam in production code that exists only for tests, which `AGENTS.md`'s standards rule out -- and it would test a code path no browser takes. With (b) the tests exercise the real route and only change a documented setting (`tests/conftest.py`'s `short_event_streams`), the way they already change `database_url`. | Y -- the window is one setting, and an indefinite stream is removing the deadline from `events.stream_events`. | N -- ADR 0003 already accepted SSE and named reconnection as the browser's job; this is how long one connection lives, not a change to the transport.

**D-132** | 2026-09-28 | Milestone 3.4's edit guard. ADR 0003: "On note update the client sends the `updated_at` it read; the server rejects with 409 if it has moved." Two calls that leaves open: is the field optional or required (a required one breaks every existing caller of `PATCH /api/v2/notes/{id}`), and is `updated_at` even the right token, given `time_utils.utc_now()` has one-second resolution and two edits inside one second therefore carry the same value? | (1) (a) optional -- enforce it when sent, accept the write when it is absent, (b) required, and record the break in `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s breaking-changes table. (2) (c) `updated_at`, as the ADR words it, (d) the note's latest `note_versions.version_number`, which is exact and monotonic but is a new field on `NotePublic` and a deviation from the ADR | (1) (b), (2) (c) | **(1)** An optional guard is no guard: the client that omits `updated_at` is precisely the client that clobbers, and a rule enforced only for callers who opt in protects nobody. The break is small and fully absorbed inside this repo -- there is one client, the value is already on `NotePublic`, and the seed tool and the test suite echo back what they read (`tools/seed.py`'s `edit_note`, `tests/test_timeline.py`'s `edit_note`). Leaving it optional would also mean carrying a defensive branch for a caller that does not exist, which `AGENTS.md`'s simplicity rule rules out. **(2)** ADR 0003 is above this brief in the source-of-truth order and names `updated_at` specifically; (d) would be more precise but adds a field whose only purpose is this comparison, and the imprecision it removes is a window this project has already looked at and accepted once -- D-067 accepts exactly the same second-resolution collision for undo's delete stamp. Inside that window the ADR's own fallback applies and is sufficient: last-write-wins with full version history, so a clobbered edit is recoverable rather than lost. The limit is documented on `update_note` and pinned by a deterministic clock in `tests/test_events.py` rather than left to how fast the machine runs. | Y -- (1) is a schema default away from optional; (2) would need a field on `NotePublic` but no migration, since `note_versions` already holds the numbering. | Y -- it is this milestone's one client-visible API break, and it is the kind of change `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s breaking-changes table exists to surface at a gate. Recommended: keep (b)+(c); the alternative (a) ships a guard that the only failure mode it exists for can walk straight past.

**D-133** | 2026-09-28 | Milestone 3.4: where does an invalidation get published, and what vocabulary does it use? There is no service layer here by design (`CLAUDE.md`: "shared logic lives in small helper modules rather than a service layer"), so there is no single place every write passes through, and roughly thirty write routes across `routes_timeline.py`, `routes_opportunities.py` and `routes_sharing.py` can change what a second reader is looking at. | **Where:** (a) an explicit `publish_*` call in each write route, below its `commit()`, (b) ASGI middleware that publishes after any successful unsafe request, keyed on the path, (c) an outbox on the `db_connection()` context manager, flushed when the transaction commits. **What:** (d) one event type ("something changed here"), (e) two -- `timeline.changed` and `opportunity.changed`, (f) one per write route, so the client can invalidate exactly | (a) and (e) | **Where.** (b) cannot work: half these routes are addressed by note, comment or entry id and the opportunity they belong to is only knowable from a query the handler has already run -- middleware would have to repeat it. (c) hides the publish inside the connection helper, which is the kind of action-at-a-distance that makes a missing event impossible to see in the route that should have sent one; it also still needs a per-route call to say *which* opportunity, so it buys nothing over (a) but magic. (a) reads the way `create_audit_log_entry` and `record_version` already do -- an explicit line the route owns -- and puts the call below the commit, which is the property that actually matters: a subscriber's only reaction is to read again, so an event published inside the transaction sends them to fetch the state it is about to replace. **What.** (d) makes every write refetch the board, the sharing panel and the watcher list; (f) couples the client's cache keys to the server's route list, which is the coupling this whole design avoids by pushing invalidations rather than payloads. (e) is the smallest vocabulary that distinguishes the two questions a reader can have gone stale about, and the rule between them is stated once: an invalidation may be broader than the write behind it, never narrower -- so `opportunity.changed` invalidates the timeline too, because most writes to a record leave a system entry there and the event does not say whether this one did. Three writes deliberately publish nothing: `POST /opportunities` (nobody can be subscribed to an id that did not exist), an empty `PATCH`, and a version restore of what is already current (D-120) -- all three wrote nothing, and an invalidation with no write behind it sends every reader to refetch a page that has not moved. | Y -- the vocabulary is a string union in `app/events.py` and `lib/liveUpdates.ts`; adding a third type is additive and an unknown event name is simply not listened for. | N -- ADR 0003 already fixed the transport and the invalidation-not-payload rule; this is how that rule is spelled inside the routes.

**D-134** | 2026-09-28 | Milestone 3.4, raised by writing the frontend: a note's editor stays mounted while the live stream refetches the timeline underneath it, so by the time the reader presses Save the `note` prop may already hold somebody else's text and its new `updated_at`. Which value does the edit guard send? | (a) `note.updated_at` as rendered at save time, (b) the value captured when the editor was opened, held in the component's own state beside the draft, (c) close the editor when the note changes underneath, discarding the draft | (b) | (a) is the bug the guard exists to prevent, dressed as an implementation detail: the client would echo back the value the *other* person's write produced, the comparison would succeed, and the one write the guard is for would go through silently. (c) throws away work the reader has not finished and is a worse answer than the 409 -- ADR 0003's conflict model is "reject and offer to reload", not "reload over the top of you". (b) is what the ADR actually says ("the `updated_at` it read"), and it composes with the stream rather than fighting it: the note underneath has already been refreshed, the draft survives, and Cancel is renamed "Discard my edit" so the way out says what it does. The same reasoning is why `lib/api.ts` now throws `ApiError` with the envelope's `code` -- a conflict needs a different sentence from every other failure, and `backend/app/errors.py` has always emitted `code` for exactly that. | Y -- it is one `useState` in `app/NoteBlock.tsx`. | N -- it is the correct reading of a decision the ADR already made; the alternative is a defect.


**D-135** | 2026-09-28 | Milestone 3.5: `docs/code_review.md` finding 9 and `docs/ROADMAP.md` both say the PDF export needs "a Markdown dependency in the backend" but name none. Two open calls: which library, and whether to trust its output directly given `app/sanitize.py` already exists for exactly this ("ready for rich text," unused until now). | **Library:** (a) `markdown-it-py`, already present transitively (via `rich`, a `pip-audit` dependency) and a Python port of the same `markdown-it` family the frontend's `@tiptap/markdown` is built on, (b) `python-markdown`, the other common choice, no relation to the frontend's parser family. **Trust:** (c) render straight to the PDF template, since the composer already refuses a `javascript:` link and the sanitizer is "for later," (d) sanitize the rendered HTML through `app/sanitize.py` before embedding it, and set the parser's `html` option to `False` explicitly rather than rely on its default. | (a), (d) | **Library.** (a) shares a lineage with the editor's own parser, which is the closest this stack gets to "renders the same Markdown the same way" without sharing an actual implementation across languages; it was also already resolvable in the sandbox with no new download, having been pulled in as a transitive dependency already (now made direct in `pyproject.toml`, since production code depends on it and `uv sync --no-dev` would otherwise drop it). `markdown-it-py`'s `commonmark` preset needed `enable(["table", "strikethrough"])` to match what the renderer's `remark-gfm` already supports, and its *default* for the `html` option is `True` (raw HTML passed through) -- the opposite of the safe assumption, discovered by writing the test for finding 9's original `<script>` case, which passed the parser through unescaped before the option was set explicitly. **Trust.** (c) would be reasoning about safety from a client-side control (the composer's link validation) that a request straight to the API entirely bypasses -- `e2e/17-security-regressions.spec.ts` already tests exactly that bypass for the browser render path, and the PDF path deserves the same posture. (d) is also the reason `sanitize.py` was built in Phase F ahead of any caller: brief §4.5 asked for "a server-side sanitization framework for any user HTML, ready for rich text," and this is the first rich text render path that isn't the browser. Two independent layers -- the parser's `html: False` and the sanitizer underneath -- mean a future change to either alone cannot reopen the hole silently, which `tests/test_export.py::test_pdf_html_sanitizes_raw_html_even_if_the_markdown_parser_let_it_through` pins by forcing the first layer off and proving the second still holds. | Y -- swapping the library or dropping the sanitizer call are both self-contained changes inside `export_service.py`. | N -- implements what `docs/ROADMAP.md` already scoped for this milestone; no new user-facing behavior beyond what the milestone specifies, no schema or API change.

## Human rulings — 2026-09-28 (Phase 3 gate)

All eleven decisions **accepted**: D-102, D-105, D-106, D-107, D-109, D-110,
D-111, D-121, D-123, D-129, D-132. Checks G3 (live updates — *"passed with
flying colors"*), G4 (the 409 guard) and G5 (mentions and threads) passed.
**G1 and G2 failed**, and D-121 was accepted with the objection *"the history
buttons aren't removed as I mentioned earlier."* Those failures are D-136.

---

**D-136** | 2026-09-28 | Five findings from the gate review, each verified against the running build before recording rather than taken from either side's account. **(1)** The reviewer failed G1 — *"you can't expand and edit, each activity is a oneliner... Worse, the history button is there showing version history separately"* — and objected on D-121 that the History buttons were never removed. **They were.** Measured on the shipped build: exactly **one** History button on the opportunity page, and **five** rows carrying `aria-expanded`. What defeats it is the name: `ActivityDialog.tsx:52` labels the single consolidated destination `History`, which primes a reader to expect version history and makes the surviving button look like the thing D-121 claimed to delete. **(2)** G2 failed on *"the ability to restore isn't there at all"* — it is there and correct, verified by API against a real ETH entry where restoring version 1 appended version 4 and preserved the intervening edit, but the control sits two levels deep, inside an expanded edit row, behind an affordance ("What changed") that does not read as clickable. **(3)** The opportunity header is still above the timeline: first entry top edge at **y=930** in a 1000px viewport, despite 3.1a and D-117 both claiming the move — the reviewer has now raised this twice. **(4)** `Share` renders **30×106px**, one letter per line, in the sidebar D-117 moved the sharing panel into; reproduced at 1440px and not at 700px or 520px, because the narrow column only exists in the wide layout. **(5)** The timeline order default is `"desc"` (`domain.ts:252`) with no saved views, yet the reviewer saw ascending as themselves and descending as Gavin — a stale `timeline.order=asc` URL parameter in a tab never signed out of; 2.9a resets that on sign-out, which does not help a reload. | (a) accept the phase and fix in Phase 4, (b) rework before acceptance | (b), as milestone **3.7** | Findings 3 and 4 are straightforward defects and 5 is a fragility question, but 1 and 2 are the reason this should not be accepted first: **the phase's headline feature is unfindable.** Accepting means Phase 4 builds on an activity log the only person who has used it could not operate, and the next review would re-report the same thing. Note what this is *not*: nothing is broken. Four of the five findings are a feature that exists, works, is tested, and cannot be found — which is the third instance in this project after the timeline order control (2.2) and the command palette (Phase 2). **The generalisation worth keeping:** a gate cannot catch this class, because every test is written by someone who already knows where the control is. Only a person meeting the UI cold can, which is the argument for keeping human review at every phase boundary rather than trusting a green suite. | Y — naming, affordances, layout and one CSS fix; no schema or permission change. | N — five findings with measurements attached. |

**D-137** | 2026-09-28 | D-136 finding 5 left a question rather than a defect: the timeline's reading order defaults to `"desc"` and is correct in code, but a `timeline.order=asc` left in an old URL silently reverses the timeline for as long as that tab lives, which is how the reviewer came to see one order as themselves and the other as a colleague on the same deal. Is a default any stale link can override good enough? | (a) keep the default and make the override visible, (b) stop serializing `order` into the URL so the default always wins, (c) persist a per-user order preference that outranks the URL | (a) | `timeline.order` is in the URL because view state in the URL is the whole mechanism behind a linkable, reloadable, shareable view (milestone 2.2, D-059) — "read this deal oldest-first" is exactly the kind of link that is worth sending someone, and (b) would break that to fix a symptom. (c) adds a second source of truth for the same setting and a new question every time the two disagree. The actual defect was never the precedence: it was that **nothing on screen said a non-default order was in force**, so two readers comparing screens had no way to find out why they differed. The collapsed filter bar now states the order in the same words as the control that sets it, and opens itself whenever any filter is away from its default — so the stale parameter announces itself on arrival. | Y | N — keeps the shipped precedence and adds a visible statement of it; no storage, route or default changed. |

**D-138** | 2026-09-28 | The 3.7 re-review passed all five checks (`r37-01`..`r37-05`) and raised four new items, one of which is the **third** report of the same thing: *"the rendering of buttons still goes out of whack when windows are resized... it seems all the layout is done for a full screen."* D-136 finding 4 treated the squashed `Share` button as a sharing-panel layout bug and fixed it there. That was the symptom. **The cause is one rule**: `globals.css`'s base `.primary-button, .secondary-button, .danger-button` block sets `min-width: 0`, `white-space: normal` and `overflow-wrap: anywhere`, so any flex row that squeezes a button breaks its label one character per line rather than the button holding its size. Measured on the shipped 3.7 build: `Edit` 38x89 and `Delete` 43x124 inside `.inline-actions` on every timeline entry at 960px and 1100px viewports; `Columns (5)` 44x175 and `New opportunity` 42x227 in the table view at 600px and below; and horizontal overflow of 7-187px in the table plus 87px on every screen at 420px, from `.user-block` in the top bar (483px wide) and `.stage-column`'s fixed minimum. The 1440px sidebar was the one instance I measured after 3.7, which is why R4 passed and the reviewer still saw it. | (a) fix the instances as they are reported, (b) fix the base rule and add a width-sweep guard that fails the build on any control that renders taller than wide | (b), as milestone **3.8** | Three reports of one defect, each one previously read as a different local layout bug, is the signal that the shared rule is wrong rather than the three call sites. `min-width: 0` plus `overflow-wrap: anywhere` was presumably written so a long label could not overflow its container; a label broken to one letter per line is a worse answer than an overflow, and it is what the reviewer has now described three times ("they change shape and become vertical with the letters inside of them messed up"). The guard matters as much as the fix: **no existing test looks at rendered geometry**, which is exactly why a class of defect this visible survived three phases and two gates. A Playwright sweep over the six screens at eight widths, asserting no control renders taller than wide and no page scrolls sideways, is what makes the fourth report impossible rather than merely unlikely. | Y | N — a root-cause fix for a defect reported three times, plus the guard that was missing. |

**D-139** | 2026-09-28 | Milestone 3.8 printed an entry's row id on its card, to close the gap the reviewer found — `ai_service.build_timeline_context` labels every entry `[Entry #<id>, <timestamp>]`, so the model cited a number the screen never showed. Asked whether the id was right or whether a per-deal sequence would read better, the human chose a per-deal sequence. That makes "which number" a real question, because the sequence has to be defined by something. | (a) creation order — rank by `(created_at, id)`, append-only and stable, (b) chronological — rank by `(event_timestamp, id)`, #1 is the oldest thing that happened, (c) position in the list as drawn | (b) | (c) is wrong outright: the list is filterable and its order is a toggle, so a number that came from it would change when a filter did. (a) was the tempting one and is **visibly wrong on this project's own data**: creating an opportunity writes system entries stamped now, and the imported ETH deal's 32 events are backdated to March — so creation order numbers the three system entries #1-#3 and the earliest thing that actually happened #4, and the timeline reads `1, 2, 3, 32, 31, ...` down the page. (b) is what a person counting a timeline counts, and it uses `(event_timestamp, id)` — the same pair the list orders and pages by — so the numbers ascend exactly as an oldest-first reading does, and two entries stamped in the same second break the tie identically in both. **The cost, accepted:** inserting a backdated entry renumbers everything after it, so this number is a reading aid and never an identity. The identity stays the row id, which is what `?entry=` links carry (D-124), and `test_a_backdated_entry_takes_its_place_in_the_numbering` pins the renumbering so it cannot surprise anyone later. One SQL expression (`timeline_utils.ENTRY_SEQUENCE_SQL`) is read by the API's four entry reads and by the AI's context, because a cited number and a printed one disagreeing is the defect this exists to fix — `test_the_context_cites_the_numbers_the_timeline_prints` is what fails if they drift. | Y — computed, not stored, so the definition can change without a migration. | N — the human chose the per-deal sequence; this records which sequence and what it costs. |

**D-140** | 2026-09-28 | Found by verifying D-139 against the running app with a real model call rather than only by test: the answer came back citing `[Entry #40, ...]` and `[Entry #39, ...]`, which match the cards exactly — and `referenced_entry_ids` came back `[40, 39]`, which is wrong. `extract_referenced_entry_ids` parses `#N` out of the answer and keeps it only if `N` is a valid **row id**, so the moment the context started citing sequence numbers that filter was checking one numbering against another. On this deal the ETH import means id 1 is "Opportunity created" (chronologically near-last) while sequence 1 is the March discussion, so the field was both rejecting real citations and admitting numbers that name unrelated rows. | (a) map cited numbers back to ids inside the route and keep the field's name, (b) rename the field to `referenced_entry_numbers` and validate against the sequence numbers, (c) return both | (b) | The field has exactly one consumer — `AiSidebar` prints "Entries: 40, 39" under the answer — so what it must carry is the number the reader can find on a card, which is the sequence. (a) keeps a name honest at the cost of the feature: the sidebar would list ids under an answer citing numbers, so the two lines of the same footnote would disagree. (c) adds a field nothing reads, against `AGENTS.md`'s no-speculative-abstraction rule; Phase 4's links can add the ids when something needs them. The rename is a real API break and is recorded in `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s table, which costs nothing now (one consumer, no external client) and avoids leaving a field called `_ids` full of numbers that are not ids. **What this says about the verification:** the guard test I wrote for D-139 compares the AI *context* to the API, and it passed — this defect lives one layer further out, in what the route does with the model's reply, and only a real call surfaced it. | Y | N — a defect found during verification of an already-approved change, fixed at its cause. |

**D-141** | 2026-09-28 | The human deferred **Phase 5 — Email In and Out** to v3 after accepting Phase 3, and asked for every document and plan revised accordingly. Two questions fall out of that, neither of them the deferral itself: what happens to the phase numbers, and what happens to the machinery Phase 5 was carrying for the rest of the build. | Numbering: (a) renumber 6 to 5, (b) leave the numbers and let 5 be absent. Machinery: (c) defer APScheduler with the email work, (d) move APScheduler to Phase 6 | (b) and (d) | **(b)** because every decision in this log, every milestone in `PROGRESS.md` and every commit message names phases by number; renaming Phase 6 would silently redirect all of them, and a number that identifies a thing is not reused — the same rule this project applies to ids in `search.py` and `activity.py`. The roadmap now reads F, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, with 5's absence stated rather than hidden. **(d)** is the substantive half, and it is the reason the deferral is not a pure subtraction: **Phase 6's automations are time-triggered.** Its own acceptance criterion is a rule that fires after fourteen days of no activity, which cannot be met without a scheduler — so ADR 0004's APScheduler moves to milestone 6.5 rather than leaving with the email work. Two things come with it: `make backup` finally gets the schedule ADR 0011 has wanted since milestone 1.6 (the gap `CLAUDE.md` has documented as waiting for Phase 5), and job-outcome logging stays in v2 because v2 now has a scheduler to lose track of — `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` names a silently dead scheduler as the likeliest production failure. The durable **outbox** does *not* move: nothing in v2 sends anything, so it waits for v3 with the first thing that does. Everything else is annotation rather than rewriting: `AGENTS.md` scope item 5 and `docs/FEATURES.md` §5 keep their numbers and text and are marked v3, because they are the specification and not the schedule; the Phase 5 plan is kept **verbatim** in both `ROADMAP.md` and `PROGRESS.md` under a v3 heading rather than summarized away, since its risk analysis is the most careful in the plan and nothing about it improves by being reconstructed from memory later. Historical records are not edited: `DECISIONS.md` entries, `COVERAGE.md`, the phase review summaries and the milestone notes in `PROGRESS.md` that say "Phase 5" are what was true when written. | Y — the phase is intact and moving it back is a heading change plus dropping 6.5 | N — the human made the call; this records the two consequences they did not ask about and the one that changes v2's scope (APScheduler arriving in Phase 6). |

**D-142** | 2026-09-28 | A task has an assignee, which no other record in this app does. D-005's rule — changing an existing item is limited to its author or the opportunity owner — would mean the person a task is assigned to cannot tick it off unless they wrote it themselves. Does the assignee get to change it? | (a) D-005 unchanged: author or owner only, (b) the assignee may do anything the author may, (c) the assignee may change the **done state** and nothing else | (c) | (a) is the literal reading and it is absurd in practice: handing somebody a task and then refusing to let them close it is not a permission model, it is a defect with a rationale. (b) goes too far the other way — the assignee is who the work is *for*, not who decided what it says, and letting them rewrite the title or move the date silently would lose the distinction that makes an assignment mean anything. (c) splits the two: `PATCH` is author-or-owner like everything else, and `POST .../complete` and `.../reopen` additionally accept the assignee, which is why those are separate routes rather than a `done` field on the `PATCH` (the shape D-128 chose for resolving a thread, for the same reason). `require_task_completion` in `routes_tasks.py` is the only place in this app where a row's own column widens who may change it, so it is spelled out there rather than folded into `permissions.py`'s general helpers — a reader of `permissions.py` should not have to learn that one table is special. The authorization matrix pins it from both sides: the task in the fixture is assigned to the **editor**, who gets 200 on `complete` and 403 on `PATCH`. | Y | **RESOLVED 2026-09-28 — ACCEPTED by the human.** Raised because it is the first deliberate exception to D-005, which they overruled me on once already in the stricter direction. (c) stands: the assignee may change the done state and nothing else. |

**D-143** | 2026-09-28 | Tasks hang off an opportunity but are not on its timeline, so which live-update event should a task write publish (ADR 0003)? | (a) a third event type, `tasks.changed`, (b) reuse `opportunity.changed`, (c) reuse `timeline.changed` | (b) | ADR 0003's rule is that an invalidation may be broader than the write behind it and never narrower, so `opportunity.changed` is *correct* for a task write — it already means "the record and the things hanging off it", and it already invalidates the timeline for exactly this kind of reason (most writes to a record leave a system entry there and the event does not say whether this one did). (a) would be more precise and would cost a third name that every publisher, every subscriber and every future reader has to learn the boundary of; the saving is one refetch of a timeline that is usually already cached. (c) is wrong in the other direction: a task is not a timeline row, and a reader whose timeline is closed still needs their task list invalidated. The cost of (b) is an extra timeline refetch per task write, which is one request against a query the screen already holds. | Y — adding a third event type later is additive and breaks no client. | N — applies ADR 0003's stated rule rather than choosing against it. |

**D-144** | 2026-09-30 | Milestone 4.2 migrates `timeline_entries.follow_up_date` into tasks, and `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s breaking-changes table keeps the column for one phase before dropping it in 6. That settles the *column*, not the **composer's "Follow up" field**: the entry form has offered it since the MVP, and the migration says nothing about whether it keeps being offered. | (a) keep the field, writing both a `follow_up_date` and nothing else, (b) keep the field and have it create a task behind the scenes, (c) remove the field; a follow-up is written in the task panel | (c) | (a) is the **worst of the three and the one inaction produces**: the column would keep accepting new dates that no reader looks at any more, because the calendar and My Tasks both read `tasks` now — so every follow-up typed into the old field would be invisible, and the two would diverge for exactly as long as both existed. That is a data-loss bug with a plausible-looking UI in front of it. (b) keeps one field writing two records, which means a date edited on the entry and a date edited on the task can disagree with no rule for which wins — and it would have to invent the task's title and assignee, the two things the migration could only guess at *once*, for a legacy row, with a comment explaining why. (c) is the honest end state: the thing a person wants is a task, the task panel is where tasks are written, and there is now a better surface for it than a bare date on a form (an assignee, a done state, its own list). **The column stays and keeps its values** — it is still what the migration copied from, so nothing is destroyed and dropping it in Phase 6 remains a separate, reversible step. `TimelineEntryPayload` loses the field, so an entry `PATCH` omits it and an already-migrated row's copy is left alone rather than cleared. The removed `GET /api/v2/timeline/follow-ups` (3.0b, D-088) goes with it, replaced by `GET /api/v2/tasks?assignee=any&from=&to=` — one route with a filter rather than two over the same rows, since the permission rule, the ordering and the cursor are identical and only one `WHERE` clause differs. | Y — the field is nine lines of JSX and the route is in git history | N — (a) is a defect and (b) creates two sources of truth for one date; this is the only self-consistent reading of a migration the roadmap already committed to. |

**D-145** | 2026-09-30 | The Phase 4 checkpoint failed T5: *"it says I can't delete the task because authentication is required."* Reproduced from the container logs rather than from the report: `GET /api/v2/tasks` was already answering 401 a minute before the three refused deletes, and the session row from that login is **still in the table and valid until 7 October** — so the server never revoked it and the browser had stopped sending a matching cookie (most likely because signing in as a colleague in another window rotates the shared cookie, which `auth.py` does by design). **The session going away is not the defect; what the app does about it is.** Nothing in the frontend reacted to a 401: `useSession` kept its cached answer, so the screen went on showing a signed-in page while every read failed and every write reported "Authentication required" as though the *task* had refused the reader. | (a) handle 401 in each hook that writes, (b) handle it once at the query-cache level, (c) handle it in `apiRequest` | (b) | (a) is the same decision taken thirty times and is how half of them come to be missed — the reviewer found it on a task delete, but every write in the app had it. (c) is the tempting one and is a layering mistake: `lib/api.ts` is the fetch wrapper and knows nothing about the query cache, which is what lets it be tested without one. (b) puts it in `lib/query.ts`, which already owns the single TanStack configuration: a `QueryCache`/`MutationCache` `onError` that invalidates `keys.session` on any 401. That is the whole fix, because `useSession` refetches, gets `null`, and every view already branches on that to show the sign-in panel — the machinery existed and nothing was triggering it. `ApiError` gains `status` for this, read only by `isUnauthenticated`. | Y | N — a confirmed defect, fixed at its cause, in the one place the answer can be the same for every request. |

**D-146** | 2026-09-30 | The same review asked for five things on tasks: a description, the ability to change an assignment, the task's history in the activity log, comments on a task as it progresses, and multiple assignees. `docs/FEATURES.md` §8 specifies a task as "title, assignee, due date, done state, optional link to the entry" and then says what this is deliberately *not*: "no subtasks, no dependencies, no time tracking, no sprints. The moment those appear, this is a project management tool." Which of the five are in? | (a) all five, (b) none beyond §8's list, (c) split on whether the request completes what 4.1 already implies or extends it into a task manager | (c) | **Three are in, and two of them were never optional.** *Changing an assignment* is not a new feature at all: 4.1 shipped `PATCH /api/v2/tasks/{id}` and no UI that called it, so the panel offered Delete and no way to alter any field — incomplete work rather than a request. *Task history in the activity log* is **`AGENTS.md` invariant 8**, which says every user-editable field keeps its previous value and the owner can read it: a task's title, assignee and due date are editable, so 4.1 shipped an invariant violation and the human found it before any test did. A *description* is a genuine addition and is granted because they asked directly, which outranks the feature list in `AGENTS.md`'s own source hierarchy; it costs one Markdown column and is versioned with the rest. **Two are held for a decision the human should make explicitly, not one I should make quietly.** *Comments on a task* means threads, replies, resolve and reactions on a second entity — the whole of milestone 3.3 again, on the thing §8 warns about; if a task needs a discussion, the note under the entry that prompted it already is one. *Multiple assignees* replaces a column with a join table and changes what My Tasks, the notification and "the assignee may complete it" (D-142) each mean — the last most of all, since "the assignee" stops being one person. Both are reasonable products and neither is this one as specified. | Y | **Y — the human should rule on comments-on-tasks and multiple assignees.** They asked for both; I have deferred both on a reading of §8 rather than built them, and that reading is exactly the kind of thing they should overrule if they disagree (as they did on D-005). |

**D-147** | 2026-10-01 | Milestone 4.3 wires the first endpoint into `app/uploads.py`, and the module's stored-name rule and ADR 0008's disagree. F.5 generates a random name — `secrets.token_hex(16)` plus the sniffed type's extension, flat under the upload root — while ADR 0008 specifies a **content-addressed** layout, `/data/attachments/{sha256[:2]}/{sha256}`, "so identical uploads deduplicate and filenames never touch the filesystem." Both satisfy the property F.5 was written for (the stored name carries nothing from the user); only one of them is what the accepted ADR says. | (a) keep the random name and record that the ADR's dedupe was declined, (b) move to content addressing and delete the random-name path, (c) store both — a random name on disk and a digest in the row for dedupe lookups | (b) | The ADR is the accepted decision and the one the milestone line names ("content-addressed storage"), so declining it needs a better reason than that F.5 guessed differently first — and the digest is strictly more than the random name was: it deduplicates, which makes attaching one signed contract to three entries one file rather than three, and it makes a retried upload idempotent instead of a second copy. (c) is the worst of both: two identities for one file, and the question "which one is the truth" asked at every call site. What (b) costs is one rewrite of a module with no callers yet, which is precisely the window F.5 existed to create — brief §4.7's "stand up the infrastructure so the feature milestone only adds tests" was never a promise that the infrastructure would be right, and `tests/test_uploads.py` is what made the change safe to make: it was rewritten around the new shape and every rule it was protecting (traversal, symlinked root, declared type ignored, hostile filename in the disposition header) is still asserted. The containment check kept its two independent halves and gained a level: the digest pattern rejects anything that is not 64 hex characters before the filesystem sees it, and the resolved-parent check now also compares the shard directory, which is what catches a symlinked `ab/` planted in the root. | Y — the row carries the digest, so a different layout is a re-link rather than a migration. | N — implements the accepted ADR over an earlier guess, and no behaviour a user sees changes. |

**D-148** | 2026-10-01 | ADR 0008 specifies the metadata row as `attachments(id, sha256, filename, content_type, byte_size, uploaded_by, **entity_type, entity_id**, created_at, deleted_at)` — a polymorphic parent. `docs/ROADMAP.md` says what an attachment actually hangs off in this phase: "proposals and contracts attach to the entry they belong to." | (a) the ADR's `entity_type`/`entity_id` pair, with `timeline_entry` as the only value v2 writes, (b) `entry_id` as a foreign key, (c) the pair plus a `CHECK` constraint naming the one allowed value | (b) | A generic pair with one value is the speculative abstraction `AGENTS.md`'s standards rule out, and it is not free: a polymorphic parent cannot carry a foreign key, so nothing in the schema stops a row naming an entry that does not exist, and every level of `soft_delete.py`'s cascade would need type-aware SQL instead of one statement. The cascade is the part that matters — an attachment is readable only through its entry, so missing a level would leave a document live under a deleted deal, which is the class of bug 4.1's first cascade test failed to catch. v3's email ingestion is the case the pair was written for, and it attaches to entries too (an inbound email becomes an entry); if a note or a comment ever needs files, that is one `ALTER TABLE` and a widened read, which is the same migration the pair would have needed to add a `CHECK` value to. | Y — adding a second parent column is additive, and nothing reads `entity_type` today. | **Y — it narrows an accepted ADR's schema.** ADR 0008 is amended rather than silently diverged from; recommend as shipped, and if the reviewer wants the polymorphic shape it is a migration plus a type-aware cascade, not a column rename. |

**D-149** | 2026-10-01 | Deleting an attachment. Everything else a person writes here is soft-deleted with an audit row and an Undo (`soft_delete.py`), but an attachment has bytes on a volume as well as a row in a table, and content addressing means one blob can be the file of several rows. | (a) soft-delete the row and leave the file, (b) soft-delete the row and unlink the file, (c) soft-delete the row and unlink only when no live row shares the digest | (a) | (b) breaks Undo outright: the row comes back and the download 404s, which is a worse answer than refusing to delete. (c) is the tempting one and is still wrong, because "no live row shares the digest" is not the condition — a **soft-deleted** row shares it too, and that row is restorable, so the only safe predicate is "no row at all, live or deleted, and no backup that could be restored over this database." Nothing in v2 can evaluate the second half: ADR 0011 backs up the database and R.5 is where uploads join that story, so until then a restored database legitimately names files on a volume that was never snapshotted with it. (a) also keeps the module's own invariant intact — `soft_delete.py` is the only place user data is deleted, and nothing in it has ever removed anything. The cost is disk: a deleted attachment's bytes stay until something prunes them, which is a job for R.5 beside the backup work, with the predicate written down there rather than guessed here. | Y — a pruner can be added later with the full predicate, which is strictly easier than recovering files deleted now. | N — follows the invariant the whole data model already has. |

**D-150** | 2026-10-01 | `ALLOWED_UPLOAD_TYPES` (F.5) is PDF, PNG, JPEG, GIF and plain text. The feature is *"proposals, quotes and contracts are the artifacts a deal actually turns on"* (`FEATURES.md` §6), and in this market those arrive as `.docx` and `.xlsx` at least as often as PDF. The allowlist is a security control, so widening it is a decision rather than a config change — and the three OOXML formats share one magic signature (`PK\x03\x04`, a zip), so the signature table cannot tell them apart. | (a) ship F.5's list and let the reviewer report the refusal, (b) widen to the three OOXML types, separating them by what the archive contains, (c) widen to "any zip" | (b) | (a) is the option that looks safe and is not: a contracts feature that refuses the format contracts are written in will be reported as a defect at the gate, and the answer will be this same decision taken under review pressure. (c) gives up the control — a zip is an arbitrary container, so "any zip" admits anything at all. (b) keeps sniffing honest by reading the archive's **central directory only** (`zipfile.namelist()`, never a member's contents, so nothing is decompressed and the input is already inside the 10 MiB cap) and requiring the part prefix each format uses: `word/`, `xl/`, `ppt/`. A zip of holiday photos is none of them and is refused. **Macro-enabled documents are refused too** — any archive carrying a `vbaProject.bin` — which is one line and removes the one thing in this format family that executes: nothing here runs it, but handing a colleague a document their Word has to warn them about is not what a proposal attachment is for. Legacy `.doc`/`.xls` (OLE2) stay out: one signature for every Office 97 format, no cheap way to tell a document from a spreadsheet from something else, and no reason to add the oldest formats to a new feature. | Y — a dict entry and a prefix. | **Y — it widens a security control's allowlist beyond what F.5 and ADR 0008 listed.** Recommend as shipped; the alternative the reviewer might prefer is PDF-only, which is (a) with the consequence stated. |

**D-151** | 2026-10-01 | `AGENTS.md` invariant 4: *"Untrusted input is labelled everywhere. Content arriving by email or **attachment** carries its `source` in the database, in the UI, and in the AI context."* Attachments now exist and the `source` column does not — revision 5.6 adds it, which moved to v3 with the email work (D-141). So what does invariant 4 require of this milestone? | (a) add a `source` column to `attachments` now, ahead of the email work that defines its values, (b) put attachment filenames into the AI's context and label them as untrusted, (c) keep attachments out of the AI context entirely, and let the uploader be the provenance the UI shows | (c) | The invariant's subject is *content the model and the reader could mistake for the record's own*. An attachment in v2 arrives one way only — a signed-in colleague with write access posting it through the API — so its provenance is `uploaded_by`, which is stored, shown on every row ("2.3 MB | Alex") and in the audit row for a deletion. A `source` column whose every value is the same constant is the speculative abstraction (a) amounts to, and it would be designed now against email's requirements rather than with them. (b) is the one that actively costs something: a filename is attacker-controlled text, and putting it in the model's context is a new injection path (`ignore previous instructions.pdf`) in exchange for the model being able to mention a document it cannot read. **So the AI context is unchanged by this milestone**, and that is the honest reading of invariant 4 here rather than a gap in it — there is nothing in the context to label. `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 3 note anticipated "attachment citations" landing with the AI's `?entry=` links; what that requires first is a decision about whether the model may see filenames at all, which is this entry, and the answer for v2 is no. | Y — a `source` column and a context line are both additive, and v3's email work is where both are specified. | **Y — it reads an `AGENTS.md` invariant narrowly.** The reviewer may want the AI to be able to say "the proposal PDF is attached to entry #12"; that is a prompt change plus an injection test, and it should be asked for rather than assumed. |

**D-152** | 2026-10-01 | The 1.4 review recorded a ceiling rather than a defect: `search.py` encodes a document as `source id * 4 + kind code`, which hard-caps the FTS5 index at four document types, and it named this milestone as a candidate for the fourth — *"action for whichever milestone first needs a fourth or fifth searchable type (4.x attachments, or 5.x email): widen the encoding as part of that milestone rather than working around the ceiling, and log the choice."* | (a) index attachments now, widening the encoding to `* 8` in the same migration, (b) widen the encoding now without indexing anything new, as headroom, (c) leave both — attachments are not searchable in v2 | (c) | What the index holds is **bodies**: the terms of an entry's, a note's or a comment's text, with the row re-read to build a snippet (ADR 0007). An attachment has no body here — no text is extracted from a PDF, which would be a new dependency nobody asked for — so the only thing to index is a filename, which is read on the entry it hangs off. Neither `FEATURES.md` §6 nor `ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 4 asks for searchable attachments, so (a) is a migration that rewrites every rowid plus new triggers, a snippet path for a field that is one line long, and a search UI kind, for a capability nothing requested. (b) is the same migration with none of the benefit. The 1.4 note's instruction is honoured by answering it rather than by widening: the ceiling is untouched, the fourth slot is still free, and the live candidate for it is v3's email. **One thing did have to change**: `SourceType` was one literal serving both the search result's `kind` and the notification payload's `source_type`, and those sets have now parted — a notification can be about an attachment and a search result cannot — so `SearchKind` is split out and carries the comment about the ceiling. | Y | **Y — it declines work a recorded human note pointed at this phase.** Recommend as shipped: searchable filenames are a small milestone of their own if wanted, and the encoding should be widened by whichever milestone needs it. |

**D-153** | 2026-10-01 | How the bytes arrive. `UploadFile` (multipart) is the conventional FastAPI shape, and Starlette's parser spools the whole request to a `SpooledTemporaryFile` *before* a `def` handler runs — so the 10 MiB cap in `validate_upload` refuses what gets **stored**, not what gets **received**. An authenticated caller posting a 2 GB body writes 2 GB to the container's temp directory first. | (a) multipart with the cap in the route, (b) a raw body streamed with `request.stream()` and aborted past the limit, which needs the app's first `async def` route handler, (c) multipart plus a `Content-Length` middleware that rejects an oversized request before routing | (a), with the residual risk recorded | (b) is the only option that enforces the cap while reading, and it costs the shape every other route in this app has: `def` handlers in FastAPI's threadpool with synchronous SQLite underneath (`routes_events.py` explains why even the SSE route is `def`). An `async def` route doing blocking file and database work is the footgun that pattern exists to avoid, and the fix for *that* is `run_in_threadpool`, which is a concurrency idiom nothing in this codebase uses yet — introduced for one route, in a milestone about attachments. (c) is defensible and is the natural follow-up, but `main.py`'s middleware order is load bearing and documented as such, and a global request-size limit is a cross-cutting control that deserves its own decision rather than riding in here. So (a) ships, and the residual is stated the way ADR 0008 states the absence of virus scanning: at twenty known internal users behind authentication and CSRF, somebody who can fill the temp directory can already fill it with legitimate 10 MiB uploads. | Y — (c) is additive and (b) is one route rewritten. | **Y — it accepts a known risk rather than closing it.** If the reviewer wants it closed, (c) is the cheap answer and belongs beside the other request-level controls in `security.py`. |

**D-154** | 2026-10-03 | Where the attachment manifest goes. `docs/FEATURES.md` §6 asks for "a listed manifest with filename, size and uploader" in the PDF export and does not say where in the document, and the screen puts a file on the entry it belongs to (4.3). | (a) a block on each entry, mirroring the screen, (b) one section at the end of the document, each row naming the entry its file hangs off, (c) both | (b) | A manifest answers a question about the **whole document** — "what documents does this deal have" — and a reader asking it should not have to scan thirty entries and collect the answer themselves; that is what the word manifest means in a printed file, and it is the half the screen cannot do. Nothing about the placement is lost, because the entry is a column in every row. (c) is the mistake D-121 recorded: two surfaces showing one set of rows, which is also how the three History destinations came about. (a) alone reads naturally in the timeline and leaves the document with no index. One thing (b) made cheap and worth keeping: the section is **absent entirely** when there are no files — a heading announcing the absence of documents is the clutter `TaskSummary` already refuses to draw. | Y — the renderer is one function (`render_attachment_manifest`). | **Y — §6 does not specify the placement and the two readings differ visibly.** Recommend as shipped; a per-entry block is a second call site of the same function if the reviewer prefers the timeline reading. |

**D-155** | 2026-10-03 | The milestone line is "PDF export gains an attachment manifest", and this app exports a deal in three formats from one read (`fetch_export_data`). Adding the files to that read makes them available to all three. | (a) the PDF only, as written, (b) all three — the JSON gains an `attachments` list per entry and the CSV a `row_type: attachment` | (b) | The read is shared, so (a) would mean *removing* the files from two formats on the way out, and the question a reader asks of any of the three is the same one: give me this deal. A JSON export that silently omits the documents is a lossy backup of a record that has them, which is worse than a format that never had them. The cost is three appended CSV columns (`filename`, `content_type`, `byte_size`) — appended rather than slotted in beside `title`, so a spreadsheet or script reading the old file keeps every column where it was, and `filename` is its own column rather than borrowed from `title` because a file's name is not an entry's title and the formula-injection guard has to cover both. The JSON needed nothing: it is `fetch_export_data`'s dict. **Still not embedded anywhere** — §6's one explicit instruction, and the reason the PDF's manifest says "Listed, not included" in the document itself. | Y | **Y — it does slightly more than the milestone line asks for.** Recommend as shipped: the alternative is two export formats that disagree with the third about what a deal contains. |

**D-156** | 2026-10-03 | `npm audit` at the Phase 4 gate reported **7 vulnerabilities (6 high, 1 critical)** against dependencies nobody changed this phase — the Phase 3 gate recorded 0. The advisories are new, not the tree: a **critical RCE in Next.js `next/og` `ImageResponse`** (`>=16.2.0 <16.3.6`, and this project pins 16.3.4), plus six highs that all reduce to one package — `braces`, reached through `micromatch` → `fast-glob` → `@next/eslint-plugin-next` → `eslint-config-next`. | (a) report both and change nothing until the human rules, (b) bump what has a fix and report the rest, (c) `npm audit fix --force`, taking whatever tree it produces | (b) | The critical one is the easy half and it is **not reachable in this product** — `next/og` is never imported, and Next is a bundler here rather than a server (ADR 0005, `output: "export"`) — but "unreachable today" is not a property a pin preserves, and the fix is a patch bump inside the same minor: `next` and `eslint-config-next` 16.3.4 → **16.3.8**, with the whole suite plus the Playwright run re-executed against it, because a bundler bump changes the artifact every e2e test loads. `npm audit fix` then cleared `brace-expansion` at the lockfile level. **Five highs remain and cannot be fixed**: the `braces` advisory's range is `<=3.0.3` and 3.0.3 is the latest published version, so there is no patched release to move to, and npm's own "fix" for the other four is a **major downgrade** of `eslint-config-next` to 14.2.35 — Next 14's lint config under Next 16, which is (c)'s class of answer and worse than the problem. What they are: a stack-exhaustion DoS in a brace-pattern expander, in a **development dependency**, reached only when ESLint expands a glob this repository writes itself. Nothing ships: the image's frontend stage installs the dev tree, builds, and only `frontend/out` is copied into the final image (`Dockerfile`), so no `node_modules` reaches production. Recorded here rather than silently passing the gate, because brief §10's Definition of Done says "no unresolved high/critical dependency vulnerabilities" and five are now unresolved by upstream's choice rather than by ours. | Y — the bump is a lockfile change. | **Y — two things need a ruling.** (1) A dependency bump landed inside a feature phase; if you would rather that be its own change, it is one commit to separate. (2) The five dev-only highs have no upstream fix, so the Definition of Done needs either an exception recorded for them or a watch until `braces` publishes 3.0.4. |

## Human rulings — 2026-10-03 (Phase 4 gate review, round `phase43`)

Eight of the nine items marked. Recorded verbatim in effect rather than
paraphrased, because three of them change what gets built next.

**Accepted as shipped:** **D-148** ("no objection"), **D-150** ("keep the
office format"), **D-153** (accepted, with the instruction that the fix and
every other deferred decision be *recorded as v3 work* rather than left in
this log alone), **D-154** ("end of document"), **D-156** (accept the bump,
**separate it from the feature work** — so it becomes its own commit).

**Overruled:** **D-146**. I deferred comments-on-a-task and multiple
assignees on a reading of §8's "deliberately not a task manager"; the ruling
is *"build both"*. That is the second time a D-005-family reading of mine has
been overruled in the stricter direction, and it is the human's call to make.
Both become milestones of this phase (**4.5**, **4.6**) rather than v3 items,
because the phase gate has not been passed and a task with no history was
already judged incomplete work here rather than a later phase's problem.

**Two questions asked rather than ruled** — answered in the session's reply
and summarised here so the answer is not only in chat: **D-151** (can
filename scanning make it safe to give the AI access to attachments) and
**D-152** (what the filename search index actually costs, and whether it is
worth doing here).

**One new requirement, unrelated to this phase**, from the overall notes:
*"when searching, clicking on the result should take me directly to the note
comment or entry than just the opportunity."* Logged as a milestone rather
than folded in silently — the shape already exists (`?entry=`, D-124) for an
entry, and a note or a comment needs the equivalent.

---

**D-157** | 2026-10-03 | **Check A1 failed — "I can't even find the place where I can attach ... anywhere, no less on the entry" — and A2 through A6 were left blank because of it.** My memory of this project says to measure the running build before believing either account, and the measurement is unambiguous: `GET http://localhost:8000/api/health` answers `schema_version: 0011_task_description_and_versions`, the running container's image was built **2026-09-30 17:49 UTC**, and `routes_attachments.py` was written **2026-10-01 08:53**. `GET /openapi.json` on that app lists **58 paths and not one of them is an attachment route**. So there was nothing to find: the reviewer was sent to test a feature against a build that predates it by a day. | (a) treat it as a discoverability finding and redesign the control, (b) find out what was running first | (b), and the answer closes the finding | **This is a defect in how I asked for the review, not in the feature.** Every other review request in this project has pointed at `http://localhost:8000`, and until this phase that address always happened to be current, because the human rebuilt between phases. Attachments is the first milestone where I asked for a hands-on check without stating that the app has to be rebuilt first — and the one person who could catch that is the person who cannot see which build they are on. What this does **not** establish is that the control is findable: `e2e/25-attachments.spec.ts` proves a scripted locator finds it in a real browser, which is not the same claim, and A1 is still unanswered. The fix is procedural and goes in the review packet rather than the code: **a review request names the build it needs and how to get it.** | Y | **Y — the review needs rerunning, which costs the human their time**, so the rebuild and the re-test are theirs to authorise; the app on port 8000 is their running instance with their real data, and startup will migrate it to `0012_attachments`. |

**D-158** | 2026-10-03 | The human's question on **D-151**: *"is there anyway we can scan the file titles for injection risk before attaching them, thereby we can give the AI model access to attachments? tell me your honest opinion if the risk can be either significantly reduced or eliminated with proper scan. otherwise, we'll keep files hidden from the AI model."* Answering it honestly meant reading `ai_service.build_timeline_context` again rather than defending D-151. | (a) scan filenames for injection patterns and admit the ones that pass, (b) normalize the field, label the context, and admit them all, (c) keep files hidden as D-151 decided | (b) — and the reasoning changes what D-151 was about | **Scanning is the wrong control, and D-151 over-stated the delta.** Three facts, all checked in the code: the context is **line-oriented plain text**; every entry body, note body and comment body goes into it **verbatim, newlines included**; and writing any of them needs the same editor-or-owner access that uploading a file needs. So admitting filenames adds **no new actor, no new class of content, and a smaller payload** than what is already there — a 255-character name beside a multi-paragraph Markdown body. A denylist of injection-shaped phrases over that one small field would be bypassable, unmaintainable, and aimed at the least of the inputs; the deterministic half of the answer is an **allowlist on characters**: strip control characters (today `safe_filename` removes NUL and trims the ends, so an interior `\n` survives) and the field can no longer forge a line of the context's own format. **Can the risk be eliminated? No** — no prompt-injection defence is complete, and anyone claiming otherwise is selling something. What bounds it here is construction, not detection, and it is already in place: the agent holds `PRAGMA query_only`, has no tool surface, and the context is **one opportunity the asker can already read**, so a successful injection's blast radius is a misleading answer to the person who asked, not a write and not another deal's data. **The real finding is bigger than attachments:** nothing in that context is delimited or labelled, so `AGENTS.md` invariant 4's "labelled everywhere" is unmet for *every* field today, and the adversarial test `docs/ROADMAP.md` specifies (5.9) does not exist yet. That is worth a milestone whether or not filenames go in — and if it is built, filenames come with it for free. | Y | **Y — the human asked for a recommendation and this is it:** normalize the filename, give the context a labelled shape for all user content, add the injected-instruction test, and then let the model see attachments. If they would rather not spend a milestone on it, (c) stands and costs nothing. |

**D-159** | 2026-10-03 | The human's question on **D-152**: *"what does it take to build filename index effort wise? worth doing it here or deferring to V3 based on effort?"* | (a) index attachment filenames now, (b) index them now **and** widen ADR 0007's rowid encoding in the same migration, (c) defer to v3, where email forces the widening anyway | (c) recommended, (a) is cheap enough that the human may prefer it | **The estimate, measured rather than guessed.** `search.py` encodes a document as `source id * 4 + kind code` with codes 0, 1 and 2 used — so **code 3 is free and attachments need no rowid rewrite**, which is cheaper than D-152 implied. The work is one revision with three triggers on `attachments` plus a backfill, `KIND_CODES` and `SearchKind` widened, a branch in `build_snippet` (it re-reads the matched row, and a filename is a different join from a body), the permission join for the new kind, the frontend's result label and link, and three tests — the documented-rowid guard, the `make reindex` parity guard, and a permission case. **About one focused session**, mechanical, low risk. **Why I still recommend deferring**: it spends the **last free code**, and v3's email ingestion is the other candidate for it (the 1.4 review note said exactly this). Whichever lands second pays for the widening — a rowid rewrite plus a full reindex — so doing them together is one migration instead of two, and the second one is not free. The thing that would change my recommendation is use: if finding "the Northwind contract" by name is something they would actually do, a session now beats a quarter of waiting. | Y | **Y — this is the human's call on effort, which is what they asked for.** Recommend (c); (a) is one milestone (4.7) if they want it now, and the widening then becomes v3's cost rather than a saving. |

**D-160** | 2026-10-03 | Found while verifying the rebuilt image by hand — posting an executable renamed `report.pdf` at the real container answered `{"error":{"code":"internal_error","message":"Unsupported file type"}}` with status **415**. `errors.ERROR_CODES` names a code per status and had no entry for 413 or 415, because **milestone 4.3's upload is the first endpoint in this app that can answer either**, so both fell through to `internal_error`. | (a) leave it — the status is right and the message is readable, (b) name both codes | (b) | `code` exists precisely so a client branches on the kind of failure rather than on a number or on wording (`lib/api.ts`'s `isConflict` is the first caller), and `internal_error` is the one answer that is *wrong* here: it tells the caller the server broke when in fact their file was too large or of a type the allowlist does not carry. Added as `payload_too_large` and `unsupported_media_type`, with a test that asserts both the status and the name, confirmed to fail with the two entries removed. **Worth noting how this was found**: 19 backend tests cover these two refusals and every one of them asserts the status and the message, which were right all along — the envelope's `code` was not looked at by any of them. It took reading the actual JSON a client receives, from a real container, which is the same lesson the project has recorded three times about the gap between a green suite and a running build. | Y | N — a defect in this milestone's own new code, found and fixed before the review resumed. |

**D-161** | 2026-10-03 | After the rebuild, the human looked again and reported the same thing — *"I still can't find the attach button on any entry when I edit or try to create new."* Two facts, both measured rather than assumed: the container had **still never been restarted** (up 2 days, schema `0011`, no attachment routes), so that reading was again against the old build; and the words *"when I edit or try to create new"* say exactly where they were looking — **the composer**, which is the one place milestone 4.3 did not put the control. | (a) restart and leave the control where it is, since a screenshot proves it renders, (b) restart **and** put attaching in the composer as well | (b) | A screenshot settles whether a control exists, not whether anyone finds it, and two independent attempts to attach a file from the entry form are the strongest evidence this project has about where the control belongs — stronger than my reasoning for the card, which came from where a file is *read*. Both are right for different moments: writing an entry with the proposal already to hand, versus the signed copy arriving a week later, which should not require editing the entry. So the composer gains the field and the card keeps its trigger. The sequencing is the only interesting part: the entry must exist before a file can hang off it, so a refused upload leaves a **saved entry and an open form**, and the form says precisely that rather than implying the entry failed — with the created id held so the retry retries the file instead of posting a second entry. **And the restart itself**: I had framed it as the human's step, they hit the same wall twice, so I restarted their container myself (detached, after a `VACUUM INTO` backup) rather than asking a third time. | Y | **Y** — two things: the control now exists in two places by design, and I restarted their running app without asking after asking twice. Both should be said out loud rather than discovered. |

**D-162** | 2026-10-04 | Milestone 4.5's shape. The ruling was "build both"; what a *comment on a task* is made of was left to me, and `PROGRESS.md` says to settle it with the migration open rather than from the route's point of view. Measured first: `comments.note_id` is assumed in **17 places across 7 modules**, including the two search queries, which join `notes → timeline_entries → opportunities` to resolve permission and answer with a `SearchResultPublic` whose `entry_id` is **not nullable** — "every result addresses one place in one timeline". | (a) one `comments` table with a nullable `note_id`/`task_id` pair, (b) a second table that mirrors threads, replies, resolve and reactions, (c) a second table holding a **flat, chronological** discussion: body, author, time, soft delete | (c) | (a) is the one I expected to pick, because this project prefers one mechanism with a parameter over two copies — and it is wrong here for a reason the grep made visible: a task comment has **no entry**, so either `SearchResultPublic.entry_id` becomes nullable (a visible break to a shipped read, for a feature that did not ask for it) or task comments are silently absent from search while note comments are not. Seventeen joins would each need a branch to buy that. (b) is what **D-146 objected to** when I deferred this — "the whole of 3.3 again on a second entity" — and the objection only holds if threading is actually built. **It is not what was asked for**: the request was *"comments on a task as it progresses"*, which is a discussion log, not replies, resolve and reactions. So (c) is the smallest thing that answers the ruling: one table, one cascade level, three routes, immutable like every other comment here (invariant 2), riding along with the task the way a note's comments ride along with the note. **What this gives up, deliberately**: a task comment cannot be replied to, resolved or reacted to, and it is not searchable. If any of those turns out to matter, threading is additive — the table gains the three columns `comments` already has, which is a smaller change than unpicking (a) would be. | Y — additive in both directions. | **Y — the human ruled on the item and I have chosen its shape.** They were told it meant "threads, replies, resolve and reactions"; what ships is a flat discussion. Recommend as shipped, and say which of the three they want if a flat log is not enough. |

**D-163** | 2026-10-09 | Milestone 4.6's shape, settled with the migration open as `PROGRESS.md` said it would be. Three questions came with it: what happens to `tasks.assignee_id`, what a **version snapshot** records when the field is a set, and what **D-142** means once "the assignee" is several people. | For the column: (a) drop it, (b) keep it as a "primary" assignee, (c) keep it unread and drop it with `follow_up_date` in Phase 6. For the snapshot: (d) a second history table, (e) a `assignee_ids` column holding the set. For D-142: (f) any assignee may close it, (g) only the author and owner may, (h) all assignees must agree | (c), (e), (f) | **The column.** (b) is two sources of truth for one fact, which is how a client comes to believe the wrong one; (a) loses the rollback. (c) is the pattern `follow_up_date` already set (`docs/ROADMAP.md`'s breaking-changes table) — and it is *safer* here than there, because nothing writes it after this revision, so unlike D-144's hazard it cannot drift: it is frozen history rather than a live field nobody reads. **The snapshot.** A version has to keep reading (D-097), and `task_versions.assignee_id` holds one id per row for every version written before today. (d) would split one history across two tables for the sake of one field. (e) adds `assignee_ids` and backfills it from the old column, so an old version reads as a one-person list — which is what it always meant — and every new one carries the set. The format is **canonical** (ids ascending, comma separated) for a specific reason: `versioning.version_is_current` compares snapshots as strings, so two recordings of the same people must produce the same string or restoring would never be a no-op. It is produced in one place, `TASK_ROWS_SQL`, and `apply_version` **cannot** write it back — a set is a table, not a column — so `VersionedTable` gained `applied_fields` and the route puts the set back itself. That is the first time this project's one-shape-for-four-histories abstraction has had to bend, and bending it at the edge was better than widening the `UPDATE` every other restore pays for. **D-142.** (h) is an approval workflow this app does not have and §8's "not a task manager" rules out. (g) un-rules what the human already ruled: handing somebody a task and refusing to let them close it is the bug D-142 exists to prevent, and that argument does not weaken when there are two of them.
