The second question was what to point it at, since the ADR names `/api/health`. | (a) healthcheck against `/api/health` as the ADR says, (b) against a new `/readyz`, with `/healthz` beside it for liveness | (b) | `/api/health` answers **200** with `database_writable: false` by design — it is a report, not a verdict — and a `HEALTHCHECK` reads an exit code and nothing else, so pointed there it would call a container with a read-only mount or a full disk healthy. That is the exact failure ADR 0011 item 4 exists to surface, so following the ADR literally would have produced a healthcheck that cannot fail for the reason it was asked for. The liveness/readiness split is the other half: restarting a process does not empty a disk, so `/healthz` does no I/O at all and `/readyz` takes the same `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` a real write would. ADR 0011 carries an amendment recording both, and `scripts/docker-smoke.sh` now waits for the container to report `healthy` — which is the part whose absence let this sit unimplemented through four phases. **Also in this milestone:** `--timeout-graceful-shutdown 8`, because ADR 0003's SSE streams are held for up to 300 s and uvicorn waits for in-flight requests, so every `docker stop` with a reader attached was ending in a SIGKILL after Docker's 10 s. | Y | N — implementing an ADR item, with the one deviation argued above |

**D-204** | 2026-10-17 | R.4 also asks for "pinned versions". Python dependencies are locked (`uv.lock`), npm dependencies are locked (`package-lock.json`), and the two base images are pinned by **tag** — `node:22-trixie-slim`, `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.13-trixie-slim` — which is a moving target within a minor version. | (a) pin both base images by digest, (b) keep the tags and say why | (b) | A digest is the only true pin, and with no automation to refresh it, a digest pin here would mean the base image stops receiving Debian security updates until somebody remembers — which for a container exposed on a LAN is the worse failure of the two. The tags move only within a minor version (Node 22, Python 3.13), the application's own dependency tree is locked, and a rebuild is how a security patch arrives. This is recorded rather than left implicit so it reads as a choice and not an oversight; the moment there is a dependency bot (R.6's CI is where that would live), digest pinning becomes the better answer and this should be revisited. | Y | N |

**D-205** | 2026-10-17 | Milestone R.5 asks for "documented volume layout; SQLite online backup and restore procedure with a restore test; **uploads included**". The database half has existed since 1.6; the uploads half had not, and `CLAUDE.md` said so out loud — a lost file is "a real state until uploads join the backup story in R.5". How to include them. | (a) a tar archive per snapshot holding the database and the blobs, (b) a blob directory per snapshot, (c) one mirror of every blob beside the snapshots | (c) | Content addressing (ADR 0008) decides this. A blob's name *is* its SHA-256, so a file never changes and two snapshots that both reference it want the same bytes: (a) and (b) would copy the same megabytes once per snapshot for no gain, while a mirror copies each digest exactly once and "already present" is sound rather than hopeful. Two consequences worth stating. **The mirror is never pruned** even as database snapshots are, because an attachment's delete is soft and never removes the file (**D-149**), so any retained snapshot may reference any digest that has ever existed — a safe prune predicate would have to open every kept snapshot and union their digests, which is machinery for a few megabytes. And **a restore puts back only what is missing**: identical digests are identical bytes, so nothing is overwritten. The test loses a live blob on purpose and recovers it, because the failure this prevents is precise — a restored row saying a file is there while the download answers 404. | Y | N |

**D-206** | 2026-10-17 | R.5's "documented volume layout" also forced a statement nobody had made: `backups/` lives on the same volume as the database it snapshots. | (a) leave the layout as it is and say plainly what it protects against, (b) write snapshots somewhere else by default | (a) | ADR 0011's whole shape is one container and one volume, and a second location would be a second mount to configure, back up and get wrong — the appetite the ADR was chosen for. What the layout does protect against is the failure this application can itself cause: a bad write, a mistaken delete, a migration gone wrong. It does **not** protect against losing the disk, and `docs/operations.md` now says so in those words rather than leaving an operator to infer it from a path. Copying snapshots off the host is the operator's job and the documentation stops pretending otherwise. | Y | N |

**D-207** | 2026-10-17 | R.6 is "GitHub Actions running `make check` on push to `main`" — and `make check` includes `npm audit`, which **fails today**: the frontend tree carries a high-severity advisory (GHSA-vfj7-8cjw-p6xm, a stack-exhaustion DoS in `braces`) for which **no fix exists anywhere**. The advisory covers `<= 3.0.3` and 3.0.3 is the newest release `braces` has ever had; npm's suggested "fix" is a downgrade of `eslint-config-next` to 14.x, which is not one. So CI could not be both green and honest without a decision. | (a) `npm audit --audit-level=critical`, (b) accept a red gate, (c) an allowlist keyed by advisory id, with a reason per entry | (c), as `scripts/audit-gate.mjs` | (a) hides every future high as well, which is trading a known-safe advisory for an unknown one. (b) teaches everybody to ignore the gate, which is worse than not having it. (c) is the shape this project already uses for exactly this problem — `DEFERRED_AXE_RULES` in `e2e/helpers.ts` — and it is a ratchet rather than a wall: **a high or critical advisory with no entry fails**, and **an allowlisted advisory that no longer appears also fails**, so an entry cannot outlive the problem it documents. Both failure modes were exercised before the gate was trusted. The one entry says why it is safe *in this deployment* rather than that it is transitive: it is reachable only through `eslint-config-next → @next/eslint-plugin-next → fast-glob → micromatch → braces`, at lint time, over this repository's own configuration — and nothing in the shipped artifact can reach it, because `output: "export"` emits static files and the runtime image copies `frontend/out` with no `node_modules` at all. `npm audit fix` was run first and cleared two genuinely fixable advisories (`sharp`, `source-map-js`); typecheck, lint, the unit suite and the production build were all re-run against the new lockfile. | Y | N |

**D-208** | 2026-10-17 | R.7 asks for "seed data exercising every shipped feature and permission relationship, **at the confirmed 800k-row scale**". Those two halves pull against each other: the demo seed is five named deals a person reads by hand, and the confirmed scale is 400 deals and 800,000 rows nobody reads. | (a) one seed at scale, (b) two separate seeds, (c) the demo seed, with the scale corpus layered on top of it behind a flag | (c), as `make seed-scale` | (a) buries the tour — "Casey is a viewer on Northwind" is useless advice when Northwind is one row in four hundred and the printed walkthrough no longer matches what is on screen. (b) means two corpora drifting apart, and the one that matters for performance would be the one nobody keeps honest. (c) keeps a single builder: `tools/scale.py` is the corpus `tests/test_performance.py` measures the budgets against, so **what a person loads is what was measured**, and the five named deals stay findable among the generated ones. The cost is that the builder had to become composable — every generated id is now relative to `MAX(id)` rather than starting at 1 — which is a small change and the reason a generated note no longer hangs itself off one of the five deals somebody is about to read. It also moved out of `tests/` into `tools/`, because a module `make seed` depends on is not test infrastructure. | Y | N |

**D-209** | 2026-10-18 | The human read the R.1b shakedown, passed all three checks, and ruled against the shape it had just confirmed: *"It is better not to mention anything instead of saying it is kept private so the other person doesn't know anything about what's kept away from them."* R.1b had deliberately chosen the **sealed card** over the invisible row, and the reason was the entry number: an entry that vanishes for one reader either leaves a gap in their numbering or renumbers their timeline against everybody else's, and the AI cites those numbers. | (a) keep the sealed card, (b) hide the row and keep one numbering for the deal, (c) hide the row and count the numbering per reader | (c) | (a) is what was built and what the ruling overturns. (b) is the cheaper half of the change and fails at the thing the change is for: deletions renumber rather than leave gaps here, so a reader who sees #41, #42, #44 is a reader who has been told exactly what the card used to tell them. (c) pays the cost D-139 already named out loud — "this number is a reading aid and never an identity", renumbered by any backdated insert — and in exchange no reader can tell that anything is missing. Two people may now call one entry by two numbers; a single reader's card and the AI's citation still come from one expression, and the AI's context has been assembled per reader since R.1b. `permissions.visible_entries_clause` moved **into** `timeline_utils.ENTRY_SEQUENCE_SQL`, which is precisely what `test_a_sealed_entry_keeps_its_number_for_everyone` existed to prevent — so that test is now its inverse, `test_a_sealed_entry_is_numbered_per_reader`. | Y | N |

**D-210** | 2026-10-18 | Reviewing Phase R before the gate found the activity log still announcing a sealed entry: an owner read *"Colleague edited an entry, changing the title"* with its id, because `activity.py`'s joins assert `deleted_at IS NULL` at every level and nothing about visibility. Under R.1b that was consistent — the owner already met a sealed card on the timeline — so it was a loose end rather than a defect. D-209 makes it a contradiction. | (a) leave it, (b) drop every entry and note version row from an owner's feed, (c) add the reader's predicate to the two joins that pass through `timeline_entries` | (c) | (a) would mean the timeline hides a card and the feed beside it names the edit. (b) throws away the owner's own history to protect a colleague's, including the owner's own sealed entries. (c) is the predicate this app already has, applied where it was missing: `fetch_activity_page` takes the reader's id — which is **not** a second permission check, since `role` still decides who reads the version half at all, but the sealed rule, which is one predicate rather than a role. The content was never reachable (the version read and the restore both resolve through `fetch_entry_context` and 404); what leaked was existence, author, time and field names. Guarded by a test confirmed to fail against the unfixed code. | Y | N |

**D-211** | 2026-10-18 | The same review found the 14-day rule written a **fourth** time. R.3 replaced the three-way activity union with a trigger-maintained column (D-201) and `app/stalled.py` became the single definition — but `automations._no_activity_candidates`, the evaluator for §11's own headline example (*"if nothing has happened in two weeks, remind me"*), still computed the union live over every accessible deal on every scheduler tick. Measured on the confirmed-scale corpus: **112 ms**, against **under 1 ms** for the column, same answer. | (a) leave it — no user waits on a scheduler tick, (b) read `opportunities.last_activity_at` | (b) | (a) is defensible on latency alone and wrong on the thing that matters: a definition of "nothing has happened" that exists in two places with no test holding them together is a definition that will drift, and this copy is the one furthest from the tests that guard the column (`tests/test_stalled.py`). The rule's own `days` parameter works against the column exactly as it did against the union, and a deal nothing has ever happened on is `NULL` and not a candidate — the same answer the union gave when its `MAX` came back empty. | Y | N |

**D-212** | 2026-10-18 | `tools/scale.py` read `first_user = MAX(id)` and gave every generated deal to that user. On an empty database that is user 1 and the docstring's claim — *"the first user owns every generated opportunity — user 1 on an empty database, Alex after a demo seed"* — is accidentally true, which is why `tests/test_performance.py` never noticed. After `make seed` it is Dana, the one demo user the printed tour describes as having access to nothing. A person following R.7's own instructions built 805,000 rows, signed in as Alex, and could not find one of them. | (a) leave it and fix the docstring, (b) own the corpus from the lowest existing user id | (b), plus a line in the printed tour naming the owner | (a) makes the document true and leaves the feature useless: the point of D-208's layering is that the scale lands *on* the demo data a person is about to read. (b) is one query and restores the docstring's own claim; on an empty database it still resolves to the first generated rep, so the performance corpus is unchanged. The printed tour gains a line because it says "Alex owns four opportunities" and `--scale` has just given them four hundred more — and `tests/test_seed.py` is what keeps that printed tour honest. | Y | N |

**D-213** | 2026-10-18 | The same review ran the end-to-end suite against the committed phase and found it **red**: `e2e/29-private-entries.spec.ts` waits for the empty-state trigger *"Keep a note on this deal, just for you"* on Alex's notebook on Northwind, and R.7's seed — written four milestones later — now writes Alex a notebook on Northwind. The spec timed out at 30s, deterministically. `FINAL-REPORT.md`'s "112 passed, 1 skipped" did not hold. | (a) stop seeding the notebook, (b) move the spec to a deal with no seeded notebook, (c) have the spec open the editor by whichever trigger is showing | (c) | (a) removes demo content R.7 added on purpose — the notebook is one of the two shapes of R.1b and the tour names it. (b) works and ties the spec to which deal happens to be unseeded today, which is the same fragility one layer along. (c) says what the test is actually about: who may read a notebook, not which state it opens in. The lesson is the one worth keeping: **a seed and an end-to-end suite are two halves of one fixture**, and a milestone that adds demo data has to re-run the suite that reads it. The second failure in that run, `22-live-updates.spec.ts:60`, passed on a rerun of the same commit and is logged as flaky rather than fixed. | Y | N |

**D-214** | 2026-10-20 | The reviewer failed check pr-4 at the Phase R gate: *"after the back up and restore ... the deleted opportunity never restored."* Reproduced, and it is not operator error: `restore_backup` resolves its target from `settings.database_url`, which on a host shell falls back to `sqlite:///./app.db` — from `backend/`, a scratch file no container has ever read, while the deployment's database is bind-mounted at `/data`. `make restore` copied a real snapshot over the wrong file, printed "Restored ... to app.db", and exited 0. `make backup` had the same flaw in the other direction, and `BACKUP=data/backups/...` was additionally resolved relative to `backend/` because the recipe runs there. | (a) document `DATABASE_URL=... make restore`, (b) have the `Makefile` name the deployment's database for both targets, (c) run the restore inside a one-off container | (b), plus a refusal in `restore_backup` and absolute paths in its output | (a) leaves the trap armed for whoever reads the command and not the paragraph. (c) is the most honest shape for a containerized deployment and a worse answer here, because `make restore` is also how a developer restores a local database and the one-off container needs the image to exist — which it does not, if what you are recovering from is a failed build. (b) makes the documented sequence work as written: `DATABASE_URL` defaults to `./data/app.db` and `BACKUP` resolves from the repository root. The guard is the half that generalises: a snapshot belongs to the database whose `backups/` directory it sits in, because `backup_dir()` is derived from the same setting, so **a snapshot from anywhere else means the two have been pointed at different databases** — refused, with both absolute paths named and `FOREIGN=1` to override for a genuine cross-machine restore. **Why no test caught it**: every test in `tests/test_backup.py` points `database_url` at its own `tmp_path`, so the snapshot and the live database are the same database by construction. The host-versus-container split is invisible to that design, which is also why ADR 0011's "actually exercised" was satisfied and still insufficient. | Y | N |

**D-215** | 2026-10-20 | Defining a **custom field** and writing an **automation rule** were open to every authenticated caller (**D-171**, **D-173**), because `docs/FEATURES.md` says "admin-defined" and this application's three roles are per opportunity with no role table. Both were recorded as the widest write surfaces in the app and left for the human. Their ruling at the Phase R gate: *"both have to be done by the owner."* | (a) an administrator named in configuration, (b) anyone who owns at least one opportunity, (c) leave creation open and narrow what a rule may touch | (a), as `ADMIN_EMAILS` | "The owner" cannot be an opportunity's owner here: neither object has an opportunity in sight, and a field lands on every record of its type. (b) barely narrows anything on a deployment where nearly everyone owns a deal, and it answers a different question. (c) addresses reach rather than permission, which is not what was asked. (a) is `FEATURES.md`'s own word, and it is **configuration rather than a column** — a deliberate departure from the flag-on-users shape the ruling named, because the two are indistinguishable to a reader (neither offers an in-app grant, which nobody asked for) while the column adds a migration, a second source of truth and state to repair when the list changes. `permissions.is_admin` is the whole implementation; a column stays an additive migration if granting it in the app is ever wanted, with this list as its seed. Three consequences worth keeping: **403 and not 404**, because both lists are readable and the message names the setting to change; **reading stays open**, since the column picker, the filter bar and both forms are built from the definitions; and **editing, archiving and running a rule remain the creator's alone**, so an admin does not inherit another admin's rules. Empty means nobody, which is the safe default for a fresh install. | Y | N |

**D-216** | 2026-10-21 | The reviewer's second R.1b tweak: *"Review the button designs again and make them all the same size and a bit smaller than the current delete button."* Measured on the shipped build before changing anything, because I had already described this once as a single sidebar override and that was wrong: the three weights rendered at **five** sizes on the deal page alone — `6px 14px` at 15px in the main column, `4px 8px` at 13px under `.sidebar-column`, `5px 8px` via `.compact` on a comment thread, `7px 10px` via `.compact-button` on the saved views, and `space-1/space-3` in the filter bar — all at one 34px height, so what differed was the type and the padding, not the box. | (a) normalise the variants to each other, (b) one size on the shared rule and delete all five variants, (c) make it a density-style root attribute | (b), as `--button-height: 30px` | (a) is what produced five variants in the first place — each was a local answer to "this one looks too big here". (c) is the shape D-075 uses for row height and it is the wrong tool: density is a *preference* with two values, and this is one size with none. (b) puts font, padding and height in the one rule that already owned the weights, and the variant classes are removed from the markup rather than left as no-ops that size nothing. 30px is smaller than the Delete they were looking at and comfortably above WCAG 2.5.8's 24px minimum, which `e2e/07-accessibility.spec.ts` checks and `e2e/23-responsive.spec.ts` measures at eight widths. **Two deliberate exceptions**: the application bar keeps `--control-height`, because its ghost buttons sit in a row with the search field and the theme select and a short control there reads as misaligned rather than as smaller; and inputs and selects keep it too, since a text field is sized by what is typed into it. Re-measured after: every weight on the deal page is 30px, 13px, `4px 12px`. | Y | N |

**D-217** | 2026-10-21 | The reviewer's first R.1b tweak: *"Follow up tick should continue to create Task by default as the follow up action. However, it shouldn't be mandatory to create a task so a follow up could be put just in the notes or be created as a task."* There was no follow-up tick: milestone 4.2 removed the field (**D-144**) and nothing in the UI had written `timeline_entries.follow_up_date` since, so "continue to" described something that did not exist. | (a) a date on the composer with a tick that creates a task, ticked by default, (b) an "Add a follow-up task" control on the posted card only, (c) both | (a) | D-144's reasoning for removing the field was sound and is the thing to preserve: a date that writes only the entry's column diverges from the task list, which is what My Tasks, the task panel and the calendar actually read. What makes the field safe is that **the tick is on by default and what it writes is a task** — so the common path produces the thing the app reads, and unticking is the reviewer's "just in the notes": the date stays on the entry, the card prints it, and the hint says in as many words that it will not appear in Tasks or on the calendar. The tick only renders once a date is set, because a checkbox about an empty field is a question nobody asked. The task is `Follow up: <title>`, revision `0010_follow_ups_to_tasks`'s own wording, assigned to the entry's author and linked back by `entry_id` — so the two kinds of follow-up task read as one kind. Three requests in order (entry, file, task) with `createdId`/`taskMade` guarding a retry, the shape 4.3a established. The backend needed no change: `TimelineEntryCreate` has validated this field all along. (b) is the weaker half of the same idea and is still open as a later addition; the composer is where a follow-up is *decided*. | Y | N |

**D-218** | 2026-10-21 | Walking T1's steps, the reviewer named a snapshot in `data/backups/` that was in `data-demo/backups/`, with `DATABASE_URL` on a wrapped second line that ran as its own command and set nothing. Two ordinary slips. Nothing was harmed -- the restore refused before opening anything -- but the answer was a `FileNotFoundError` **traceback**, which is the wrong register for an operator mistyping a path and is inconsistent with the refusal D-214 had just added two lines below it. | (a) leave it, (b) a clean message naming where it looked and where this database's snapshots are, plus what is in there | (b), as `BackupNotFound` | The two failures an operator meets at a restore are both the same question -- *which database?* -- and D-214 answered one of them with instructions while the other still printed a stack. Listing the newest snapshots in the directory it did look in is what turns "no such file" into the next command, and naming both directories is what surfaces the real cause when they disagree: `DATABASE_URL` pointing somewhere else. An empty directory says `make backup` instead, which is a different problem and a different instruction. The page's own steps were the other half of the fix: one `export DEMO=...` up front and `BACKUP=$(ls -t ...)` afterwards, so there is no long line to break in half and no timestamp to type. **The lesson is about instructions, not code**: a command long enough to wrap is a command that will be pasted in two pieces, and the half that goes missing is the one at the end. | Y | N |

**D-219** | 2026-10-21 | R.1c's one button size made the deal's record band shorter, which landed `16-edit-in-place.spec.ts`'s axe audit on a knife edge: a control at the top of the timeline column sat *half* behind the sticky band, which axe reports as `target-size` (`partiallyObscured`). Fixing that meant checking `--sticky-deal`, R.2's literal for how much sticky chrome sits above the content — and **measuring it found the literal was wrong at every width**. Stuck: 196px at 1440 and 1280, 230px at 1100, **256px at 1024**, 238px at 960; and below 900 the band is `position: static`, so the only sticky chrome is the 56px bar. The token said 190 everywhere. | (a) one larger literal, (b) values per width, measured, with the inequality asserted at eleven widths, (c) make the filter strip sticky so nothing is ever half-covered | (b), plus auditing from a defined scroll position | The 1024 case is the one that matters: `scroll-padding-top` 66px short of the chrome means a control reached by `Shift+Tab` can sit two thirds of a band under it — the 2.4.11 failure R.2 existed to fix, still present at the width below the one it measured, and invisible to its own test because that test asserts *entirely* hidden and only ever runs at 1280. (a) over-pads a short viewport by a quarter of its height. (c) is robust and costs 34px of permanent chrome on the screen the reviewer twice asked to make smaller. (b) is three values that each clear their measurement with room — over-padding is harmless, under-padding is the defect — and the guard measures **while the bands are stuck**, which the first version of it did not: at rest it read the band's position in the document flow and reported 543px of "chrome" at 900px wide. The widths are the responsive suite's eight plus 1200, 1100 and 960, because the worst case is inside that range rather than at either end. The axe audit in 16 now runs from the top of the page, with the reasoning in the test: what 2.5.8 is about is audited at rest on every screen by 07, and what sticky chrome threatens is 2.4.11, which is measured here. | Y | N — a 2.4.11 defect found by measurement, fixed from measurement. |
